This is a digital reproduction of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world's books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain <note>A legal status where a work is no longer protected by intellectual property laws and is free for anyone to use.</note>. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that's often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other marginalia <note>Handwritten notes or marks made in the margins of a book by previous readers or librarians.</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book's long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying <term>automated querying: the use of software scripts or "bots" to automatically download or search data</term>.

We also ask that you:

+ *Make non-commercial use of the files* We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *Refrain from automated querying* Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google's system: If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <term>OCR: the electronic conversion of images of typed, handwritten or printed text into machine-encoded text</term> or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ *Maintain attribution* The Google "watermark" you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ *Keep it legal* Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that your use is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can't offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book's appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

## About Google Book Search

Google's mission is to organize the world's information and make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world's books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. --- Page 8 ---
# PREFACE.

---

The translation now placed before the public was prepared as long ago as 1895; and the various changes and misfortunes through which the manuscript has passed have made it absolutely impossible for it to undergo any revision. So what is presented here is the work of a mere beginner in the mysteries of translating Sanskrit philosophy; and the translator would therefore, at the very outset, offer his apologies to the reader for providing what he himself considers a rather poor offering. It may however be added, as a justifying circumstance, that the printing of the old "copy" was allowed to proceed in view of a promise given to the late Mr. Tukaram Tatya—an obligation from which the translator could not free himself.

The chief fault of the present translation lies in the fact that it is not readable on its own. This is due to the extremely obscure nature of the original text, a quality which is inseparable from all works dealing with subjects where the full truth cannot be shared with the general public. <note>The author implies that Yoga was traditionally an esoteric or "secret" science passed from teacher to student.</note> Imperfect as it is, it is hoped that it may help, to a slight extent, future workers in this same field.

By way of making up for this deficiency in the translation, it is proposed to give here a readable summary of the teachings of the <term>Yogasutras</term> <note>The foundational collection of short aphorisms on Yoga philosophy attributed to the sage Patanjali.</note>.

"<term>Yoga</term>" <note>Union or discipline</note> has been defined as the "<term>nirodha</term>" <note>restraint or cessation</note> of the "<term>vritti</term>" <note>fluctuations or thought-waves</note> of the "<term>chitta</term>" <note>mind-stuff or consciousness</note> brought about by "Practice" and "Freedom from Attachment"; and the only explanation that will make this intelligible to the ordinary reader is that *yoga* consists in the controlling of the mind;—though it must be admitted that the definition in this form becomes philosophically inaccurate;

<summary>The translator provides a humble preface explaining the delayed publication of this 1895 manuscript and introduces the fundamental definition of Yoga as the restraint of mental fluctuations.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga, Patanjali, Yogasutras, Tukaram Tatya, Chitta, Nirodha, Vritti, Sanskrit translation, mental control</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface, transitioning from the general introduction of the work to the specific definitions of mental states and the types of meditation as described in the Yoga Sutras.</meta>

<page-num>ii</page-num>
# PREFACE.

as the ‘<term>chitta</term> <note>original: "chitta"; refers to the "mind-stuff" or the collective functions of the psyche including intellect, ego, and memory</note>’ of the original is something different from ‘mind’, and ‘<term>nirodha</term> <note>original: "nirodha"; the cessation, suppression, or restriction of mental fluctuations</note>’, by which is meant ‘withdrawal’ or ‘inhibition’ different from ‘control.’ But all the accurate renderings of the definition that have been attempted in English have only helped to make the original less intelligible. This ‘control of the mind’ becomes *<term>yoga</term> <note>original: "yoga"; here used in its technical sense as the state of union or disciplined stillness</note>* only when the ‘mind’ is so far ‘controlled’ that it abides in its own pure form of consciousness.

The functions or operations of the *<term>Chitta</term>* (Mind) are fivefold—Right Cognition, Wrong Cognition, Supposition, Sleep, and Remembrance. Right Cognition is of three minds—Perceptional, Inferential and Verbal. Wrong Cognition is mistaken conviction brought about by some defect either in the cognitive agency or in the cognised object. Supposition (or Imposition) is distinguished from Wrong Cognition by the fact that while the latter is rectified by subsequent Right Cognition, the former is such that it persists all through worldly existence, just as tenaciously as any ordinary cognition; to this class belong all those popular errors of regarding Intelligence as a *quality* of the Soul, and so on. Remembrance is cognition brought about by impressions left by previous cognitions. By ‘Sleep’ is meant the cognition of pleasure that we have during sound sleep.

<term>Yoga</term> or Meditation is of two kinds—*unconscious* or *abstract* and *conscious* or *concrete*. In Conscious Meditation, also called *<term>Salambana</term> <note>original: "salambana"; meditation "with support" or a focal point</note>* or *<term>Sabija</term> <note>original: "sabija"; "with seed," meaning meditation that still contains a mental object</note>*, the object meditated upon is distinctly or directly apprehended; that is to say, in this meditation the inhibition of the mind enables the agent to directly apprehend the object on which he is meditating—for instance, some form of personal divinity. In Unconscious Meditation, also called *<term>Niralambana</term> <note>original: "niralambana"; "without support," a state of objectless meditation</note>* or *<term>Nirbija</term> <note>original: "nirbija"; "seedless," the highest state where even the seed of thought is dissolved</note>*, on the other hand, there is a complete inhibition of all the functions of the mind, wherein the agent loses all consciousness of things

<summary>This page defines the five functions of the mind (Chitta) and distinguishes between conscious meditation (Sabija) and unconscious meditation (Nirbija).</summary>
<keywords>Chitta, Nirodha, Yoga, Cognition, Meditation, Sabija, Nirbija, Indian Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion of the states of consciousness and the mechanics of spiritual liberation, specifically addressing the resolution of past actions.</meta>

<page-num>iii</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

outside himself; he is literally self-conscious, not indeed conscious of his self as apart from other *selves*, but of *the Self and that alone as One, Absolute, Eternal, Unchanging.* The effect of the former is visible or perceptible, consisting of the experiencing of desirable pleasures, and finally actually perceiving the Divinity; this last perception puts a stop to all kinds of pain, and thereby gradually leads to final Release <note>The Sanskrit term is likely 'Moksha,' referring to the liberation from the cycle of rebirth.</note>. Unconscious Meditation also leads to final release; but immediately and directly; and it does not stand in need of any intervening processes.

This raises an interesting question: when the man has reached the stage of conscious meditation, what becomes of his past <term>Karma</term> <note>The law of cause and effect where one's actions determine their future state.</note>—If he obtains final Release all at once, is all his <term>Karma</term> wiped off at one stroke? If not, how can he obtain perfect Release? The answer to this lies in the fact that <term>Karma</term> is divided into three classes—(1) the <term>Prârabdha</term> or *commenced* <note>original: "Prârabdha"</note>, those whose machinery has been set in motion towards their fruition in the present life; (2) the <term>Sanchita</term> or *Accumulated* <note>original: "Sanchita"</note>—those that are lying latent, like seeds stocked up in the granary, for fruition in future lives; and (3) <term>Kriyāmāṇa</term> or *Being done* <note>original: "Kriyāmāṇa"</note>,—those that are being done in the present life. Now there is nothing that can stop the machinery that has been set going; the tree that has sprouted must grow, to some extent at least,—the effects of the <term>Prârabdha karma</term> must be experienced. With regard to the <term>sanchita</term> however the case is different: the seeds may be deprived of their germinating power under the influence of extreme heat or cold; in the same manner the accumulated karma can be rendered ineffective by the force of wisdom. Lastly over the <term>kriyāmāṇa</term> the agent has full control. Hence when the man reaches the stage of conscious Meditation, he accelerates the fruition of his ‘<term>Prarabdha</term>’ Karma, renders ineffective the ‘<term>sanchita</term>,’ and being entirely free from personal desires, does not acquire any

<summary>This page explains how different types of Karma (commenced, accumulated, and current) are resolved through meditation and wisdom to achieve spiritual liberation. It distinguishes between the inevitable fruition of past actions already in motion and the neutralization of latent past actions through spiritual insight.</summary>
<keywords>Karma, Prarabdha, Sanchita, Kriyāmana, Meditation, Spiritual Release, Self-consciousness, Divinity</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion regarding the mechanics of Karma and the specific stages of meditative practice required for spiritual liberation.</meta>

<page-num>iv</page-num>
# PREFACE.

*Righteousness* <note>original: "dharma"</note> or *unrighteousness* <note>original: "adharma"</note>, and thus has no <term>accumulating karma</term> <note>original: "kriyamâna"; refers to actions performed in the present life that will bear fruit in the future</note>; and the *Destined Karma* <note>original: "Prârabdha Karma"; the portion of past karma that has already begun to bear fruit in the current life</note> being only limited, as soon as that has become exhausted, Release is attained. This is what happens in <term>Conscious Meditation</term> <note>also known as Savikalpa Samadhi, where the distinction between the meditator and the object remains</note>. <term>Unconscious Meditation</term> <note>also known as Nirvikalpa Samadhi, a state of pure consciousness without objects</note>, on the other hand, is so powerful in its action that it tends to exhaust the *Destined Karma* also, not indeed, by wiping it off, but by making it ineffective by depriving it of such auxiliaries and aids during present life without which it cannot bring about its effects. In fact this is what is meant by <term>Karma</term> being destroyed or burnt. As a matter of fact, in bringing about its results the *Destined Karma* stands in need of the aid of such auxiliaries as Ignorance, Egotism, Attachment and Longing after life, on the part of the agent; hence when the agent has by practice of meditation become free from these 'Obstacles' or 'Troubles',—he makes his *Destined Karma* entirely ineffective; and so attains <term>Final Release</term> immediately. In Conscious Meditation, there is some personal motive present, however pure, it may be; and so long as this is so, Egotism is there; and hence *Destined Karma* remaining effective, the Release is obtained, it is true,—but only after *Destined Karma* has become exhausted by actual experiencing of its results.

Of Conscious Meditation there are four kinds—which have been regarded as the four stages in the advancement towards meditation. All the four are not necessary for all men. If the aspirant has succeeded in reaching the higher stage he need not revert to the lower; and this for the simple reason that 'the ends of the latter will have been served by the former' (<term>Commentary on Yoga</term> <note>original: "Yoga-Bhashya"; the classic commentary on Patanjali's Yoga Sutras</note>). Then again, all these four stages are to be practised with reference to one and the same 'object of meditation'; as if one wavers from one to the other object the process will lose much of its force. With regard to the same object however the aspirant must proceed from the grosser or more easily perceptible aspects of it to the subtler

<summary>This page explains how different forms of meditation interact with Karma to achieve spiritual liberation, specifically distinguishing between Conscious and Unconscious Meditation. It also outlines the progressive stages of meditation, moving from tangible to subtle objects of focus.</summary>
<keywords>Karma, Prarabdha, Conscious Meditation, Unconscious Meditation, Yoga-Bhashya, spiritual release, Egotism, dharma, adharma</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the stages of meditation and the metaphysical structure of the universe as defined in the Yoga and Sankhya systems.</meta>

<page-num>v</page-num>
-># PREFACE.<-

or imperceptible aspects; and thus by the time he has passed through the four stages, the object is present before him in all its aspects.

These four kinds are—(1) the ‘Argumentative’ <note>original: *Savitarka*; a stage of meditation where the mind still reasons about the physical properties of the object</note>, whereby the aspirant is enabled to apprehend all the past, present and future aspects of the ordinary perceptible kind, of the object of his devotion—such for instance as the elements and the sense-organs; (2) the ‘Deliberative’ <note>original: *Savichara*; a subtler stage where the mind reflects on the abstract essence of the object</note>—whereby he is enabled to apprehend the ordinarily imperceptible aspects of that object; as for instance, Nature, Intelligence, Self-consciousness and the Rudimentary Elements. (3) the ‘Joyous’ <note>original: *Sananda*; a state of bliss arising from the clarity of meditation</note>—whereby contemplating the object of devotion the aspirant feels a peculiar blissful sensation; and (4) the ‘Self-conscious’ <note>original: *Sasmita*; the highest stage of conscious meditation where one focuses on the pure sense of "I am"</note>—whereby the aspirant comes to look upon himself as one with the object of devotion.

A distinction is made between what is called the *human self*,—which forms the twenty-fifth ‘principle’ <term>principle: or *Tattva*, the fundamental building blocks of reality in Indian philosophy</term> in the constitution of the Universe, whereof Nature, Intelligence, Self-consciousness, the eleven organs, the five rudimentary elements and the five gross elements are the other twenty-four ‘principles’—and the *Supreme Self*, on the ground that the latter is far more subtle than the former; as the human self is directly perceived in the aforesaid fourth stage of Conscious Meditation, while that of the Supreme Self we can have no direct knowledge; the only conception that we can have of it is what we may form out of our ideas of such qualities (if ‘qualities’ they can be called) as Absolute Unchangeability, Indivisibility and the like. The contemplation of the *self* (human) is possible during the aforesaid egoistic meditation; this is what is spoken of in Sankhya and Yoga works as ‘the discernment of the distinction between the self and the other principles’ <note>original: *Sattvapurushânyatâkhyâti*; the realization that the pure consciousness of the self is different from the material mind-stuff</note>. The meditation of the supreme *Self* however is spoken of only in aphorism <note>original: *sutra*</note> I. 23.

<summary>This page outlines the four stages of conscious meditation and distinguishes between the individual human self and the Supreme Self within the framework of Sankhya and Yoga philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>meditation stages, Argumentative meditation, Deliberative meditation, Joyous meditation, Self-conscious meditation, human self, Supreme Self, Sankhya philosophy, Yoga Sutras, twenty-five principles</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface's exploration of Yoga philosophy, specifically focusing on the theological role of the Supreme Self and the classification of practitioners.</meta>

<page-num>vi</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

It is not very easy to find what part this ‘supreme self,’ or ‘God’ plays in the cosmogony <note>The study of the origin and development of the universe.</note> of <term>Yoga</term> <note>A school of Hindu philosophy focusing on meditation and liberation.</note>. He is nowhere spoken of as the ‘creator’; nor even as the consciousness permeating through all existence. He is spoken of only as an object of devotion, devotion to whom leads to highest results. In this respect the ‘god’ of the *<term>Yogin</term>* <note>A practitioner of Yoga.</note> appears to hold the same position as the ‘*<term>devatâ</term>*’ <note>A deity or divine being.</note> of the *<term>Mimamsaka</term>* <note>A follower of the Mimamsa school, which emphasizes ritual and the interpretation of the Vedas.</note>, who posits the ‘*<term>devatâ</term>*’ only as one to whom the prescribed sacrifices can be offered; He has no other function at all. Later writers on <term>Yoga</term> were conscious of this; hence when dealing with the sutra <note>A concise aphorism or rule in Sanskrit literature.</note> defining God simply as ‘that self which is ever untouched by the five kinds of troubles, Ignorance and the rest, as also by virtue, vice and their modifications’ they proceeded to supplement this by additional accounts of the Godhead obtainable from other sources, chiefly *<term>Vedântic</term>* <note>Relating to Vedanta, a philosophy based on the Upanishads focusing on the ultimate reality.</note>. For instance, *<term>Vijnana Bhiksha</term>* <note>A 16th-century Indian philosopher who integrated Yoga and Vedanta schools.</note> gives the following description:—‘His powers and omniscience are equalled or excelled by none; He is the Lord or Spiritual Chief and Father of all the gods, *<term>Brahma</term>*, *<term>Vishnu</term>* and *<term>Rudra</term>* <note>The three primary aspects of the divine in Hinduism: the Creator, the Preserver, and the Destroyer.</note>; is the imparter of spiritual vision to the gods, in his character of the *Inner Guide* (Conscience?) and also through the *<term>Vedas</term>* <note>The oldest and most sacred scriptures of Hinduism.</note>. *<term>Pranava</term>*—‘*<term>Om</term>*’ <note>The sacred syllable representing the sound of the universe.</note> is His name; Devotion to Him consists in contemplation of Him, beginning with the reciting of the *<term>Pranava</term>*, and ending in the direct perception of His Effulgence. (*<term>Yogasârasangraha</term>* <note>A text titled 'The Essence of Yoga Philosophy.'</note> pp. 27-28.)’

The ‘Conscious’ *<term>Yogin</term>* also has been classed under four heads, in accordance it would seem, with the above-mentioned four stages of Conscious Meditation: *viz*: (1) The *<term>Prathamakalpika</term>* <note>The beginner or one at the first stage.</note>, one who is at the first stage, who is still practising the Argumentative form of meditation, wherein he looks upon all ordinary things of the world as true under ordinary conditions, and so forth. (2) the *<term>Madhubhumika</term>* <note>One who has reached the 'honeyed' or sweet stage of insight.</note>—one occupying the honeyed or sweet stage—is one who has come to realise that the characters that he is

<summary>This page discusses the unique, limited role of God in Yoga philosophy as an object of devotion rather than a creator, and begins classifying the four stages of a practitioner's progress.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga philosophy, Ishvara, Vijnana Bhiksha, meditation stages, Prathamakalpika, Madhubhumika, Om, Vedas</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of the stages of yogic practitioners and the types of meditation, specifically focusing on the higher levels of conscious and unconscious absorption.</meta>

<page-num>vii</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

generally accustomed to attribute to things are not real, but merely imposed upon them by usage; he looks upon the very essence of things, as free from all such impositions; for this reason he is called ‘<term>Ritamhbaraprajna</term>’ <note>One whose wisdom is filled with truth; from the Sanskrit *ritam* (truth) and *prajna* (wisdom).</note>; and this stage is called ‘<term>Madhumati</term>’ (Honeyed) because it makes the aspirant feel extremely happy. (3) the ‘<term>Prajnâjyoti</term>’—of bright Intelligence—who has won complete control over all subtle entities from Nature downwards; it is into this stage that the aforesaid ‘Joyous’ meditation enters. (4) the <term>Atikrântabhavaniya</term> <note>One who has transcended the state of becoming or worldly experience.</note>—one who has passed beyond all that has to be experienced—is one who has reached the aforesaid ‘self-conscious’ meditation. The highest stage of this has been called ‘<term>Dharmamegha—Samadhi</term>’ ‘Cloud of Virtue’—which is thus described:—All desire for occult powers having been renounced, there immediately follows the discernment of the Self from Nature; and thus all Ignorance and consequent evils having disappeared, there appears in the mind of the aspirant a feeling of satiety, a sense of ‘enough’, with regard to all external things, gross and subtile alike; this is the step that leads to the highest unconscious meditation and hence has been called ‘<term>Dharmamegha</term>’—that which showers <term>dharma</term> <note>In this context, the highest spiritual merit or essential nature.</note> i. e., such virtue as omniscience and the like; when arrived at this stage the aspirant becomes a <term>Jivanmukta</term>, a Living Adept <note>Literally "liberated while alive"; one who has achieved spiritual freedom while still in the physical body.</note>.

Abstract or Unconscious Meditation is of two kinds—the <term>Upâyapratyaya</term> <note>Meditation resulting from conscious effort or specific means.</note> and the <term>Bhavapratyaya</term> <note>Meditation resulting from objective existence or birth.</note>. The <term>Upâyapratyaya</term> Meditation is that which is accomplished by practice, during present life, of the various means prescribed in the <term>Shâstras</term> <note>Sacred scriptures or authoritative texts.</note>. Such means are faith, energetic concentration of the mind, constant contemplation, meditation and discernment born of Conscious Meditation. These five lead to Unconscious Meditation through Absolute Dispassion; and when all this is employed with great energy there then comes

<summary>This page details the final stages of conscious meditation, culminating in the 'Cloud of Virtue' state and the attainment of the Living Adept status, while introducing the two types of unconscious meditation.</summary>
<keywords>Ritamhbaraprajna, Madhumati, Prajnâjyoti, Dharmamegha-Samadhi, Jivanmukta, Upâyapratyaya, Shâstras, Yoga, Meditation, spiritual liberation</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the classifications of meditation, specifically focusing on the distinction between Conscious and Unconscious states and the role of divine devotion.</meta>

<page-num>viii</page-num>
<header>PREFACE.</header>

regarding Unconscious Meditation <note>original: "Asamprajnata Samadhi," a state of deep absorption where the distinction between the meditator and the object of meditation disappears</note>. But even though the practitioner is not sufficiently energetic, if he is devoted to God, he obtains the same results. ‘Devotion to God’ is a very potent factor in this, inasmuch as it puts an effective end to all evils that impede the progress of meditation. The *Meditation of Becoming* <note>original: "Bhavapratyaya"</note> is that which is accomplished during the present life, but by force of practices carried on during preceding lives. This belongs to such persons as are either ‘*Bodiless Beings*’ <note>original: "Videhas"</note>—that is, beings such as the Golden Womb <note>original: "Hiranyagarbha," the first manifestation of the creative principle</note> and the like, who carry on all their functions by means of the subtle body, and do not need the gross physical body—or ‘*Those Merged in Nature*’ <note>original: "Prakritilayas"</note>, or those who have attained to the position of the godhead after having worked up their way through the several ‘coverings’ or ‘obstacles’ to their upward progress, in the shape of Nature and its modifications. As this passage upward cannot be completed in only one life, it is not possible in the case of Conscious Meditation <note>original: "Samprajnata Samadhi"</note>, which must come about as soon as its details have been fulfilled—after which there is nothing to delay the accomplishment of the meditation; all this therefore being finished in a single life. It is for this reason that Conscious Meditation has not been divided into *Meditation by Effort* <note>original: "Upayapratyaya"</note> and *Meditation of Becoming*: it is always *Meditation by Effort*.

Though Unconscious Meditation is of the form of Inhibition <note>the suppression of mental fluctuations</note>, yet, while being practised, it develops in due course fresh powers and faculties at each step; and through these the Meditation develops itself in due time; as each faculty appears it reduces the force of opposing faculties tending to retard the required Inhibition of Mind, until true knowledge is finally attained. Thus it is that in the final stage of Unconscious Meditation all contending forces and faculties are laid at rest, and all evils having ceased, there remains no force in the opposing *Accumulated Fate* <note>original: "Prarabdha Karma," the portion of past actions currently bearing fruit in this life</note> of the aspirant. The mind thus having all its functions duly performed and inhibited, melts away along with all its products, into its source.

<summary>This page explains the mechanics of Unconscious Meditation, distinguishing between progress made through effort in a single life and progress inherited from past lives, while emphasizing the power of devotion to God in removing obstacles.</summary>
<keywords>Unconscious Meditation, Devotion to God, Bodiless Beings, Golden Womb, Conscious Meditation, Inhibition of Mind, Accumulated Fate, spiritual evolution</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory preface, transitioning from the theoretical definition of spiritual liberation to the practical methods of Yoga as categorized by the practitioner's progress.</meta>

<page-num>ix</page-num>
-># PREFACE.<-

This absolute sleep of the mind constitutes the <term>Isolation</term> <note>Sanskrit: Kaivalya; the state of absolute freedom where the soul is detached from matter</note> (Release) of the Self.

The reader who is interested in tracing unity among the diverse philosophical systems will be interested to find that writers on Yoga have tried to reconcile the view that Release is obtained by Meditation with the theory that it can be obtained by <term>true knowledge</term> <note>Sanskrit: Jnana</note> alone. Both views are correct; both Yoga and <term>Knowledge</term> <note>original: "Jnâna"</note> lead to release, each in its own way and independently of the other. But one who would seek to obtain it by means of true knowledge, would also have to practise that much of Concrete Meditation which leads to the discernment of the self from other things.

This closes our study of the first section. The second section deals with the *means*—<term>Practice</term> <note>original: "Sadhana"</note>—of Yoga.

Aspirants to Yoga are divided into three classes—(1) <term>The Beginner</term> <note>original: "Ârurukshú"; literally 'one desirous of climbing'</note>, (2) <term>The Practitioner</term> <note>original: "Yunjâna"; one actively engaged in the exercises</note>, and (3) <term>The Adept</term> <note>original: "Yogâruddha"; one who has already ascended or attained the state of Yoga</note>; and for each of these distinct means or methods are laid down. We may recall here the description of the last given in the <term>Song of the Lord</term> <note>original: "Bhagavadgîtâ" VI. 4</note>:—"One is said to be an Adept in Yoga when he ceases to become attached to any action or to any objects of sense, and when he has given up all desire for fruits of actions."

Those who belong to this highest class have passed through all the preliminary stages during their previous lives, and at once attain the highest Yoga; all that is needed for such people is constant Practice and Dispassion; they do not need the external disciplinary yoga. The 'Practice' meant here is the 'endeavour to fix the mind in unflinching concentration'; and 'Dispassion' is the feeling of 'enough' that one has with regard to objects of enjoyment; it is not mere absence of passion or attachment. It is of two kinds,

<summary>This page explains the reconciliation between knowledge and meditation as paths to liberation and introduces the three stages of Yoga practitioners: the beginner, the practitioner, and the adept.</summary>
<keywords>Isolation, Kaivalya, Jnana, Sadhana, Bhagavad Gita, Dispassion, Vairagya, spiritual classification</keywords>

the inferior kind having its origin in our knowledge of
defects in the objects, and the higher consisting in our dis-

elements in them, but because of these being ‘not self’.

As the means tending to the accomplishment of the aforesaid
Exercise, the books lay down six methods called ‘***preparatory practices***.’ <note>original: "parikarma"; these are purifying acts intended to steady the mind.</note>
These are—(1) *Peace of mind*, brought about by friendly feel-
ings towards happy beings, sympathy with the suffering, joyous
regard for the virtuous and sympathetic disregard for the
vicious;—(2) *Functioning towards objects*—consisting of the
sensing of superphysical objects of sense;—(3) *Joyous Res-
plendent function*—tranquility of mind brought about by the
recognition of Intellect <note>original: "Buddhi"; the higher determinative faculty of the mind.</note>, and of the Self as distinct from it;—
(4) *Contemplation of dispassioned minds*—of such people as
Nârada <note>A semi-divine sage in Indian tradition known for his devotion and wisdom.</note> and the like;—(5) *Looking upon ordinary cognitions*
as those of dream or sleep,—and (6) *Contemplation of the
object of worship in the form of some divinity*. Stress has
not been laid upon the order in which the last two have to
be practised. Practice and Dispassion are means to both
Conscious and Unconscious Meditation.

For the second-class yogin, the Practitioner <note>original: "Yunjâna"; one who is actively engaged in the process of joining or yoking the mind.</note>, has been pres-
cribed what may be called ‘Disciplinary Yoga.’ The highest
form of this consists of—(1) *Religious Austerity*, the habi-
tuating of one’s body to the bearing of the ‘pairs of oppo-
sites’ <note>Such as heat and cold, or pleasure and pain.</note>,—(2) *Study of works teaching Final Release*, and
silent repetition of the Sacred Syllable <note>original: "Pranava"; the holy sound 'Om'.</note> and such other Sacred
Chants <note>original: "Mantras".</note>;—and (3) *Devotion to God*—the offering of one’s ac-
tions to the Lord, and the renouncing of all desire for fruits
of actions.

The direct purpose served by this Disciplinary Yoga is the
attenuation of the five *afflictions* <note>original: "kleshas"; the root causes of suffering and mental agitation.</note>, troubles or obstacles,—in the
shape of—(1) Ignorance—the regarding of the non-eternal

<page-num>xi</page-num>

as eternal; (2) Egotism—the identifying of the Self with the not-self; (3) Affection; (4) Aversion; and (5) Attachment to life—fear of death. These five have been called ‘<term>Viparyaya</term>’ <note>original: "Viparyaya" — meaning "misconception" or "error"</note> by the <term>Sankhyas</term> <note>Followers of Sankhya, one of the oldest orthodox schools of Indian philosophy</note>, under the names respectively of—<term>Tamas</term> <note>Darkness or inertia</note>, <term>Moha</term> <note>Delusion</note>, <term>Mahamoha</term> <note>Great delusion</note>, <term>Tamisra</term> <note>Gloom</note>, and <term>Andhatamisra</term> <note>Utter darkness</note>. Among them, Ignorance is the source of the other four. All these disturb the mind, and as such are impediments to Meditation. The attenuation of these lies in their being rendered incapable of putting obstacles in the way of right discernment of the Self from the Not-self.

The abovementioned Disciplinary Yoga purifies the mind, and thereby all chance for vice being removed, the cessation of vice is followed by the cessation of its effects in the form of Ignorance etc. All these—Ignorance and the rest—having been attenuated by the force of Disciplinary Yoga, the course of Right Discernment being no longer impeded, the Self comes to be rightly known; there being no chance for the further operation of Ignorance and the other ‘troubles,’ the Agent arrives at the stage known as that of the ‘<term>Jīvanmukta</term>’ <note>One who is "liberated while living"</note>. He continues, however, to live for a while, in order to afford opportunity for the working up of his *prārabdha* Karma <note>The portion of past karma that is currently bearing fruit in the present life</note>, on the exhaustion of which,—and on all the rest of his karma having been rendered inoperative by the absence of their necessary auxiliaries, Ignorance etc.,—there is no more need for the Self to be born again; it therefore experiences no more pain—It is in the state of Absolute Bliss or Release.

When we understand the real source of ‘bondage,’ the process of ‘Release’ becomes more intelligible. As a matter of fact all virtue and vice are the result of Ignorance, Egotism, Affection, Aversion and Attachment to Life; and we know also that it is only virtue and vice that are the sources of the fruits of past actions, in the shape of birth, experiences during life and so forth; these experiences giving rise to

<page-num>xii</page-num>

pleasure and pain, the Self becomes bound up in these. When therefore, the root of all this evil, Ignorance <note>original: "Avidya," the fundamental misunderstanding of the nature of reality</note>, is destroyed by the right Discernment of the Self from the Not-self, all evils attendant upon that root-evil, cease forthwith. Thus there being no cause left, the effects, virtue and vice, cannot appear; and as these evils are necessary auxiliaries to the fruition of that portion of the past <term>karma</term> <note>the sum of a person's actions in this and previous states of existence, viewed as deciding their fate in future existences</note> which has not been set in motion, all these latter are rendered ineffective; and those already set in motion having been exhausted, there is no further occasion for the Self to undergo experiences—whereupon it becomes "free," "released."

For the aspirant of the lowest class, the man of the ordinary life, there are eight "means to <term>yoga</term>" <note>literally "union" or "discipline"</note>. These are—(1) <term>Yama</term>, Restraint, consisting in harmlessness, truthfulness, respect for other's property, continence, and freedom from avarice, (2) <term>Niyama</term> or obligation, consisting of religious austerities, study, contentment, purity and adoration of god. These two have been regarded as purely extrinsic and as such chiefly purificatory in their character. (3) <term>Asana</term>, Posture;—much undue importance has been attached to this factor of <term>Yoga</term> by later writers; but <term>Patanjali</term> <note>the sage credited with compiling the Yoga Sutras</note> contents himself by the simple statement, that "Posture" (for <term>yoga</term>) is that which is most conducive to fixity and comfort. (4) <term>Prânâyâma</term>, Regulation of Breath—the effect of this is more or less physiological in character, "physiology" pertaining also to the inner spiritual centres of energy. (5) <term>Pratyâhâra</term>, Abstraction, consists in the withdrawal of the mind and the sense-organs from their respective objects. These five have been regarded as "external" factors, exercising a more or less exterior influence, dealing as they do with the controlling of the body, breathing and the senses. The next three have been called "internal," as exercising an inner influence, and hence of greater importance, bearing upon the mind directly. These are—(6) <term>Dhâranâ</term>, steadiness, fixing of the mind to a definite point in space

of Pure Meditation:—‘*These are obstacles to Samadhi, but are considered perfections in the worldly state*.’ <note>original: "Samâdhâvupasargâ vyutthâne siddhayah"</note> These have been described with a view chiefly to show to
the aspirant that even such sources of pleasure are not things
to long for—they are as imperfect in their character as the
ordinary pleasures of the world.

The fourth and the last section deals with <term>Kaivalya</term> <note>Absolute freedom or isolation from worldly bonds</note> or
<term>Mukti</term> <note>Liberation or release from the cycle of rebirth</note>. This we have already explained above.

# Bibliographical Information pertaining to the Yogasutra.

---

1. **Yogasutras** <note>The foundational collection of aphorisms on Yoga philosophy</note> by Patanjali—printed with commentaries at Calcutta, Bombay and Benares.
2. **Bhashya** <note>A primary "exposition" or commentary</note> of Vyasa—commentary on ( 1 ) printed in Benares, Bombay Sanskrit Series, and Calcutta.
3. **Tattvavaisharadi** <note>The "Exposition of Truth"</note> by Vachaspati Mishra, a commentary on No. ( 2 )—printed along with it in Bombay Sanskrit Series, and also in Calcutta and Benares.
4. **Patanjalarahasya** <note>The "Secret of Patanjali"</note>—commentary on No. ( 3 )—not printed. Manuscripts available.
5. **Rajamartanda** <note>The "Royal Sun"</note> by Bhojadeva—a brief commentary on No. ( 1 )—translated by Dr. Rajendralal Mittra, and printed in the *Bibliotheca Indica*.
6. **Yogavartika** <note>A "critical gloss" on Yoga</note> by Vijnanabhiksu—commentary on ( 1 ) and parts of ( 2 ), printed in the 'Pandit,' Benares.
7. **Yogamaniprabha** <note>The "Jewel-light of Yoga"</note> Commentary on (1), printed in Benares.
8. **Yogachandrika** <note>The "Moonlight of Yoga"</note>—commentary on ( 1 )—printed in Benares.
9. Nagesha Bhatta's commentary on ( 1 )—printed in the 'Pandit.'
10. Narayana Tirtha's commentary on ( 1 )—not printed; available in Manuscripts. This is a work of exceptional merit and usefulness.
11. English Translation of ( 1 ), with English commentary by M. N. Divedi—printed by the Bombay Theosophical Publishing Fund.
12. English Translation of ( 1 ) and ( 2 ), here offered to the public, and also in course of preparation by Mrs. Annie Besant and Bhagavan Dasa, and yet another by Dr. Woods (for the Harvard Oriental Series).
13. Hindi Translation of (1) and (2)—printed at Moradabad.

# THE PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA.

## THE APHORISMS OF PATANJALI—WITH THE COMMENTARY OF VYASA.

### WITH EXTRACTS FROM THE "CLARITY OF TRUTH" BY VACHASPATI MISRA.

->An English Translation—by GANGANATHA JHA, Master of Arts<-

---

# SECTION I.

*Commentary:*—May the resplendent Lord of Serpents <note>An invocation to Ananta or Shesha, often identified with Patanjali</note>, the many-hooded One, protect us—He who administers Yoga while himself equipped with it, having a body of pure white, delighting in a serpentine form, the fountain-head of all wisdom, whose troubles are at an end, holding, as he does, terrific poison—One who, having renounced his original divine form, is born in this world for the manifold benefits of humankind.

***Aphorism (1):***—**Now begins the instruction on Yoga.**

*Commentary:*—The word *Now* <note>original: "Atha"</note> denotes "Commencement" <note>original: "Adhikara"</note>, that is, the formal beginning of a specialized topic. The meaning of the Aphorism is: The Science of the instruction of Yoga is to be understood to have begun here.

<term>Yoga</term> <note>The practice of mental discipline and union</note> is <term>Samadhi</term> <note>Deep meditative absorption or concentration</note>; but this meditation is a property of the internal organ <note>The mind-stuff or "Chitta"</note>, common to all its various stages. The stages or states of the internal organ are: (1) The Fickle <note>original: "Kshipta"; a state of constant movement and distraction</note>, (2) The Infatuated <note>original: "Mugdha"; a state of dullness or stupor</note>, (3) The Distracted <note>original: "Vikshipta"; occasionally steady but prone to wandering</note>, (4) The One-pointed <note>original: "Ekagra"; focused on a single object</note>, and (5) The Controlled <note>original: "Niruddha"; where all mental fluctuations are restrained</note>. From among these, the meditation in the Distracted state of the internal organ, being subordinate to the distractions—

<summary>This opening page introduces the Yoga Sutras of Patanjali, beginning with a traditional invocation and defining Yoga as "Samadhi" (meditative absorption). It outlines the five fundamental states of the mind, ranging from total distraction to complete control.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Sutras, Patanjali, Vyasa, Samadhi, mental states, meditation, Indian philosophy, Chitta</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory exposition of the Yoga-Sutras, specifically defining the different states of the mind and the types of Samadhi (meditative absorption).</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>THE PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA.</header>

tion, has no place on the side of <term>Yoga (the practice of union)</term> proper. That (meditation) however which belongs to the one-pointed internal organ, enlightening the true and agreeable object, destroying the troubles, loosening the bonds of action, and bringing one face to face with Control proper—such meditation is called the Conscious (<term>Samprajnâta: cognitive absorption</term>) <term>Yoga</term>. This is attended by Argumentation, Deliberation, Joy and Egoism.

The entire subjugation of all the functions (of the internal organ) constitutes what is called Unconscious or Abstract (<term>Asamprajnâta: supra-cognitive absorption</term>) <term>Yoga</term>.

**Notes :**—At the outset it may be stated that the translation follows the interpretation of **Vâchaspati Misra**, modified in places by that of **Vijnâna Bhikshu** <note>Two influential historical commentators on the Yoga Sutras</note>.

“*<term>Yoganusaśana (The Instruction of Yoga)</term>*”—**Dr. Rajendra Lal Mitra** has been apparently misled by a wrong reading of the <term>Bhâshya (Commentary)</term>—having read it as “*<term>yoganus’âsànam</term> nam śàstram* <note>original: "The science named the instruction of Yoga"</note>,” he makes the commentator explain the word (“*<term>yogà’nusàsana</term>*”) as being the specific name of the work. There is no “*name*” in the <term>Bhâshya</term>, however; hence the meaning of the <term>Bhâshya</term> is as given in the translation. If however we accept **Dr. Mitra’s** reading (and it is by no means a bad reading), his interpretation would be the most appropriate. But **Vâchaspati Misra** evidently rejects this reading; using, as he does, the root meaning of the word <term>anusâsana (instruction/exposition)</term>, to point to the prior authorship of **Hiranyagarbha** <note>The mythical founder of the Yoga system</note>, with regard to the science of <term>Yoga</term>. It is remarkable that **Dr. Mitra** should have overlooked this fact when quoting from the *<term>Tattwa-vais’âradi (The Clarity of Truth)</term>*.

“*<term>Véditayvam</term>*” (“to be understood”) by the disciples (**Vijnâna Bhikshu**). This word is made by **Vâchaspati Misra** to mean that though the object to be explained is <term>Yoga</term> (practical) and not the science, yet the pupil is to *understand* the latter as the topic taken in hand, because practical <term>Yoga</term> itself can be explained only by means of the science; and as such the final aim of the exposition comes to be practical <term>Yoga</term> itself.

“*And it...* &c.” *And* = but. Though etymologically the word *<term>yoga</term>* = *<term>Samadhi (meditative absorption)</term>*, yet this latter is only a part of <term>Yoga</term> (as will be explained later on). “*Stages*”—The *<term>Madhumati (Honeyed state)</term>* and the rest, to be explained below. ‘Meditation’ (*<term>Samâdhi</term>*) is only the root-meaning of the word <term>yoga</term>, the real meaning being ‘the suppression of the functions of the internal organ.’

“Property of the internal organ”—this is added in order to set aside the view that the functions belong to the Spirit. *<term>Chitta (Mind-stuff)</term>* is *<term>Buddhi (Intellect)</term>*, the Internal Organ; for certainly no cognition can belong to the unchanging eternal Intelligence (*<term>Chiti-S’akti: the power of consciousness</term>*).

The *<term>Kshipta (Restless state)</term>* abounds in <term>Rajas (activity/passion)</term>, the *<term>Mugdha (Stupefied state)</term>* in <term>Tamas (darkness/inertia)</term>, and the *<term>Vikshipta (Distracted state)</term>* in <term>Sattwa (purity/light)</term>. The One-pointed or Concentrated state is that in

<summary>This page defines the two primary forms of Yoga—Conscious (Samprajnata) and Unconscious (Asamprajnata)—and provides scholarly commentary on the linguistic roots and philosophical interpretations of the text.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga-Darsana, Samadhi, Samprajnata Yoga, Asamprajnata Yoga, Vachaspati Misra, Vijnana Bhikshu, Chitta, Gunas, Rajas, Tamas, Sattwa</keywords>

# CHAPTER I—APHORISM 2.

in which all the functions of the internal organ have ceased, and only the <term>Sanskara<note>The latent impressions or "residue" left behind by past actions and thoughts.</note></term> is left behind.

“***True and agreeable object***” (“***Saddhâtam artham***”)—The particle ***Bhâta*** precludes all superimposed (and hence false) objects; and “***Sat***” is employed in order to preclude the functions of Sleep which abound in <term>Tamas<note>The quality of darkness, inertia, or ignorance.</note></term>, and as such, causing pain, cannot be called “agreeable.”

“*Enlightening.*”—The prefix *pra* denotes direct perception.

In order to define the Yoga above mentioned we have the next aphorism—

**Aphorism (2):—Yoga is the suppression of the functions of the internal organ.**

*Commentary:*—Since the word ‘all’ does not appear before ‘functions,’ *Conscious* or *Concrete* meditation also becomes included in the name ‘*yoga*.’

The internal organ is made up of three attributes—inasmuch as it has the characters of truth, activity, and inertia. <term>Sattwa<note>The quality of purity, light, and harmony.</note></term> in the form of the internal organ, when intermixed with <term>Rajas<note>The quality of passion, energy, and movement.</note></term> and <term>Tamas</term>, comes to be attached to power and the objects of the senses. <term>Sattwa</term> again, when mixed up with <term>Tamas</term>, tends towards sin, ignorance, attachment, and weakness. The same <term>Sattwa</term> lastly everywhere shining, on having its covering of illusion removed, when tinged by <term>Rajas</term>, tends towards virtue, knowledge, dispassion, and power. The same <term>Sattwa</term> however, when passed beyond the taint of <term>Rajas</term>, fixed in its own form, consisting purely of <term>discriminative knowledge<note>The ability to distinguish between the eternal soul and the temporary material world.</note></term> of Matter and Spirit, tends towards contemplation, called the “Cloud of Virtue.” This is what is called “Supreme Contemplation” by the <term>Dhyánis<note>Meditators or practitioners of deep contemplation.</note></term>.

The Sentient Faculty (or Intelligence proper), the Unchanging and the Immobile—having the objects presented to Itself, is pure and endless; and It consists in the attribute of <term>Sattwa</term>. For this reason discriminative knowledge is contrary to this (since such knowledge is modifiable, mobile, non-intelligent, finite and impure).

<summary>This page provides a philosophical breakdown of the second Yoga Sutra, defining Yoga as the restraint of mental activities and explaining how the three qualities of nature (Sattwa, Rajas, and Tamas) influence the mind's state.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Sutras, internal organ, Chitta, Sattwa, Rajas, Tamas, discriminative knowledge, Cloud of Virtue, meditation, Sanskara</keywords>

# YOGA-DARSANA

For this reason, the internal organ—having become indifferent to the sentient faculty—(naturally) suppresses the aforesaid discriminative knowledge; and when in this condition, it centres itself in mere residuum (<term>Sanskara</term> <note>The latent impressions or "seeds" left behind in the mind by past actions and thoughts</note>). This is "Seedless Meditation"—and because nothing else is cognised in this state, therefore it is called "Unconscious" or Abstract (<term>asamprajnâta</term> <note>A state of deep absorption where the distinction between the knower, the known, and the act of knowing disappears</note>).

Thus is Yoga—of the form of the suppression of the functions of the internal organ—two-fold.

**Notes:**—"Functions" <term>Pramâna</term> <note>Valid means of knowledge or correct cognition</note> and the rest are to be described hereafter. "All"—If Yoga were defined as the suppression of all the functions etc., then Conscious Meditation would be excluded; because such meditation is not totally free from such functions of the internal organ as abound in pure <term>Sattwa</term> <note>The quality of light, goodness, and harmony</note>. The definition given however includes this Meditation also, inasmuch as there too we have the suppression of the functions of <term>Rajas</term> <note>The quality of passion, activity, and movement</note> and <term>Tamas</term> <note>The quality of darkness, chaos, and inertia</note>.

"Truth etc."—representing <term>Sattwa</term>, <term>Rajas</term> and <term>Tamas</term> respectively. These characteristics however imply the others also—namely, happiness and buoyancy (of <term>Sattwa</term>), remorse and unhappiness (of <term>Rajas</term>), and sluggishness (of <term>Tamas</term>). This three-fold character of the internal organ is laid down in order to explain the three stages of it mentioned above. (The <term>Kshipta</term> <note>The "wandering" or restless state of mind</note> etc.)

"Intermixed with <term>Rajas</term> and <term>Tamas</term>"—in equal quantities. This represents the Distracted (<term>Vikshipta</term> <note>A state where the mind is occasionally steady but easily distracted</note>) stage.

"When mixed up with <term>Tamas</term>"—representing the Infatuated (<term>mugdha</term> <note>A state of mental stupor or ignorance</note>) stage.

"Ignorance"—Mistaken knowledge.

"Tinged with <term>Rajas</term>"—representing the Active (<term>Kshipta</term>) state.

"Passed beyond the taint etc."—Representing the fourth stage of the internal organ. Here ends the application of the definition to Conscious Meditation.

"The Sentient faculty etc."—This laying down of the propriety of the sentient faculty and the impropriety of discriminative knowledge serves as an introduction to the supreme suppressive (unconscious) Meditation which leads to the rejection of discriminative knowledge, and the acceptance of the Sentient Faculty pure and simple.

"Pure"—because free from impurities in the shape of pleasure, pain and the like.

"Having objects presented to herself"—This is added in order to meet the objection that 'the sentient faculty (1) cognising the various objects of sense and (2) accepting and rejecting the various forms of such objects,—

<summary>This page defines "Seedless" or Unconscious Meditation and explains how Yoga involves the suppression of mental functions categorized by the three qualities of nature: Sattwa, Rajas, and Tamas. It details the various stages of the mind—from distracted and infatuated to the pure state required for deep meditation.</summary>

<keywords>Yoga-Darsana, Unconscious Meditation, Sattwa, Rajas, Tamas, mental functions, Sanskara, asamprajnata, mental states, discriminative knowledge</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion of Yoga from the previous page, specifically addressing the nature of the "Self" or "Spectator" when the mind is at rest.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
->CHAPTER I—SUTRA 3.<-

cannot be said to be either pure or endless'. The objection would have held good if the sentient faculty <note>The Purusha or pure consciousness</note> took upon itself the various forms etc. But such is not the fact; it is the Intellect <note>original: "Buddhi"</note>, the internal organ, which assumes the various forms of the objects of sense, and presents these as such to the sentient faculty, which latter being by its very nature immobile, is untouched by these. The possibility of such cognition by a Faculty itself untouched by the objects, will be explained later on.

> "For this reason etc."—From here begins the application of the definition to Unconscious Meditation <note>Asamprajnata Samadhi, the highest state of focus where even the seed of thought is absent</note>.

> "Seed-less"—devoid of the seeds of Birth, life, and experience etc.

The definition of Yoga then comes to be this: Yoga is that particular state of the internal organ, in which its functions (in the form of Right Knowledge <note>original: "Pramâna"</note> and the rest) have been suppressed.

***

The internal organ being in that condition—and as such there being a complete absence of objects—what is the character of the Spirit <note>original: "Purusha"</note>—being as it is of the nature of the cognition of the Intellect <note>original: "Buddhi"</note>?

**Reply:—**

## **Sutra (3):—Then there is an abiding of the spectator in its own form.**

*Commentary:*—"The abiding in its own form" is *then*, as in beatitude <note>The state of liberation or Kaivalya</note> (identical with) the sentient faculty. In the (ordinary) waking state however, the sentient faculty, though being the same, is not (exactly) the same.

**Notes:—**The present Sutra is introduced in order—(1) to show the motive for the said Yoga, (2) to complete the definition of Yoga, and (3) to show the unchanging character of the Spirit.

The sense of the question may be thus rendered, in the words of Vijnana Bhikshu <note>A 16th-century philosopher and commentator on Yoga and Vedanta</note>: "When the internal organ is in the condition of Abstract Meditation, then in what character does the Spirit stand—being as it is of the form of the cognition of the Intellect, *i.e.* its witness? Does it even then, as in the ordinary waking state, stand in the form of illumination <note>original: "prakâs'a"</note>,—its non-perception being due to the absence of objects? or does it then become, like a log of wood, non-illuminating?" The aphorism replies, admitting the former of these two alternatives.

<summary>This page explains that during the highest state of Yoga, the Spirit (Purusha) remains in its pure, illuminating state rather than being lost, even though the mind's functions have ceased. It distinguishes between the Intellect (Buddhi), which changes shape to match objects, and the Spirit, which remains an unchanging witness.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Sutras, Purusha, Buddhi, Samadhi, Vijnana Bhikshu, consciousness, meditation, internal organ</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical commentary on the nature of the soul and its relationship to the mind during different states of consciousness, specifically contrasting the meditative state with the ordinary waking state.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
<header>6 PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA.</header>

"*Then*"—during Abstract Meditation <note>original: "Asamprajnata Samadhi," a state of deep absorption where the mind is still</note>,—and not during the ordinary waking state.

"*As in beatitude*"—This is added in order to show that the motive is no other than the removal of pain in the form of the mental functions.

"*In the waking &c.*"—This is added in anticipation of the following objection: "The sentient faculty abiding in its form during meditation, brings about absence of pain; however, not abiding in it in the ordinary state—and as such bringing about pain—would make that faculty subject to change; and if such a difference were set aside, there would be an identity between the meditative and the waking states." The sense of the reply is that the unchanging sentient faculty never swerves from its nature, but continues the same in all the states—waking or meditative. The only difference however is, that in the waking state, the Intelligence does not shine so well as it does during Meditation or Beatitude.

"*Abiding &c.*"—The unconditioned and pure form of the <term>Purusha</term> <note>The true Self or pure consciousness, distinct from the mind and body</note> is pure intelligence (a sentient faculty),—the calm and the active &c. due to the predominance of the one or the other of the attributes <note>original: "Gunas," the three qualities of nature: balance, activity, and inertia</note> being only its conditioned form, like the redness of the crystal due to its proximity to a red flower; as the crystal regains its pure whiteness on the removal of the red object, so on the removal of the functions of the internal organ <note>original: "Antahkarana," the mind-complex</note>, does the <term>Purusha</term> regain its unalloyed abiding in its nature. The unchanging <term>Purusha</term> is of the nature of pure light, during meditation, as well as during the waking state.

In comparison with Abstract Meditation, Concrete Meditation <note>original: "Samprajnata Samadhi," meditation with an object of focus</note> is to be taken along with the waking state.

---

How then? On account of the objects having been presented,—

### **Aphorism (4):**—At other times, the Self appears to take the form of the mental functions.

**Commentary:**—In the waking state the functions of the <term>Purusha</term> are identical with those of the internal organ. Hence the <term>Aphorism</term> <note>original: "Sutra"</note>: "Perception is one and cognition or knowledge is Perception."

The internal organ, like the magnet, affecting by mere proximity, being the object of perception, becomes the self of the <term>Purusha</term>, the Lord. Therefore the cause of the <term>Purusha</term>'s perception of the functions of the internal organ is its eternal connection therewith.

**Notes:**—"*At other times*"—In the waking state.

"*Identical &c.*"—That is to say—as in the case of the crystal and the red flower, so in that of the <term>Purusha</term> and the internal organ, close proximity leads to a notion of identity; and this leads to the imposition of the func-

<meta>catchword: func- (continuation of "functions")</meta>

<summary>This page explains that while the pure consciousness (Purusha) remains unchanging, it appears to take on the qualities of the mind's activities during the waking state, much like a clear crystal appears red when placed near a red flower. It introduces the fourth Yoga Sutra, which describes how the Self identifies with mental fluctuations when not in a state of deep meditation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical commentary on the Yoga Sutras, specifically focusing on the relationship between the Purusha (Pure Consciousness) and the internal organ (the mind or intellect) during the state of worldly experience.</meta>

# CHAPTER I—SUTRA 4.

The reflections of the internal organ <note>The "internal organ" refers to the *Antahkarana*, which includes the mind, intellect, and ego.</note> on the Spirit <note>original: "Purusha"; the eternal, unchanging silent witness or Pure Consciousness.</note>—for example, "I am happy," etc. Although this imposition is also only a function of the internal organ and, as such, does not affect the true nature of the Spirit, yet the presentation of the Spirit in the colors of the internal organ leads to the notions of "wrongness," "agency," "discriminative wisdom," etc., with regard to the Spirit, which is, by its very nature, free from all these. This point is dealt with at length in IV-22 and III-34.

"*Hence the Aphorism <note>original: "Sutra"</note> etc.*"—This Aphorism is attributed by Vachaspati Misra as well as by Vijnana Bhikshu <note>Two influential historical commentators on Yoga and Sankhya philosophy.</note>, to Panchasikha Acharya, the great master of the Sankhya <note>One of the six classical schools of Indian philosophy, closely allied with Yoga.</note> school.

The question started by the first part of this Aphorism is—How can the perception be *one* and the same in the case of the internal organ and that of the Spirit? For, the perception of the internal organ is no other than the function with regard to the various objects of sense and to discriminative knowledge—a function inferred to be insentient on account of its owing its origin to insentient Nature <note>original: "Prakriti"</note>; whereas the perception of the Spirit must be other than this—being simple cognition in the form of pure intelligence.

To this the second part replies—"*Cognition is Perception.*" The sameness or oneness spoken of is with regard to the fleeting (appearing and disappearing) cognition, to which the term is ordinarily applied. Intelligence is the Spirit's nature, not its cognition (as the question presupposes), amenable as it is to inference from sacred texts, and not to ordinary perception. By this it is shown that in the waking state the final cause is Ignorance <note>original: "Avidya"</note>, which also leads to the connection of the Spirit with the internal organ, which in its turn leads to the idea (in the Spirit) of ownership, and thence of experience (pleasure and pain).

"*The internal organ like the magnet etc.*"—This is added in anticipation of the objection that the fact of the connection of the internal organ with the Spirit helping towards the experience of the latter, would prove the modifying <note>i.e., changing or evolving</note> character of the Spirit. The sense of the Commentary <note>original: "Bhashya"</note> is that the internal organ is not in physical contact with the Spirit, but only in proximity with it; and this proximity too is neither in space nor in time (because the Spirit is unconnected with these, being eternal and omnipresent), but only in the form of *Capability*. And the faculty of experience (of the Spirit), and that of being the object of experience (of the internal organ) cannot be denied; with this last point in view it is added—"*being the object of perception*"—that is, having developed into the form of the various objects of sense, Sound, etc.—and as such becoming objects of experience.

"*Of the Spirit*"—Though experience, being of the form of the objects of sense, is really a function of the internal organ, yet it is spoken of here as belonging to the Spirit, on account of the identity of the functions of the two (in the waking state).

<summary>This page explains how the unchanging Spirit (Purusha) appears to experience pleasure and pain through its proximity to the mind (internal organ), despite remaining essentially unaffected. It clarifies that this perceived "oneness" of experience is a result of ignorance and the mind's reflection upon the Spirit, much like a magnet influencing nearby objects without physical contact.</summary>

<keywords>Purusha, internal organ, Vachaspati Misra, Vijnana Bhikshu, Panchasikha Acharya, Sankhya, Bhashya, discriminative wisdom, cognition, ignorance, Prakriti</keywords>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>THE PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA.</header>

Thus it is proved that though there is no real contact of the <term>Purusha</term><note>The pure, eternal Consciousness or True Self, distinct from the mind and body.</note> with the internal organ <note>The "Antahkarana," comprising the mind, intellect, and ego.</note>, yet there applies to it the character of participating in the benefits offered by the internal organ, as also that of being unchanging.

" *The Cause of the Purusha's etc.*"—This is added in anticipation of the following objection: "The idea of ownership, leading to experience, has been said to be due to Ignorance—but to which cause do you attribute the action of this Ignorance? There must be some cause for this." The sense of the reply is that the cause is no other than an (hypothetical) eternal connection (between the eternal Soul and the eternal Ignorance), which is like the relation of the seed and the sprout <note>A classic Indian philosophical metaphor for a beginningless cycle where cause and effect perpetually follow one another.</note>.

->The internal organ being manifold, the objects of suppression,<-

# Aphorism (5):—the functions are five-fold, painful
# (and) non-painful.

*Commentary:*—The "painful" are causes of pain—the fruitful ground for the aggregate of the <term>karmic residua</term><note>Samskaras; the subconscious imprints left by past actions that shape future character and destiny.</note>. And the "non-painful" have knowledge for their object, and are opposed to the action of the <term>Attributes</term><note>The Gunas: Sattva (purity), Rajas (activity), and Tamas (inertia), which constitute the material world.</note>. The non-painful ones falling in the current of the painful ones, though occurring between two painful ones, are still non-painful; similarly are the painful ones, occurring among non-painful ones, called "painful."

Residua of one kind are those brought about by the functions of the same kind, and also the functions by the residua—this wheel of function and residua is incessantly revolving.

Thus the internal organ, having attained to this condition, and its action having ended, stands equal to the <term>Spirit</term><note>Purusha; the state of liberation where the mind no longer obscures the Soul's purity.</note>, or is dissolved.

These functions being painful, and non-painful, are five-fold.

Notes.—"<term>Klesáhetukah</term><note>original: "Klesáhetukah"</note>" is explained by Vachaspati Misra <note>A 10th-century philosopher and famous commentator on the Yoga Sutras.</note> as also meaning—"caused by the afflictions—<term>Asmitâ</term><note>Egoism or "I-am-ness."</note> etc." "Knowledge"—Knowledge discriminative of the Spirit and the Attributes; hence "opposed to the action of the Attributes."

"The painful ones falling etc."—This is added, in order to meet the following objection: "All individuals being born with passions and attachments, they are all possessed only of the painful functions; and no non-painful ones are possible among a host of the other kind; consequently to assert that the painful functions are suppressed by the non-painful ones, and these latter

<summary>This page explains that the Soul's apparent involvement with the mind is due to a beginningless cycle of Ignorance and classifies mental fluctuations into five types, which are either painful or conducive to liberation.</summary>

->PADA I—SUTRA 6 & 7.<-
<margin>2</margin>

again by the higher **Dispassion** <note>original: "Dispassio"; refers to *Vairagya*, the practice of non-attachment</note>, is a mere waste of words. **Vijnana Bhikshu** <note>A 16th-century philosopher who wrote influential commentaries on Yoga and Vedanta</note> however explains it as anticipating the following objection: “The author of the aphorisms has laid down the advisability only of the Dark (the painful) and the Good (the non-painful) functions, and he has altogether ignored those of a mixed character.” The reply serves to include these latter in the two mentioned in the aphorism.

> “*The remaining ones, etc.*”—With a view to describe the effects of the functions of the internal organ <note>The *Antahkarana*, or the mind-complex</note>, the commentator begins here with the mention of the cause of the troubles of Re-birth.

> “*Of one kind, etc.*”—The two kinds here spoken of are the ‘painful’ and the ‘non-painful.’

> “*Thus the internal, etc.*”—Thus, *i.e.*, being of the nature of the karmic cycle.

> “*In this condition.*”—Having been suppressed, *i.e.*, during meditation.

> “*Is turning*”—Till the completion of the suppression.

> “*Stands equal to the spirit*”—This in the case of a living adept—the <term>***Jivanmukta***</term> <note>One who has attained spiritual liberation while still inhabiting a physical body</note>.

> “*Is dissolved*”—In the case of the ordinary beatitude. The former disappearance of the internal organ is called its <term>***Sarupanāśa***</term> (*i.e.*, the destruction of the internal organ while its form remains), and the latter the <term>***Arupanāśa***</term> (destruction without form).

## Sutra (6):—Right Notion, Misconception, Fancy, Sleep and Memory.

**Commentary:—Among these ( *i.e.* the five-fold functions of the internal organ just named )—**

## Sutra (7):—Perception, Inference, Testimony are the right Notions.

**Commentary:—The internal organ being affected by the external object through the path-way of the sense-organs, there arises a functioning thereof, having the aforesaid external thing for its object, and having as its principal concern the ascertainment of a certain specific aspect of the object, which naturally partakes of the generic as well as of the specific. Such a function**

<summary>This page defines the five types of mental functions and begins explaining "Right Notion" (Pramana), specifically how the mind perceives external objects through the senses. It also distinguishes between liberation while living and final liberation after death.</summary>
<keywords>Patanjali, Yoga Sutras, Vijnana Bhikshu, Jivanmukta, mental functions, perception, inference, testimony, liberation, dispassion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical analysis of the "Right Notions" or valid sources of knowledge within the Yoga system, specifically defining Perception, Inference, and Testimony.</meta>

# YOGA PHILOSOPHY

constitutes the Right Notion, called *Perception*. The effect of this Right Notion is the cognition of the function of the internal organ <note>The "internal organ" or *antahkarana* refers to the mind's processing faculty</note>—the cognition belonging to the Spirit <note>original: "Purusha"</note>, and being co-ordinate or identical (with the Intellect <note>original: "Buddhi"</note>). The Spirit is the joint-cogniser with the internal organ. This we shall explain later on.

Of the object of inference there is a certain relationship which is common to all homogenous objects, and dissociated from the heterogenous ones; the function, having this relation for its object, concerned chiefly with the ascertainment of the generic (character of things), is *Inference*. As for example—"The planets have motion, because they approach different regions, like Chaitra <note>A common name used in Indian logic examples, similar to "John Doe"</note>;—the Vindhya Mountain having no such approach, has no motion."

A certain object, having been either perceived or inferred by an authoritative person, is verbally expressed for the sake of transferring that cognition to another person. The function having, through words, such a thing for its object, is Testimony for the listener. That Testimony fails which is based on the assertion of an untrustworthy speaker, who has neither seen nor inferred an object truly. If however, the original speaker has seen and inferred an object truly, then the testimony becomes infallible.

**Notes: (1)** "*Cognition of the function of the internal organ*"—The form of the function is—"*This is a jar;*" whereas that of the cognition is—"*I perceive the jar.*"

**(2)** "*Is co-ordinate or identical etc.*"—This is added in anticipation of the following objection: "The cognition being in the Spirit, it cannot be the effect of a function of the internal organ." The sense of the reply is that the cognition of the Spirit is not *produced*; what happens is that the intelligence of the Spirit, being reflected in the mirror of the internal organ, is stamped with the character of the objective form then predominating in the *Intellectual* <note>original: "Buddhic"</note> function. Thus then, this intelligence being identical with the internal organ, with which again the function is identical,—both come to have a common substrate; and as such the cognition of the Spirit is quite rightly said to be the effect of the function (See Aphorism I—4.)

**(3)** "*Having as its principal concern etc.*"—This serves to differentiate Perception from Inference. Though the generic character of things also appears in Perception, yet this is always subordinated to the specific.

<summary>This page defines the three valid sources of knowledge in Yoga philosophy: Perception (direct experience), Inference (logical deduction), and Testimony (reliable verbal authority). It explains how the Spirit perceives objects through the reflection of the mind's functions.</summary>

<keywords>Yoga Philosophy, Perception, Inference, Testimony, Spirit, Internal Organ, Intellect, Logic, Epistemology</keywords>

# SECTION I—SUTRA 8.

This definition of Perception implies direct cognition of all kinds, thus applying also to discriminative knowledge <note>The ability to distinguish between the material world and the pure self</note>.

“Joint Cogniser.”—In anticipation of the following objection:—

> ‘All external perception has been seen to take the form of the object; Spirit <note>original: "Purusha"; the pure, unchanging consciousness</note> on the other hand, being unchanging, can never take any objective form; under these circumstances, how can the Spirit be said to be the witness of the function of the internal organ? Or, secondly, how can the cognition of the Spirit be identical with the said function?’ The sense of the reply is that though the real cogniser is the internal organ <note>The mind-complex or "chitta"</note>, yet the Spirit becomes its co-partner (in the manner explained in note 2).

(4) “*Later on*”—i.e. in Sutra IV. 21.

(5) “*Common to the homogenous*”—This differentiates the definition from all kinds of Contradictory Inference.

(6) “*Dissociated from the heterogenous*”—This sets aside the *Too Broad General and Partial Inferences*.

(7) “*Relationship*”—This relationship is constituted by the Middle Term, the mark or the characteristic.—This sets aside the Incomplete Inference.

(8) “*concerned Chiefly etc.*”—This differentiates the given definition from Perception.

(9) “*An authoritative person*”—The authority, meant here, implies the possession of the knowledge of truth, mercy, and the extreme subtle sensitiveness of the sense-organs.

(10) “*If the original speaker etc.*”—This is added in order to validate the authority of the *Traditional Texts* <note>original: "Smritis"; a category of Hindu texts based on remembered tradition, such as the Laws of Manu</note>, where the original speaker is said to be God Himself.

---

## Sutra (8): Misconception is false notion, abiding in a form, which is not that (of the object).

**Commentary:—**This is not a right notion; because, it is always set aside by right notion, which has for its object something really existing in the external world. It has been always seen that a right notion always sets aside a wrong one; *e.g.* the perception of the double moon <note>A classic optical illusion used in Indian philosophy to illustrate error</note> is set aside by the subsequent perception of the single moon.

It is this that constitutes the five-fold Illusion or Nescience <note>original: "Avidya"; spiritual ignorance or lack of true knowledge</note>:—(1) Ignorance, (2) Egotism, (3) Attachment, (4) Envy, and (5) Tenacity of life—the five Troubles <note>original: "Kleshas"; the mental states that cause suffering and bind one to the cycle of rebirth</note>. These five

<summary>This page concludes the technical requirements for valid inference and verbal testimony before defining "Misconception" (Viparyaya) as a false mental state corrected by right knowledge. It introduces the five "Troubles" or Kleshas that cloud human perception, including ignorance and attachment.</summary>
