<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>THE PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA.</header>

have also the respective names of: (1) *Darkness* <note>original: "Tamas"</note>, (2) *Illusion* <note>original: "Moha"</note>, (3) *Great Illusion* <note>original: "Mahamoha"</note>, (4) *Darkness of Anger* <note>original: "Tamishra"</note>, and (5) *Blinding Darkness* <note>original: "Andhatamishra"</note>.
This will be defined in connection with the impurities of the internal organ <note>the mind-body complex or 'antahkarana'</note>.

**Notes:** (1) “*Abiding etc.*”—This includes “Doubt.”

(2) “*False notion*”—Serves to set aside “Fancy;” because people ordinarily act professedly in accordance with “Fancy,” but never with “False Notion.”

(3) “*Ignorance etc.*”—Compare the *Verses on Sankhya Philosophy* <note>original: "Sankhya-Karika"</note>.

->“The varieties of Darkness are eightfold, as are those of Illusion; Great Illusion is tenfold.”<-
->“The Darkness of Anger is eighteen-fold, and so also is Blinding Darkness.”<-

---

### Aphorism (9):—**Fancy is a notion founded on a knowledge conveyed by words, but of which there is no object corresponding in reality.**

**Commentary:** This cannot be included either within Right Notion, or within Misconception. Because though it has got no object corresponding in reality, yet its acceptance in usage is seen to be based on the power of the knowledge of words. As for example, the assertion—“Intelligence is the form of the Spirit.” If the Spirit is nothing more than intelligence, then what would be named and qualified by what? <note>The author argues that language often creates a distinction where none exists in reality, such as separating a thing from its own essence.</note> It is only when there is such nomination or qualification that we have the function—for example, “Chaitra’s cow” <note>A standard logical example where the owner and the owned are distinct</note>. Similarly—“The Spirit, having all objective properties alienated from it, is devoid of action;” and—“*Bana* sits, will sit, and sat.” With regard to absence of motion, however, only the meaning of the root is signified. Similarly again, “Spirit has the property of non-production”—the qualifying adjunct here implying merely the negation of production, and not any property belonging to the Spirit: and for this reason, in the example given, the qualification is a fancied one, on which the said usage is based.

<summary>This page details the five types of ignorance or "darkness" and defines "Fancy" (Vikalpa) as a linguistic construct that lacks a physical reality but is necessary for communication.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Sutras, Fancy, Vikalpa, Misconception, Darkness, Tamas, Spirit, Purusha, Sankhya philosophy, linguistics</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical commentary on the Yoga Sutras of Patanjali, specifically focusing on the definition of "Fancy" (Vikalpa) as distinct from other mental states like right knowledge or error.</meta>

# CHAPTER I—VERSE 9.

## Notes:—

(1) "**This cannot etc.**"—This is added in order to guard against the possibility of Fancy <note>Sanskrit: *Vikalpa*; a mental process where words create an image that has no corresponding reality.</note> being included in Testimony <note>Reliable verbal communication.</note>, (because it is based on a knowledge of words), or in Misconception (because it lacks a real object). The two qualifications in the Verse are such that the first distinguishes Fancy from Misconception, and the second from Right Notion <note>Valid knowledge or correct perception.</note>.

(2) "***If the Spirit etc.***"—The meaning of this is: Sometimes a difference is perceived where there is actually identity, and at other times identity is perceived where there is difference. Fancy consists in the appearance of such false difference and identity; therefore, it cannot be called Right Notion. Nor can it be called Misconception, because that would contradict common linguistic usage.

(3) "**What**" **and** "**What etc.**"—The object being described (the qualified), and the description itself (the qualification). There can be no true qualification when the thing being described and the description are identical.

(4) "**We have the function**"—that is, the function in the form of meaning conveyed by a sentence—the verbal signification.

(5) "**Similarly etc.**"—Another example of Fancy.

(6) "**Objective properties**"—Such as mobility and so on, which belong to the earth and other similar external objects.

(7) "**Devoid of action**"—This is called Fancy because, according to the Samkhya <note>One of the six classical schools of Hindu philosophy, emphasizing the dualism between consciousness (Spirit) and matter.</note> school, there can be no property that is merely a negation; as such, a "lack of action" cannot truly be said to be a property belonging to the Spirit <note>Sanskrit: *Purusha*; pure, eternal consciousness.</note>.

(8) "***Bana sits etc.***"—The third example (a popular one). In the sentence "**Bana Sits etc.**", what is implied by '*sits etc.*' is a mere sequence of time, similar to the phrase '*he cooks.*' <note>Bana is likely a proper name used here in a logical example to show how we attribute actions to subjects.</note>

(9) "***With regard to the absence of motion etc.***"—This is said in anticipation of the following objection: "All right, we may accept that the subject '*Bana*' is described by the action of '*sitting*', which is certainly something other than the person Bana himself." The sense of the reply according to Vijnana Bhikshu <note>A 16th-century philosopher who treated Yoga and Samkhya as complementary.</note> is that in the case of the Fancy regarding the "absence of motion," the only real meaning consists in the root of the verb modified by various tenses. The 'nominative agency' <note>The role of the subject performing an action.</note> and 'the tense with reference to the agency' etc. are all imagined; because the "action" of "not moving" cannot truly belong to the subject. Vachaspatimisra <note>A 9th-century philosopher and commentator on multiple schools of Indian thought.</note> however explains it this way: "The absence of the thing itself is assumed first, then follows a whole series of assumptions regarding its positive character and the sequence of time related to it."

(10) "**Similarly etc.**"—This last example is cited with the following fact in view: "The assumed negation, like an affirmation, appears as if it refers to all Spirits; yet it is not a property that exists apart from the Spirit itself."

<summary>This page provides technical commentary on the definition of "Fancy" (Vikalpa) in Yoga philosophy, distinguishing it from valid knowledge and errors by explaining how language creates mental constructs without external reality. It utilizes examples of grammar and the nature of the "Spirit" to show how we mistakenly attribute qualities like "inactivity" to pure consciousness.</summary>
<keywords>Patanjali, Yoga Sutras, Vikalpa, Fancy, Samkhya, Purusha, Spirit, Vijnana Bhikshu, Vachaspatimisra, linguistics, logic, Indian philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the five types of mental fluctuations (vrittis) as defined in the Yoga Sutras, specifically focusing on sleep and memory.</meta>

# YOGA PHILOSOPHY

### **Aphorism (10):—Sleep is the mental activity that rests on the absence of any other content.**

*Commentary:*—And this is a specific type of mental activity, because it is remembered upon waking. How so? In this manner: 
> “(1) I have slept well; my peaceful mind makes my understanding clear. (2) I have slept poorly; my mind was restless and feels dull. (3) I have slept heavily, like a block of wood; my limbs feel heavy, and my exhausted mind is sluggish and feels as if it were missing.” 

This act of remembering by a person upon waking would not occur if there had been no experience of any mental state; the memories associated with sleep could not exist without an object of experience. Therefore, Sleep is a specific mental activity; and this too, like all other mental activities, must be restrained during the practice of deep meditation <note>original: "Samadhi"</note>.

**Notes:**—“*Remembering*”—that is, in all its specific details. These details are illustrated in the following examples.

(2) “*I have slept etc.*”—The three types of sleep described here differ based on which of the three fundamental qualities of nature <term>Gunas: the three forces of balance (Sattva), activity (Rajas), and inertia (Tamas)</term> is dominant during sleep. For example: (1) The first shows a dominance of *Purity* <note>Sattva</note> over *Restlessness* and *Inertia*. (2) The second shows a dominance of *Restlessness* <note>Rajas</note> over *Purity* and *Inertia*. (3) The third shows a dominance of *Inertia* <note>Tamas</note> over the other two.

(3) “*Must be restrained*”—Because it is full of *Inertia* <note>Tamas</note>, it is an obstacle to both types of meditative absorption.

---

### **Aphorism (11):—Memory is the retention of an experienced object without adding anything new.**

*Commentary:*—Does the mind <term>Antahkarana: the internal organ or psyche</term> remember the act of knowing, or the object itself? The act of knowing—which is shaped by the object known and reveals the forms of both the object and the knowledge itself—leaves behind a mental impression <term>Sanskara: a latent tendency or "groove" in the mind created by experience</term> of that same nature. This impression, being identical in form to its cause, triggers a memory of the same nature, containing both the object and the original act of knowing it.

<summary>This page defines sleep as a distinct mental state characterized by the absence of other thoughts and explains memory as the preservation of past experiences through mental impressions.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Sutras, Sleep, Memory, Gunas, Sattva, Rajas, Tamas, Sanskara, mental fluctuations, meditation</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Yoga Sutras, specifically focusing on the definition and nature of Memory (Smriti) as the fifth mental modification.</meta>

# CHAPTER I—SUTRA 11.

Of these, the cognition has the form of the act of knowing as its principal factor; and Memory or Recollection has the form of the object known as its precedent.

This *Memory* is two-fold—(1) One whose object to be remembered is *assumed* <note>imaginary or constructed during sleep</note>, and (2) the other whose object is *not assumed* <note>based on actual past experience</note>. During a dream we have the former, whereas in the waking state, we have the latter.

All kinds of Memory owe their existence to the prior experience of Right Notion, Misconception, Fancy, Sleep and Memory.

All these functions are constituted by pleasure, pain and illusion. These three will be explained under ‘*Afflictions*’ <note>original: "Klesa"; these are the mental states that cause suffering</note>. Attachment belongs to pleasure and envy to pain; while delusion is ignorance. All these functions are to be suppressed—their suppression leading to one of the two sorts of Meditation.

**Notes:—**(1) Memory differs from the preceding functions in this that the aforesaid functions lead to the cognition of strong and unseen objects, whereas Memory can never go beyond the field of past experience.

(2) “*Of the same character*”—*i. e.* Partaking of the character of the *object known* as well as the *act of knowing*.

(3) “*Of these, the cognition &c.*”—*Grasping* <note>original: "Grahana"; literally holding or accepting</note> and certainly no holding is possible of an object already held; consequently the characteristic of the cognition here noted, implies the province of cognition (*notion* or *Knowledge* <note>original: "pratyaya" or "Jnana"</note>) to be restricted to strange and unseen objects. The use of the word *Intellect* <note>original: "Buddhi"</note> is here ambiguous. Here it = *Knowledge* <note>original: "Jnâna"</note>; and not the *internal organ* <note>original: "antahkarana"</note>, as is usual in this philosophy.

(4) “*Memory has got &c.*”—This precedence of the cognised object is the same as the fact of its having already been the object of some foregoing function. Thus then the definition of *Memory* comes to be the function having for its object something that has already been the object of a foregoing function. And this is the “non-relinquishment” (of an object already cognised by a foregoing function) mentioned in the aphorism.

(5) “*This Memory is two-fold*”—This is added in order to meet the assertion that in a dream memory is found to be touching unknown regions. The sense of the reply is that what we find in a dream is not real recollection but only a semblance thereof.

(6) “*Assumed*”—*i. e.* not really existing.

<summary>This page defines Memory as a mental function that relies on previous experiences and distinguishes between real memories and the "assumed" memories found in dreams. It also introduces the concept that all mental functions are tied to pleasure, pain, or ignorance, which must be suppressed for meditation.</summary>
<keywords>Memory, Recollection, Afflictions, Klesa, Cognition, Knowledge, Dreams, Meditation, Yoga Sutras</keywords>

### What is the means for the suppression of these? <note>Referring to the mental fluctuations or "vrittis" mentioned in previous sections.</note>

## *Aphorism (12):*—**By practice and detachment, their suppression is achieved.**

*Commentary:*—The river of the internal organ <note>The "internal organ" or *Antahkarana* refers to the collective functions of the mind, intellect, and ego.</note> flows in two directions. It flows toward good, and it flows toward evil. That which leads toward Spiritual Liberation <note>original: "Kaivalya" or Isolation of the soul from matter</note> and is grounded in Discernment <note>The ability to distinguish between the eternal self and the temporary world.</note> flows toward good. Whereas the one leading toward Rebirth and grounded in Lack of Discernment flows toward evil. Among these, the flow toward worldly objects is weakened by Detachment; and the flow of discernment is opened by the Practice of visionary insight. Thus we see that the suppression of the functions of the internal organ is dependent upon both of these.

*Notes:*—" *Having its mouth etc.*"—meaning, having its destination in Spiritual Liberation and its current flowing through the realization of the distinction between Spirit and Matter.

(2) " *Weakened.*"—Obstructed or stopped.

---

## *Aphorism (13):*—**Practice is the effort toward steadiness.**

*Commentary:*—"Steadiness" (*Sthiti*) is the calm flow of the internal organ when it is clear of its turbulent fluctuations. And Practice consists in an effort toward this—that is, an undaunted, *courageous*, and spirited endeavor, consisting in acting according to the means of attaining the aforementioned calmness.

*Notes:*—(1) " *Clear of its functionings*"—meaning, free from the qualities of Passion and Ignorance <note>original: "Rajas" and "Tamas"</note>. This is added by the commentator in consideration of Focused Meditation <note>original: "Samprajnata Samadhi"</note> which is filled with the quality of Purity and Light <note>original: "Sattva"</note>.

(2) " *Acting according to the means*"—The means refer to the Eight Limbs of Yoga, such as Moral Restraint and the others to be described in Section II.

---

## *Aphorism (14):*—**But this practice becomes firmly grounded when cultivated for a long time, without interruption, and with earnest devotion.**

<summary>This page explains that mental mastery is achieved through the dual pillars of persistent practice and emotional detachment, likening the mind to a river that must be diverted from worldly desires toward spiritual liberation. It defines practice as a courageous, long-term commitment to achieving a state of inner steadiness and purity.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Sutras, mental discipline, detachment, discernment, spiritual liberation, meditation, internal organ, steadiness, Patanjali</keywords>

***Commentary:—***Being attended to for a long time, and unremittingly—and brought about by penance, celibacy, knowledge and faith, and as such endowed with proper devotion—it becomes steadfast; that is to say, its object is not then easily suppressed by the impressions of the agitative stage <note>The "agitative stage" refers to *Vyutthāna*, the state of mind where thoughts are scattered and restless, as opposed to the focused state of Yoga.</note>.

***Notes:—***( 1 ) The “but” of the aphorism points to the following difficulty in the mind of the author: Exercise being bound up with its eternal contradictory, the impressions of the Agitation stage, how can it lead to calmness? In reply, the aphorism lays down the steady character of exercise when accompanied by the qualifications herein enumerated.

## *Aphorism ( 15 ):—Dispassion is the <term>Vaśikárasanjná</term> <note>Literally: "The consciousness of mastery or subjugation."</note> Consciousness of being the subjugator, belonging to one who is devoid of any thirst for perceptible and scriptural objects.*

***Commentary:—***When one becomes devoid of any desire for the temporal perceptible objects—women, food, drink, and wealth—and for the scriptural objects—the attainment of heaven, dis-embodiment, dissolution in Nature—then his internal organ <note>The *Antahkarana*, or the collective functions of the mind, intellect, and ego.</note>, noticing the objective discrepancies even in the relation of celestial and terrestrial objects, attains to the name of <term>Vaśikárasanjná</term> which, on the strength of the recognition of discrepancies, is of the nature of <term>Anābhoga</term> <note>original: "अनाभोग" — meaning a state of effortless indifference or lack of enjoyment in worldly objects</note>, *i.e.*, free from all faults of attachment. (And this <term>Vaśikárasanjná</term> or consciousness of being the subjugator, is Dispassion.)

***Notes:—***(1) The internal organ, noticing the discrepancies, becomes indifferent to the various objects of sense, and to this indifference is given the name of <term>Vaśikarasajnà</term>.

(2) “Discrepancies of objects”—*i.e.*, the fact of their being subject to the three kinds of pain <note>In Yoga philosophy, all worldly experiences are ultimately considered painful due to their impermanence, the anxiety of losing them, and the conflict of the natural qualities (gunas).</note>.

The other kinds of Dispassion will be described later on.

---

## *Aphorism ( 16 ):—Indifference to the attributes <note>The *Gunas* or the three fundamental qualities of nature: lucidity, activity, and inertia.</note>, being conducive to the knowledge of the Spirit, is the highest (form of it).*

<summary>This page defines the nature of spiritual exercise and the first stages of dispassion, explaining how a practitioner gains mastery over desires for both worldly pleasures and heavenly rewards. It distinguishes between lower dispassion toward objects and the highest dispassion, which is indifference to the fundamental qualities of nature itself.</summary>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the methods of attaining spiritual liberation, specifically focusing on the definition and stages of Dispassion as outlined in the Yoga-Sutras.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
# THE PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA

**Commentary:—(1)** The practitioner who recognizes the flaws in both worldly and spiritual objects, and thus becomes dispassionate, and **(2)** one whose internal mind has been calmed by supreme discernment—resulting from the purity of spiritual practice—and who has therefore become detached from the qualities <note>original: "gunas"</note> that make up the visible and invisible forms of matter;—these two represent the two types of Dispassion. Of these, the latter is the pure radiance of wisdom. Upon its appearance, the practitioner, now filled with knowledge, reflects as follows:

> “I have reached what I was meant to reach; the obstacles to be destroyed—the afflictions—have been destroyed; the tightly woven chain of the cycle of rebirth <note>original: "metempsychosis"</note> is cut off, that unbroken cycle which causes the death of the living and the birth of the dead.”

Dispassion is the highest stage of knowledge; and the inevitable result of this state alone is Absolute Liberation <note>term: Isolation (Kaivalya) — the state of the soul being completely detached from matter</note>.

**Notes: (1)** “*Purity*”—Freedom from the qualities of Passion <note>original: "Rajas" or Foulness</note> and Ignorance <note>original: "Tamas" or Darkness</note>.

**(2)** “*One whose internal organ etc.*”—This state of qualification is technically known as the “Cloud of Virtue” <note>term: Cloud of Virtue (Dharma-Megha-Samadhi) — the highest level of meditation where the yogi is showered with spiritual merit</note>.

**(3)** “*Two kinds of Dispassion.*”—The first form occurs when the internal mind is still slightly stained by a trace of Passion, even though all Ignorance has been washed away by an abundance of Purity <note>original: "Sattva" or Goodness</note>. This state can be reached even by the Contented Ones <note>original: "Taushtikas" — those who stop at the nine forms of spiritual satisfaction</note>, who thereby achieve “dissolution into nature.” [*Compare the Sankhya Verses: “From dispassion comes dissolution into nature, etc.”*]

**(4)** “*Sole effulgence of wisdom*”—The word “sole” excludes all external objects from this state of Dispassion. This second form is free from even the smallest stain of Passion, and thus remains gloriously in pure Purity. This is why it is called “Radiance.” Although radiance is the true nature of the internal mind, it becomes hidden when the mind is filled with the impurities of Passion and Ignorance. However, when these impurities are washed away, there is full radiance, through and through.

**(5)** “*Equipped with knowledge*”—Remaining in the meditation of the “cloud of virtue” upon reaching the spiritual radiance mentioned above.

<summary>This page defines two levels of Dispassion (Vairagya), explaining how the highest stage leads to the "Cloud of Virtue" and ultimately to the soul's absolute liberation from the cycle of rebirth.</summary>
<keywords>Dispassion, Vairagya, Rebirth, Isolation, Kaivalya, Cloud of Virtue, Purity, Sattva, Passion, Rajas, Ignorance, Tamas, Yoga Philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of the Yoga Sutras, specifically detailing the four stages of Conscious Meditation (Samprajnata Samadhi) following the discussion on practice and detachment from the previous pages.</meta>

# CHAPTER I—SUTRA 17.

(6) “ *What I had to attain* ”—that is, <term>Isolation</term> <note>Sanskrit: Kaivalya; the ultimate state of liberation where the soul is freed from matter</note>; because under these circumstances, there is only the mental impression remaining, now deprived of its worldly root.

(7) “ *The objects to be destroyed etc.* ”—The following assertions provide the reasoning for the preceding declaration. *See* Chapter II, verses 12 and 13, and Chapter IV, verses 29 and 31.

---

*Question*:— “When the practitioner has suppressed his internal organ <note>The 'antahkarana' or the collective functions of the mind, intellect, and ego</note> through the two means just described <note>Practice and detachment</note>, how can the meditation be said to be *Conscious*?”

( *Answer* ) :—

**Sutra ( 17 )**:—**Conscious**, because it is accompanied by the forms of argumentation, deliberation, joy, and egoism.

*Commentary*:—(1) “Argumentation” is the gross perception of the <term>internal organ</term> with regard to its object; whereas (2) “Deliberation” is subtle in its nature. (3) “Joy” is a state of delight. (4) “Egoism” is the partial cognition of identity <note>The sense of 'I am' without further worldly association</note>.

Of these, the first stage, followed by the other four, constitutes Argumentative Meditation; the second stage, which moves beyond Argumentation, is Deliberative; the third, moving beyond Deliberation, is Joyful. The fourth, moving beyond even this, is purely Egoistic. All these forms of meditation are considered "concrete" or "with seed" because they still rely on an object of focus.

*Notes*:—(1) “ *Gross perception* ”—the “perception” is called “gross” because the objects of focus are physical or external. Such objects include the Gods contemplated as having four arms, wearing yellow cloth, and so on.

(2) “ *Subtle* ”—that is, Deliberation takes as its objects the subtle primary elements <note>The 'tanmatras' or the essence of sound, touch, sight, taste, and smell</note> and other non-physical principles.

->( *The first two stages refer to the perceived object* )<-

(3) “ *Joy* ”—refers to the *instrument* of perception (the senses). The perception of the internal organ with regard to the gross senses constitutes “Joy.” The senses are the products of <term>Self-consciousness</term> in which the attribute of Goodness <note>Sattva: the quality of light, purity, and peace</note> predominates; and goodness is inherently pleasant; therefore the senses are also pleasing; consequently, the perception achieved by means of these is “Joyous.”

(4) “ *Cognition of partial identity* ”—this describes meditation with regard to the perceiver themselves.

<summary>This page defines the four stages of Conscious Meditation (Samprajnata Samadhi), explaining how the mind moves from focusing on gross physical objects to subtle essences, then to the joy of the senses, and finally to the pure sense of 'I'. It clarifies that these stages are "concrete" because they still involve a mental focus or support.</summary>
<keywords>Conscious Meditation, Samprajnata Samadhi, Isolation, Kaivalya, Argumentation, Deliberation, Joy, Egoism, Internal Organ, Sattva, Yoga Sutras</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of the Yoga Sutras, specifically moving from conscious meditation to the higher state of unconscious or abstract meditation.</meta>

# YOGA PHILOSOPHY <note>original: "Yoga-Darasan"</note>

Now, what is the nature of **Unconscious** or **Abstract** meditation <note>The state of Asamprajnata Samadhi, where the mind is still but no longer focused on a specific object</note> and what are the means to it?

Reply:—

***Sutra (18):—The other form of meditation, reached through the constant practice of the cause of suspension, is that in which only the mental impressions remain.***

***Commentary:***—When all the functions of the mind have been suspended and only the latent impressions <note>residua</note> remain behind, then we have a suppression of the internal organ <note>the mind-stuff or Chitta</note>, which constitutes a form of meditation called the *Unconscious* or *Abstract*.

The means to achieve this is the highest form of Dispassion <note>Renunciation of even the most subtle desires for knowledge or existence</note>. Because an exercise based on a physical or mental object would not be capable of achieving this state, an objectless or immaterial cause of suspension is used as the basis here; this cause is entirely devoid of any material object. Consequently, the internal organ, through the practice of this objectless focus, becomes immaterial, appearing as if it does not exist. This Immaterial or "seedless" meditation is what is called the Unconscious or the Abstract.

**Notes: (1) "Suspension"**—meaning the halting of the mind's active functions.

**(2) "Preceded by"**—Meaning caused or brought about by a specific practice.

**(3) "And because etc."**—This is added to show why lower forms of Dispassion cannot cause Unconscious Meditation. As a rule, a cause must be of the same nature as its effect; therefore, we cannot claim that a material form of Dispassion causes immaterial meditation. Such a state can only arise from the immaterial radiance of wisdom. Therefore, the cause of this Immaterial Abstract Meditation is the "Cloud of Virtue" meditation <note>Dharma-Megha-Samadhi</note>. This state exists through pure Goodness <note>Sattva</note> following the complete destruction of the impurities of Foulness <note>Rajas</note> and Darkness <note>Tamas</note>. It is immaterial because it rejects all material objects and rests within itself.

**(4) "As if non-existing"**—The mind appears non-existent because it no longer produces its usual effects or functions.

**(5) "Seedless"**—This refers to a state beyond the reach of "seeds," which are the troubles, actions, and desires that bind a soul. This is the literal meaning provided by the scholar Vachaspati Misra; the functional meaning is that no "seeds" of future rebirth remain.

<summary>This page defines "Unconscious" or "Seedless" meditation as a state where the mind's active functions cease, leaving only latent impressions. It explains that this state is achieved through the highest form of dispassion and the "Cloud of Virtue" meditation, which transcends material objects.</summary>

<keywords>Asamprajnata Samadhi, Abstract meditation, Seedless meditation, Dispassion, Vachaspati Misra, Cloud of Virtue, Sattva, Rajas, Tamas, Yoga Sutras</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the types of Samadhi (meditative absorption), specifically distinguishing between those born into higher states and those who achieve them through disciplined practice.</meta>

<header>CHAPTER I—SUTRA 20.</header>

This (meditation) is of two kinds—The ***World-caused*** <note>original: "Bhavapratyaya"; refers to a state of absorption arising from birth or nature</note> and the ***Means-caused*** <note>original: "Upáyápratyaya"; refers to absorption achieved through specific yogic techniques</note>. Of these the latter belongs to the Yogis.

### Sutra (19):—The *world-caused* belongs to the Disembodied and to the Resolved-into-Nature.

**Commentary:—**To the Disembodied—*that is* the celestial beings—belongs the World-caused absorption. These (beings),—experiencing a state similar to liberation <note>original: "isolation" or Kaivalya</note> by means of their internal organs <term>Antahkarana: the mind, intellect, and ego acting together</term> aided by their subconscious impressions <term>Residua/Samskaras: the latent traces left in the mind by past actions</term> alone,—carry over the residual results which are of the same nature. Similarly, the "Resolved-into-Nature" <note>those whose minds have merged back into the primal material cause of the universe</note> experience a state similar to liberation upon the dissolution of the internal organ into Nature while it is still fully active; (and this experience continues only) so long as the internal organ, by its inherent power, does not return (to its worldly work).

**Notes: (1)** "*Caused by the means*" *that is* by the methods prescribed in the *Sacred Scriptures* <note>original: "S'ástras"</note>.

**(2)** "*Disembodied*"—Thus described by Váchaspati Misra <note>A 9th-century philosopher and famous commentator on the Yoga Sutras</note>: 'Thinking either one of the elements or one of the senses to be their spirit, contemplating on these, and hence having their internal organs colored by a desire for these, and consequently on death, dissolving either into the senses or the (subtle Elements), their internal organs consisting only of the subconscious impressions, and finally devoid of the six-sheathed (material) body'.

**(3)** "*Experiencing isolation as it were by means etc.*"—the similarity between true liberation and the state of the internal organ of the Disembodied depends only on the fact of the latter being temporarily without its functions; the differences consist in the fact of the internal organ still being active and carrying a balance of former subconscious impressions.

**(4)** "*Carry over Etc.*"—*that is* they are born again.

**(5)** "*In full activity*"—*that is* having its purposes unfulfilled. The purposes of the internal organ would be fulfilled only on the achievement of discriminative knowledge <note>The ability to distinguish between the pure Self and the material world</note>.

---

### Sutra (20):—Of others, preceded by faith, energy, memory, meditation and discrimination.

**Commentary:—**To the yogis belongs the Means-caused absorption. "Faith" is the confidence of the mind, like the loving...

<summary>This page distinguishes between 'World-caused' meditation, which is a temporary state for celestial beings, and 'Means-caused' meditation, which is the disciplined path of the Yogi. It explains that even high celestial states are not true liberation if subconscious impressions remain.</summary>

<page-num>22</page-num>
-># THE PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA<-

mother, sustains the **Yogi** <note>A practitioner of Yoga</note>. "**Energy**" accrues to an agent endued with faith and desirous of discrimination; and on the energetic attends "**Memory**"; on this the mind rests peacefully in contemplation; and to one of calm mind comes **Discrimination**; by means of which he comes to recognise things in their true colours. By constant practice of this, and by means of Dispassion with regard thereto, is brought about **Unconscious** or **Abstract Meditation** <note>original: "Asamprajnata Samadhi," a state of deep absorption where the distinction between the observer and the object disappears</note>.

### Notes
(1) "**Faith**" *etc.*—Confidence in the senses *etc.* are not faith because it is based on illusion, hence it is specified "**of the mind**".

(2) "**Sustains**"—that is, keeps him from erring paths and keeps him fixed to the one true path of salvation.

(3) "**Memory**"—is explained by Vachaspati Misra <note>A 9th-century Indian philosopher who wrote influential commentaries on Yoga and Vedanta</note> as '**Contemplation**' <note>original: "Dhyana"</note>.

(4) "**Rests etc.**"—this also implies the various stages of Meditation Restraint and the rest to be described hereafter.

(5) "**Discrimination**"—so far we have the ingredients of concrete meditation. The meditation following this is Abstract meditation.

(6) "**Is brought about etc.**"—suppression following on the discriminative knowledge of the **Spirit** <note>original: "Purusha," the true, eternal self</note> puts a stop to the activity of the **internal organ** <note>original: "Antahkarana," the mind-body complex</note>, whose ends have been all fulfilled by that time.

---

The nine *yogis* are such as have their means mild, moderate and excessive. For example: (1) One of mild means, (2) Of moderate means, and (3) Of excessive means; of these again the first is of three kinds: (1) The mildly—dispassionate, (2) the moderately—dispassionate and (3) the ardently—dispassionate. There are similar divisions of the moderate—means and the excessive—means.

From among these, for those of excessive means (is laid down).—

**Aphorism (21):**—For the ardently—dispassionate, success is proximate.

**Commentary:**—is the accomplishment of meditation, as well as its end.

It emphasizes that those with the highest intensity of "excessive means" achieve the goals of Yoga most quickly.</summary>

# CHAPTER I—SUTRA 23.

**Notes: (1)—"Mild means etc."—These "means" refer to Faith and the other qualities just described.**

**(2) “Mildly dispassionate”—Vachaspati Misra <note>A 10th-century philosopher and commentator on Indian systems of thought</note> explains “<term>Samvega</term>” <note>often meaning momentum or intensity</note> as “<term>Vairagya</term>” <note>dispassion or detachment</note>. “Dispassion”—Though Bhojadeva and Vijnana Bhikshu <note>later influential commentators on Yoga philosophy</note> both explain it as “energetic exertion” or “impetuosity”—The mildness or the excessiveness of these is due to causes from previous lives.**

**(3) “Its end”—The state of abstract meditation <note>Samadhi without an object</note> as the result of concrete meditation <note>Samadhi with an object</note>, and ultimate Liberation as the result of the former.**

---

## ***Sutra* (22): —A further distinction, on account of the mild, the moderate, and the excessive.**

*Commentary*: —There is a distinction further than this, such as: (1) The mildly-ardent, (2) the moderately-ardent, and (3) the excessively-ardent. On account of this distinction, the accomplishment of meditation and its goals is near to one of excessive means when they are mildly-ardently-dispassionate, nearer to them when they are moderately-ardently-dispassionate, and nearest to them when they are excessively-ardently-dispassionate.

*Question*:—“Is the extreme proximity of meditation due to this (above mentioned) cause alone? Or is there any other means to its attainment?”

*Answer*:—

## ***Sutra* (23): —Or by devotion to God.**

*Commentary*: —The Supreme God, being attracted by the devotion (of the *<term>yogi</term>* <note>a practitioner of yoga</note>) favors them by mere prescience <note>divine foreknowledge or will</note>. And from this prescience also comes to the practitioner the accomplishment of meditation and its goals.

**Notes: (1) “Attracted”—brought face to face.**

**(2) “Prescience” in the form:—“May the devoted practitioner attain to their desired end”—Such is the form of the prescient favor as explained by Vachaspati Misra.**

**(3) “Mere prescience”—That is, by His sheer will without any form of physical exertion.**

<summary>This page discusses how the speed of reaching spiritual liberation depends on the intensity of a practitioner's effort and introduces devotion to God (Ishvara) as an alternative, direct path to success.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical inquiry into the nature of the Divine within the Yoga system, specifically defining the unique status of God (Ishvara) in relation to the human soul and material nature.</meta>

# YOGA-PHILOSOPHY <note>original: "Yoga-Darsana"</note>

Question:—"Who is this God, distinct from Nature and Spirit?"

Answer:—

**Aphorism (24):—God is a distinct spirit, untouched by afflictions, actions, fruits of karma, and subconscious traces.** <note>original: "Sutra (24)"</note>

*Commentary*: "Afflictions"—meaning ignorance and the other obstacles to liberation. "Actions," both good and evil—the results of these are the "Fruits of Karma" <note>original: "Deserts"</note>—and the desires that follow these results are the "Subconscious Traces" <note>original: "Impressions"</note>; though these reside in the internal organ <note>the mind or 'antahkarana'</note>, they are attributed to the Spirit, who experiences their results; just as defeat or victory, while actually belonging to the soldiers, is attributed to their leader. That particular Spirit, who is never touched by such experiences, is God.

Though there are many master-adepts who have attained to Absolute Liberation <note>original: "Isolation" or 'Kaivalya'</note>—yet such individuals have attained this state only after cutting off the three bondages; whereas the connection of God with these bondages has never existed, nor will it ever exist. We do not know of any previous state of bondage for God, as we do for the emancipated souls; nor is any future bondage possible for God, as it is for those "Merged-in-nature" <note>beings who have reached a high state but are still subject to rebirth</note>. He is eternally Liberated and eternally Supreme.

*(Question)*: "This eternal supremacy of God, which is due to supreme goodness—is there any proof for it, or is it incapable of proof?"

*(Answer)*: Its proof lies in the Sacred Scriptures.

*(Question)*: What is the proof for the authority of these Scriptures?

<margin>Beta <note>The Greek letter β likely marking a specific point in the argument or a cross-reference.</note></margin>
*(Answer)*: It lies in their supreme goodness. The connection between these two—the Scripture and its supreme character, residing as they do in God's Intellect <note>original: "Buddhi"</note>—is eternal. From Scripture, the conclusion is this: that God is forever the Lord and forever emancipated.

And this supremacy of His is free from equality or excess, because it is never exceeded by any other supremacy; whatever should exceed it, would become *that* very supremacy itself. Hence, wherever there is the final, highest stage of supremacy, that is God.

<summary>This page defines God (Ishvara) as a unique Spirit eternally free from the karmic cycles of action and suffering that affect human souls. It argues that God's supremacy is absolute, proven by Scripture, and represents the ultimate limit of excellence.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Sutras, Ishvara, Karma, Kaivalya, Buddhi, Divine Supremacy, liberation, spiritual philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the nature of God (Ishvara) as defined in the Yoga Sutras, specifically addressing the uniqueness of His supremacy and the validity of sacred scripture.</meta>

# CHAPTER I—SUTRA 24.

->Nor is there any supremacy equal to it.<-

Because when there are two things equally supreme (contending) with regard to a desired object, (one saying) “may this be new” and (the other contending) “may this be old”—then (in such a case) only one can be fulfilled, and as such there would result the resistance of the will of another, which would imply the lesser character of his supremacy. And further, to two equal persons cannot belong the simultaneous accomplishment of the desired object, —the objects being contradictory. Therefore He whose supremacy is free from equality and excess, is God; and He is a distinct Spirit.

### Notes:

(1) "**Though there are many master-adepts etc.**"—The objection here met is based on the following Sutras of Kapila <note>Kapila is the traditional founder of the Samkhya school of philosophy</note>,—I, 92, V, 1—12. (See Rajendralal Mitra’s Yoga Sutras).

(2) "**The three bondages**"—(1) The natural bondage of those absorbed into nature, (2) The modificatory bondage of the disembodied, and (3) The "right-hand" <note>referring to traditional or ritualistic</note> bondage of the celestial beings.

(3) "**Lies in supreme goodness**"—The Lord has recourse to supreme goodness for the following reasons: God has no possessive relation with the goodness of the internal organ which is due to Ignorance; He only desires to save the three-fold-bound spirits from the cycle of death, by instructions with regard to wisdom and virtue; but such instructions are not possible without the assistance of wisdom and action, which latter is not possible without having recourse to Goodness <note>original: "Sattva"</note> washed clear of all taint of Foulness <note>original: "Rajas"</note> and Darkness <note>original: "Tamas"</note>.

This is the purity that is implied by the epithet “supreme” (**<term>Prakrishta</term>** <note>meaning "excellent" or "manifested in a high degree"</note>). One is said to be subject to Ignorance when he does not recognize it as such. One however who recognizes Ignorance as ignorance and governs his actions accordingly, cannot be said to be *subject* to ignorance.

(4) "**Eternal Supremacy**"—This epithet is added to meet the objection of mutual subserviency; which is said to be no fault, when in connection with two Eternal Entities.

(5) "**What is the proof for this scripture?**"—Perceiving the infallibility of the <term>Mantra-Shastra</term> <note>The science or scripture of sacred chants and spells</note> and the medical science, we must acknowledge the force of God’s intellect, clear of all taint of Foulness and Darkness, as shining everywhere. Similarly the Scripture, consisting of instructions for the highest bliss, and owing its compilation to God, must be attributed to the excellence of his Goodness. And when Goodness reigns supreme, there is no room for doubt and deception. Thus then the validity of the scripture is based upon the excellence and supremacy of Goodness. (*compare* **<term>Nyayasutra</term>** <note>The foundational text of the Nyaya school of Indian logic</note>—II—i—53 Et. Seq.)

(6) "**The connection between these two etc.**"—This meets the objection that the Scripture, being an effect of excellence and as such leading to the inference

<sig>4</sig>

The reply means, that the Scripture indicates excellence, not by its character of an effect, but by means of the eternal relation of the denoter and the denoted. That is to say the excellence resides in God's <term>Intellect<note>original: "Buddhi"; the faculty of discernment and higher consciousness</note></term>, and the Scripture too, being denotative of it, resides therein.

( 7 ) " It comes to this "—Scripture, implying the excellence of God's <term>Intellect</term> leads to the following conclusion.

And further,—

### **Aphorism (25):—In Him is the highest limit of the seed of omniscience.**

**Commentary:**—The seed of omniscience is the cognition of the smallness <note>parvitude</note> and largeness <note>magnitude</note> of the perception of objects beyond the reach of the senses <note>supersensorious</note>, whether past, present, or future, individually as well as collectively. And that person is omniscient in whom this seed has grown to its utmost extent. There is a gradual progress of the seed of omniscience because it has various degrees, like <term>Dimension<note>original: "parimána"; the scale or measure of an object</note></term>. That person in whom wisdom attains its limit, is omniscient; and this is a particular Spirit.

The above inference, ending in only a general conclusion, is not capable of denoting any particular being; consequently the knowledge of particular names is to be looked for in the Scripture.

For God, though there is no selfish motive, yet there is the motive of mercy for the creatures,—( being of the form ) “By means of instructions in wisdom and virtue, I shall rescue all spirits caught in the cycle of rebirth <note>transmigratory spirits</note>, at the periodical and the final dissolution”. As is declared: “The first Knower, Lord and Great Sage, with a view to creation, in his mercy, taught the Science to the enquiring <term>Asuri<note>A prominent disciple in the early Samkhya tradition</note></term>”.

Notes: (1) “ *The above inference etc.* ”:—This assertion precludes the possibility of the name ‘God’ being applied to the various masters of the various philosophies—e.g. Buddha etc.

(2) “ *Particular names* ”:—Such as <term>Siva<note>The Auspicious One; a primary deity in Hinduism</note></term>, <term>Sakti<note>The Divine Energy or Power</note></term> etc.

(3) “ *As is declared etc.* ”:—by <term>Panchasikha<note>An ancient teacher of the Samkhya-Yoga school</note></term>, The “Great Sage” is <term>Kapila<note>The traditional founder of the Samkhya philosophy</note></term> an incarnation of <term>Vishnu<note>The Preserver deity in the Hindu trinity</note></term>.

<summary>This page explains that God's omniscience is the ultimate limit of knowledge and that His motivation for teaching sacred wisdom is pure compassion for beings trapped in the cycle of rebirth. It identifies the "Great Sage" Kapila as the first teacher of this divine science.</summary>

# CHAPTER I—APHORISM 27.

And such a one is—

**Aphorism (26):—the Teacher of even the earliest teachers, because he is not limited by time.**

*Commentary:*—The early great teachers <note>original: "great ones," referring to the first created beings</note> are all limited by time. The One for whom time does not act as a limitation is the instructor and therefore the greatest of even the earliest ones; and as the existence of such a Being is established in this current world-cycle by His superiority, so should it be understood with reference to all past cycles as well.

**Notes: (1) "*The early great ones*":—for example, Brahma, Vishnu, etc.** <note>Brahma and Vishnu are primary deities in the Hindu trinity, here described as being subject to the cycles of time unlike the Supreme Ishvara.</note>

**This aphorism serves to distinguish the supreme God from His earliest manifestations, Brahma, Vishnu, and others.**

**Aphorism (27).—His signifier is the *Pranava*.** <note>The *Pranava* refers to the sacred syllable 'Om'.</note>

*Commentary:*—God is the object signified by the *Pranava*.

> (Question): "Is the meaning of this word based on human agreement, or is it inherent in the word itself, like the light is inherent in a lamp?"

> (Reply): "The relationship between this signified Being (God) and the signifier (the syllable *Om*) is inherent. The convention established by God only points to objects that already exist; just as, for example, the natural relationship between a father and son is expressed by the convention: 'This is the father, and that is his son.' In other world-cycles as well, the convention is always dependent upon the inherent powers of the signifier and the signified. The masters of scripture declare the relationship between word and meaning to be eternal because of the eternal nature of universal agreement in usage."

**Notes: (1) "*In other cycles too etc*"—This is added to answer the following objection: 'Since a word is an effect of Nature, it is dissolved back into its cause during the Great Dissolution; therefore, when the universe is created again, there would be no previous *power* of meaning left to base new names upon.' The reply explains that although the word and its *power* are resolved into their cause, when the word is brought forth again, the *power* inherent in it returns with it. It is in accordance with the previous relationship between the word and its meaning that God establishes the names for the new cycle.**

**(2) "*Agreement*"—referring to long-standing, traditional usage.**

For the practitioner of Yoga <term>Yogi: one who practices the discipline of mental and spiritual concentration</term> who has recognized the character of the signifier and the signified—

<summary>This page explains that the Supreme Being is the eternal teacher of all, transcending time and the primary deities, and identifies the sacred syllable 'Om' as His inherent, eternal name.</summary>

### **Sutra (28)** Its repetition, and the contemplation of its meaning.

**Commentary:** The repetition of the <term>Pranava</term> <note>The sacred syllable 'Om'</note> and the contemplation of its meaning—God. In this manner, the internal organ <note>The mind or 'antahkarana'</note> of the <term>yogi</term> <note>A practitioner of Yoga</note>, who repeats the <term>Pranava</term> and reflects on its meaning, becomes concentrated; as is declared: 
> “After repetition, one should have recourse to meditation; and after meditation, again he should take to repetition. Through perfection in repetition and meditation the supreme spirit lightens.” (Vishnu Purana)

**Notes:** (1) “*Becomes concentrated*”—and then follows the direct perception of the supreme spirit; and hence the higher dispassion, and finally, <term>Abstract meditation</term> <note>Asamprajnata Samadhi, the highest state of cognitive absorption</note>.

(2) “*After repetition*” of the <term>Pranava</term>.

What more accrues to him?

### **Sutra (29):—** Hence the cognition of “reversed perception” and absence of obstacles.

**Commentary:—** All the obstacles—disease and the rest—cease to exist, by virtue of devotion to God. And to him belongs the perception of his real character. 
> “As God is a Spirit, pure, blissful, isolated and free from troubles,—so also is this spirit, the counter-cogniser of <term>Buddhi</term> <note>The higher intellect or discerning faculty</note>”—such being the recognition.

**Notes:—** (1) “*Reversed perception etc.*”—<term>Vachaspati Misra</term> <note>A 9th-century philosopher and commentator</note> explains this as the Spirit subject to illusion; and interprets the aphorism thus: Thence accrues to him the cognition of the real character of the spirit of contradictory perception (i.e., the spirit under illusion). And this is quite in keeping with the <term>Bhashya</term> <note>The primary commentary, usually attributed to Vyasa</note> which explains as “*To him accrues the perception of his real character*” <note>original: "Swarupadarsanamapyasya bhavati"</note>. The interpretation of the <term>Bhojavritti</term> <note>The commentary by King Bhoja</note> is very subtle, very inviting; and I quote the explanatory note of Dr. <term>Rajendralal Mitra</term> <note>A prominent 19th-century Indian scholar</note>: 
> “The natural function of the senses is to extend outwards in order to receive the impressions of external objects and carry them to the sensorium, but that being suppressed by the Yogi, the senses turn inwards and find their object within, and therefore the function is called ‘reversed’ or reflex. The object of this roundabout way of description is to say that the senses hold communion exclusively with the soul.”

The final upshot of both the interpretations is the same, the only difference lying in the interpretation of the epithet *pratyak* <note>meaning 'inward' or 'opposite'</note>, the *Vachaspatya* <note>The school or work of Vachaspati</note> making it—contradictory.

<summary>This page discusses the spiritual benefits of repeating the sacred syllable Om and meditating on its meaning, which leads to the removal of obstacles and the realization of the soul's true nature. It contrasts different scholarly interpretations of "reversed perception," explaining it as either the soul's inward turn or its liberation from illusion.</summary>

<page-num>29</page-num>
->PADA I—SUTRA 30.<-

knower (subject to illusion); and the <term>Bhojavritti</term> <note>A famous 11th-century commentary on the Yoga Sutras by King Bhoja</note>, explaining it as “turned back” (inwards instead of outwards). Hence the words “reversed perception” have been placed within inverted commas in the <term>Sutra</term> <note>The foundational aphorisms of Yoga philosophy</note>—thus admitting of both interpretations.

(2) “*Real character*”—that is, spiritual character.
(3) “*Pure*”—that is, unchanging and hence free from rise and fall.
(4) “*Isolated*”—Free from the effects of virtue and vice.
(5) “*Troubles*”—Birth, life and experience.
(6) “*Counter-cogniser of the intellect*”—Though the supreme spirit and the spirit in general are both similar, yet there is some difference in similarity, and this epithet serves to point out that dissimilarity.

The contemplation of one object leads to a cognition of its similars. So by the contemplation of one’s human spirit, there arises the re-cognition of the supreme spirit.

*Question:* Which are the obstacles, and what the agitations of the internal organ <note>The "internal organ" or *antahkarana* refers to the collective functions of the mind, intellect, and ego</note>; and how many are they?

**Sutra (30):—Disease, Languor, Doubt, Carelessness, Sloth, Worldly-mindedness, Mistaken Notions, Missing the Point, and Instability;—these causing the distraction of the internal organ are the obstacles.**

**Commentary:**—The obstacles, the distractors of the internal organ are nine. These are possible only in the company of the functions of the internal organ, which latter too are not possible without these. “Disease”—disorder of the humours, fluids and the senses. “Languor”—incapability of the internal organ to work. “Doubt”—cognition touching both ends of a subject—‘this may be, this may not be’. “Carelessness”—non-reflection on the means of meditation. “Sloth”—Inactivity of the body and mind, due to lethargy. “Worldly-mindedness”—desire with regard to the connection of the internal organ with sensorious objects. “Mistaken Notions”—contrary or false cognition. “Missing the Point”—non-acquirement of the state of meditation. “Instability”—the unfixed character of the internal organ with regard to the stages already acquired. The mind would become steady for the accomplishment of meditation.

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Yoga Sutras, specifically addressing the obstacles to mental stability and the method for overcoming them through single-pointed focus.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>
# THE PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA

These are the distractors of the internal organ <note>The "internal organ" refers to the *antahkarana*, the collective functions of the mind, intellect, and ego</note>, the nine impurities of meditation, also called the "Enemies" and "obstacles" of meditation.

> **Sutra (31):—Pain, Irritation, Trembling, Inhalation and Exhalation are the companions of distractions.**

**Commentary:—**"Pain"—the natural, the terrestrial, and the celestial Pain is that which, when it strikes, causes living beings to strive for its removal. "Irritation"—agitation of the mind, due to the non-fulfilment of desire. "**Trembling**"—that which shakes the body. "**Inhalation**"—is that breath which draws in the external air; and "**Exhalation**" is that which expels the internal air.

These are the companions of distractions. These happen to those whose minds are distracted; and not to those whose minds are at peace.

<note>
Notes:—(1) "Pain"—The "natural" pains are the diseases of the body and the like; "terrestrial pains" are those that are due to earthly beings, such as serpents, tigers, etc.; and "celestial pains" are such as are due to the influences of planets and the like.
(2) "**Trembling**"—Disturbs the posture, and thence the mind.
(3) "**Inhalation**"—Disturbs the <term>Rechaka</term> <note>original: "Rechaka"; the yogic practice of controlled exhalation</note> and "exhalation" disturbs the <term>Puraka</term> <note>original: "Pùraka"; the yogic practice of controlled inhalation</note>.
</note>

> **Sutra (32):—For their prevention, practice on one principle.**

**Commentary:—**One ought to apply the internal organ fixed upon one single principle for the prevention of distraction. For one whose internal organ consists only of mere perceptions based upon several objects and is transitory, such a person's internal organ can never be concentrated, and as such must be distracted. If, however, it is drawn away from all other objects and fixed on any one object, then it becomes concentrated, and hence not based on several objects.

One who thinks the internal organ to be concentrated when it is found to consist in an uninterrupted flow of similar perceptions—for him, if concentration is a property of the current of the internal organ, then there is the objection that such a

<summary>This page identifies the physical and mental symptoms that accompany a distracted mind, such as pain and irregular breathing, and proposes "one-pointedness" or focus on a single principle as the primary remedy.</summary>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical defense of a singular, enduring internal organ (mind) against the Buddhist theory of momentary, discrete cognitions. It follows the discussion on overcoming distractions through the practice of a single principle.</meta>

# CHAPTER I—SUTRA 32.

The mental current cannot be *one*, because it is momentary <note>The author is arguing against the Buddhist view that the mind is just a series of fleeting moments rather than a permanent entity</note>. If, however, concentration is said to belong to the cognition only so far as the current is concerned—then there is the objection that in that case, the whole of this current, consisting either of a stream of *similar* cognitions or *dissimilar* ones, would be fixed upon several objects and as such be considered "concentrated." If that were true, there could never be any distraction of the internal organ <term>internal organ: the 'Antahkarana' or the mind-complex that processes thoughts</term>. Therefore, it must be concluded that the *internal organ* is a single entity that has many objects. 

Again, if unconnected and heterogeneous (or dissimilar) cognitions were brought about by a non-unified internal organ, then one cognition would remember the object perceived by a completely different cognition, and one would experience the effects of the karmic residua <term>karmic residua: the subconscious imprints or 'samskaras' left by past actions</term> gathered by another cognition. Such a theory of cognition, even if fixed in some way, would fall under the rule of "the cowdung and milk-pudding" <note>A traditional Indian logical fallacy (Gomaya-payasiya-nyaya) where two things are wrongly equated just because they share a source, like claiming cowdung and milk-pudding are the same because they both come from a cow</note>.

And further, if the *internal organ* were different at every moment, then the Buddhist <note>original: "Bauddha"</note> would fall into the danger of having done away with the cognition of his own self. How? "I am touching what I had seen," "I am seeing what I had touched"—such a cognition is present to the person as being *identical*, even though the specific acts of sensing are different. How could this consciousness of a single cognition—the cognition of the identical self—rest itself upon a single conscious agent if it existed in several and altogether dissimilar internal organs? This consciousness of personal identity is quite perceptible to the agent; and certainly, the force of direct perception is not invalidated by any other proof; for all other proofs owe their strength in practical use to perception alone.

Therefore, the internal organ is one, can be fixed upon various objects, and is steady.

---

->**Notes:**<-

(1) "*One principle*"—meaning God <note>The 'Ishvara' or supreme source mentioned in previous sutras</note>.

(2) "*Based upon several objects*"—That is, beginning and ending in the appearance of the objects themselves, whether these are one or many, and not extending to others.

(3) "*Transitory*"—This description is added to rule out the possibility of the internal organ perceiving a new object after having already perceived one, if it were truly momentary.

<summary>This page argues for the existence of a single, permanent mind (internal organ) by demonstrating that personal identity and memory would be impossible if the mind were merely a series of momentary, disconnected thoughts. It refutes Buddhist momentary-cognition theories using the logic of personal experience and the fallacy of false equivalence.</summary>
<keywords>internal organ, mind, Buddhism, cognition, karma, personal identity, perception, logic, God, Yoga Sutras</keywords>

object already. A momentary organ can never perceive more than one object. In the yoga system however the internal organ <note>Sanskrit: *antahkarana*; the collective functions of the mind, ego, and intellect</note> not being momentary is capable of running over many objects in a single moment; and as such it is subject to distraction.

(4) "*One who thinks &c.*"—The opinion of a section of the nihilists <note>Referring to certain schools of Buddhist philosophy that argue for the non-existence of a permanent self</note> who hold that though no distraction is possible in a single momentary internal organ, it would be possible in an everlasting current of the internal organ.

(5) "*Such a current cannot be one*"—That is one and connected with many cognitions, as they happen to come forth. Such a one cannot be momentary; and anything other than this, the nihilist denies.

(6) "*The cowdung and the pudding*"—*i. e.* the vulgar reasoning that the cowdung is the pudding, because both owe their existence to the cow. <note>A logical fallacy used to mock the idea that a "current" of moments can be treated as a single, unified entity</note>

### Aphorism (33):—The peacefulness of the internal organ through friendliness, compassion, complacency and indifference in regard to pleasure and pain, and virtue and vice.

*Commentary:*—One ought to bear friendship towards all the beings that may be enjoying pleasure; compassion towards the distressed; complacency <note>Or "joyful appreciation"</note> towards the virtuous; and indifference towards the vicious. To one who thus bears himself, accrues pure virtue; thence the internal organ becomes cheerful (peaceful); and being cheerful, it becomes concentrated, and attains to steadiness.

*Notes:*—(1) "*For some*"—*i. e.* The internal organ for which the purifications are laid down.
(2) "*Friendliness*"—Removes envy.
(3) "*Compassion*"—The thought of removing another's pain removes the sinful desire of harming others.
(4) "*Complacency*"—Towards the virtuous, removes envy.
(5) "*Indifference*"—Removes anger.
(6) "*Pure virtue*"—*i. e.* Abounding in goodness <note>Sanskrit: *Sattva*; the quality of light, clarity, and harmony</note>.

### Aphorism (34):—Or by expulsion and retention of breath.

*Commentary:*—The throwing out of the internal air through the nostrils, by a special effort, is "Expulsion;" 'Retention' <note>Sanskrit: *Pranayama*</note> is the regulation of breath. By these two means also is one to bring about steadiness of the internal organ.

# PADA I—SUTRA 35.

### Sutra (35):—Or Cognition resulting from sensuous objects may be the cause of steadiness.

**Commentary:—Of one who concentrates his mind on the tip of the nose, the consciousness of superphysical <note>original: "superphisical"</note> odor is the disposition of smell; by concentration on the tip of the tongue, the consciousness of taste; that of color, in the palate; that of touch, in the center of the tongue; and that of sound at the base of the tongue. These dispositions, when produced, bind the internal organ <note>The "internal organ" refers to the mind or psyche (Antahkarana) in Yoga philosophy.</note> to steadiness, remove all doubt, and become the means of meditative wisdom. A similar objective cognition is to be understood with regard to the cognitions of the sun, the moon, the planets, the gems and the like.**

<margin>Though all principles, got at through the various sciences, inferences and the instructions of teachers, are always true,—because all these agents have the capacity of expounding only true principles,—yet, so long as even a single portion thereof has not been amenable to one’s own sense-organs, the whole of it appears far removed, and as such does not bring about the steadiness of the internal organ with regard to such subtle entities, as <term>Isolation</term> <note>Isolation (Kaivalya) is the ultimate goal of Yoga: the soul's liberation from matter.</note> and the like. Therefore for the sake of strengthening the scientific, inferential and tutorial doctrines, one should always try to directly perceive any one of the particulars. And when a particular portion of the imparted doctrine has been directly perceived, then the agent gains faith in even the subtlest elements thereof, ending in <term>Isolation</term>. For this reason is this objective cognition called “the perfection or embellishment of the internal organ.”</margin>

With regard to the non-fixed (that is, stray) cognitions, when there has been produced the <term>Consciousness of having subdued</term> <note>original: "Vasîkârasanjnâ"</note> with regard thereto, then would it (the internal organ) be capable of directly perceiving the various objects. And on this will follow unimpeded, faith, energy, memory and meditation.

### Aphorism (36):—Or the sorrowless, luminous state.—

**Commentary:**—Add the phrase “disposition which, when produced, causes the steadiness of the internal organ” <note>The "internal organ" refers to the *antahkarana*, the collective functions of the mind, intellect, and ego</note>—this much must be carried over from the previous aphorism. The perception (or consciousness) of the internal organ belonging to one who focuses on the <term>lotus of the heart</term> <note>A spiritual center or chakra located in the chest</note> is of two kinds: (1) The “sorrowless,” which is directed toward objects, and (2) the pure sense of "I" or self-consciousness. This latter state is also called the “luminous,” through which the internal organ of the <term>yogi</term> <note>A practitioner of yoga</note> attains stability. The internal organ, in its state of essential goodness, is radiant and vast like the sky; the state resulting from the purity of its steadiness develops into a brightness resembling the sun, the moon, planets, or precious gems. Similarly, when the internal organ rests upon the pure sense of self, it becomes like the still waters of the ocean—calm and infinite. In connection with this, it is stated: “The practitioner, having contemplated the infinitesimal self, possesses the realization, *I am*.”

**Notes:**—(1) “Sorrowless”—meaning a state free from all pain.

(2) “*Lotus of the heart*”—There is a symbolic lotus located between the stomach and the breastbone, facing downwards; the <term>yogi</term> must turn it upwards by means of <term>Rechaka</term> <note>The controlled exhalation of breath</note> and concentrate the mind upon it. Within this lotus, the <term>Sushumna channel</term> <note>original: "Sushumnâ nâdî"; the central nerve or channel for spiritual energy in the subtle body</note> originates. Since this <term>channel</term> is the designated seat of the internal organ, the <term>yogi</term> attains consciousness of the mind by focusing on this lotus.

(3) “*Like the Sky*”—Meaning it is all-pervading and expansive.

(4) “*Similarly etc.*”—Having explained the condition of the mind (which is an effect of the ego), the commentator now explains the nature of the ego or self-consciousness itself.

(5) “*Calm*”—That is, free from the turbulent waves of <term>Rajas</term> <note>The quality of activity, passion, and restlessness</note> and <term>Tamas</term> <note>The quality of darkness, inertia, and ignorance</note>.

(6) “*Pure egotism*”—This refers to a singular, unified light, unlike the various celestial brightnesses mentioned previously.

(7) “*As is declared*”—This refers to a statement by the ancient sage <term>Panchasikha</term>.

<summary>This page explains Sutra 1.36, describing a meditation technique focused on the "sorrowless, luminous" state found within the heart's spiritual center to achieve mental stability. It distinguishes between objective luminous perceptions and the higher, calm realization of the pure "I am" consciousness.</summary>

### Aphorism (41):—To him whose mental functions are subdued, like that of a clear gem, there is—with regard to the knower, the means of knowing, and the object known—a state of total absorption, identity, or becoming one with that which the mind rests upon.

### Commentary:—“To him of over-powered functions”—To one whose wandering thoughts have been suppressed. “Like that of a clear gem” only cites an example. That which is affected by minute elements assumes their character, and as such appears in the same form; similarly that which is affected by a gross object assumes its character and hence appears in a gross form; and lastly, in the same manner, that which is affected by the various modifications of the universe, assumes the character

### **Aphorism (42):—The argumentative condition <term>Savitarka Samapatti: a state of meditation where the mind still distinguishes between the word, the object, and the idea.</term> is that which is mixed with the modification of words, meaning and understanding (or idea.)**

Where however, they are differentiated, there are some properties of the word, others of the meaning,

<meta>Continuing the discussion of the stages of Samadhi (meditative absorption) from the previous page, specifically focusing on the distinction between Savitarka (argumentative) and Nirvitarka (non-argumentative) states.</meta>

# YOGA-DARSHANA <note>The Philosophy of Yoga</note>

and others again of the idea—Such is the way of their differentiation. If the object ‘cow’, rising to the contemplative consciousness of the *yogi* <note>a practitioner of yoga</note> engaged in the aforesaid (fanciful perception), happens to be affected by the modification of word, meaning and idea, then it becomes of a mixed up condition, and is called the “argumentative.” <note>original: "Savitarka"; a state where the mind still confuses the word, the object, and the mental concept.</note>

**Note:—(1) We fancy the identity of the object ‘cow’ and the word when pointing to the animal as a ‘cow’, and this identity is further transferred to the idea of the animal in the mind of the speaker and the listener.**

When on the other hand, in the contemplative consciousness, which is free from all notions of verbal convention as well as from all modification of knowledge due to valid testimony and inference—then there appears the object in its own real form, characterised by nothing but this form alone; and that condition is the “Non-argumentative.” <note>original: "Nirvitarka"; a higher state of focus where the mind perceives the object directly without the interference of language or labels.</note> This is the highest perception; and it is the root of valid testimony and inference, both of these arising from it. Such a perception cannot be said to occur in the company of knowledge due to assertion and inference. Therefore the perception of the *yogi* due to non-argumentative Meditation, is unmixed with any other kind of right notion; and the author explains the definition of this non-argumentative condition, by means of the following aphorism:—

**Aphorism (43):—The non-argumentative is that in which, on the dissolution of memory, the meaning (or object) alone is apparent, and which appears as if devoid of its own identity.** <note>original: "Sutra"; a concise rule or statement in Sanskrit literature.</note>

***Commentary:***—All verbal convention, fancy with regard to knowledge due to testimony and inference, and memory, having been dissolved—the consciousness, coloured by the form of the object of cognition <term>cognition: the mental action or process of acquiring knowledge and understanding through thought, experience, and the senses</term> (acceptance), forsakes, as it were, that form of its own which is constituted by the acceptance.

<summary>This page distinguishes between "argumentative" meditation, which is clouded by language and mental labels, and "non-argumentative" meditation, where the object is perceived in its pure, essential form. It introduces Sutra 1.43, which defines the highest state of perception as one where the mind's own identity seems to disappear into the object of focus.</summary>
