--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical analysis of the "non-argumentative" (Nirvitarka) state of Samadhi, specifically addressing how the mind perceives a whole object rather than just its constituent atoms.</meta>

# CHAPTER I—APHORISM 43.

...or cognition, and becomes solely of the form of the *object*—that is, it assumes the form of the *object* of (acceptance or) cognition; and this (state of consciousness) is the **non-argumentative condition** <note>original: "Nirvitarka Samapatti." This refers to a state of deep focus where the mind stops labeling the object with words or memories and simply "becomes" the object itself.</note>. Thus has this been explained already (in the introductory remarks to this aphorism).

The object of this condition is the universe of objects, animate or inanimate, which consists (respectively) in the object as a whole (in the case of living beings) or in a particular agglomeration of minute particles <note>original: "paramanu," the smallest indivisible units of matter in Indian philosophy, similar to atoms.</note> (in the case of non-living things), and which gives rise to a **unitary consciousness** (that is, the awareness of the object as a single, unified whole). 

This particular agglomeration (the gross, visible object) is the common property of the minute particles (constituting it); and as such, constituting their very nature, it is inferred from the manifested effect (the mental concept of the object as a whole). It presents itself through the conditions that allow it to be seen, and disappears when other properties (of those same particles) appear. This same property (described above) is called the **“component whole”** <note>original: "avayavin." This is a vital concept in the Nyaya-Vaisheshika schools, arguing that a "whole" is a distinct reality from the sum of its parts.</note>, which is characterized as ‘one, large, small, tangible, active, and transient’; it is by means of such component wholes that all worldly business and interaction is carried on. 

To a person who believes such a particular agglomeration is nothing (and merely an object of abstract perception), and to whom a minute (or subtle) cause is imperceptible (even to abstract, undefined perception)—to such a person, there being no "component whole," all knowledge would be false. False knowledge has been defined as a “notion abiding in a form which is not that of the object” (Aphorism 7). And under these circumstances, there being no real object to perceive, what could possibly constitute true knowledge? Whatever object is perceived has been determined to be a component whole. Therefore, it must be admitted that there is a component whole—possessing qualities like largeness and tangibility—which becomes the true object of the **non-argumentative condition**.

<summary>This page argues for the reality of the "component whole" (the object as a single entity), asserting that without recognizing the whole as a real object, all human knowledge would be considered false.</summary>
<keywords>Nirvitarka Samapatti, non-argumentative condition, atoms, paramanu, component whole, avayavin, epistemology, true knowledge, perception</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical commentary on the Yoga Sutras, specifically focusing on the nature of perception and the transition from gross objects to subtile (fine) objects of meditation.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
-># PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA<-

**Notes:—(1)** ***"Which gives rise to unitary consciousness"***—that is, the mental concept of the perceived object as a single, complete whole in itself.

**(2)** ***"This particular agglomeration etc."***—The object as a whole is the common property of every single particle that makes it up, rather than belonging to any specific subset of them. This implies that each individual atom contains within itself a "miniature," so to speak, of the entire object.

**(3)** ***"Which consists respectively in the object as a whole etc."***—The phrase ***"giving rise to a unitary consciousness"*** is used to argue against the *Vaibhashika* <note>A school of early Buddhist philosophy that viewed the world as composed of momentary discrete particles</note> view that an object is merely a collection of particles, denying the existence of a "whole." The phrase ***"which consists in the object as a whole"*** argues against the *Yogachara* <note>A Buddhist school of Idealism</note> view that nothing exists except for the "idea" or mind. Finally, the phrase ***"which consists in a particular agglomeration of particles"*** argues against the atomic theory of the *Vaishesikas* <note>An ancient Indian school of logic and atomism</note>.

**(4)** ***"Inferred from the manifested effect"***—It should be noted that the *Bhashya* <note>The traditional commentary, likely by Vyasa</note> is here supporting the ***"Effect-as-an-Entity"*** theory (*Satkaryavada*) of the *Sankhya* philosophy <note>The dualistic school of philosophy upon which Yoga is built</note>; this suggests that the atom carries within it the blueprint or miniature of the final object it helps create.

**(5)** ***"On the appearance of other properties"***—that is, when the particles possess the property of a "jar," the jar exists; however, when an external cause creates in those same particles the property of a *Kapala* <note>original: "Kapala"; a broken piece or shard of a ceramic vessel</note>, the original object (the jar) disappears.

**(6)** ***"One to whom such etc."***—According to the Buddhists, the gathering of particles, though tiny, is still perceivable through abstract or indeterminate perception; however, the "subtile cause" (the underlying essence) is not perceivable even then. *Vachaspati Misra* <note>A 10th-century philosopher who wrote the "Tattva-Vaisharadi" commentary on Yoga</note> provides a specific reading—***"For one to whom the specific accumulation is not a real thing"***—which has been followed in this translation.

---

### Aphorism (44):—By this, the deliberative and the non-deliberative states, as they relate to subtile objects, are also described.

**Commentary:**—Of these, the state called "deliberative" (*Savitarka*) is that which relates to the subtile Elements <note>The "Tanmatras" or five fine essences of sound, touch, color, taste, and smell</note> whose properties are currently active, and which are defined by a specific sense of place, time, and cause. In this state, the object of Meditative consciousness is a subtile element characterized by a visible property and understood through a Unitary consciousness (*see the notes above*). On the other hand, the "non-deliberative" (*Nirvitarka*) state is that—

<summary>This page provides technical commentary on the perception of objects as "wholes" versus "parts," refuting rival Buddhist and Atomist schools. It introduces the transition from meditating on gross physical objects to meditating on "subtile" or elemental essences.</summary>
<keywords>Unitary consciousness, Vaibhashika, Yogachara, Vaishesika, Sankhya, Satkaryavada, Vachaspati Misra, Savitarka, Nirvitarka, subtile elements, meditation theory</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of the stages of Samadhi (meditative absorption) from the previous page, specifically transitioning from the "argumentative" stage dealing with gross matter to the "deliberative" stage dealing with subtile elements.</meta>

# CHAPTER I—APHORISM 45.

which belongs in every way and by every means to such subtile <note>Subtile here refers to the "Tanmatras" or the underlying essence of matter before it becomes visible or solid.</note> objects as are free from the specialization of all properties—whether past, present, or future—and yet are accompanied by all properties and contain them. Being of such a character, the subtile Element, by means of this same form, becomes an object of perception, and as such colors or affects the form of meditative consciousness. When this consciousness loses its own identity or form and consists solely in the object, it is called the “non-deliberative.” Of these, the “argumentative” and the “non-argumentative” belong to gross, extended objects, whereas the “deliberative” and the “non-deliberative” belong to the subtile ones. Thus, also, by this very “non-argumentative” condition has been described the destruction of fancy <note>Fancy here refers to "Vikalpa," the mental construction or linguistic labeling that distracts from the pure essence of the object.</note> with regard to both.

---

### **Notes :—**

(1) “*A notion of place, time and cause*”—The causal series is thus laid down: the Earth-atom arises from the five rudimentary Elements abounding in the odoriferous <note>The essence of smell.</note> Element, and so on.

(2) “*Of this that which appears etc.*”—This points out the similarity between the “deliberative” and the “argumentative” of the last aphorism.

(3) “*In all ways by all means etc.*”—That is, in all the forms of red, green, yellow etc. and by the notions of place, time, and cause. This implies that the atoms are not affected by the limitations of time—not even through the properties begun in time, as is implied by the Epithet “*Peaceful and Arisen*” <note>original: "Sântodita." This refers to properties that have either subsided into the past or have just manifested.</note>. But for all this, the atom is related to the properties (“*Following all qualities*” <note>original: "Sarvadharmànupàtishu."</note>) as being their substratum.

(4) “*Fancy with regard to both*”—The fancy of word and meaning with regard to the “non-deliberative” and the “non-argumentative”; and not the “deliberative and the non-deliberative” as Vijnana Bhikshu <note>A 16th-century philosopher and commentator on Yoga and Vedanta.</note> explains; because the deliberative is free from the aforesaid fancy.

---

## Aphorism (45) :—The Subtile objectivity terminates in the indissoluble. <note>The "indissoluble" or "Alinga" refers to Pradhana, the primary root of nature which cannot be further divided.</note>

**Commentary:—**Of the Earth-atom, the odoriferous Element is the subtile object; of the Aqueous, the taste-Element; of the Fiery, the color-Element; of the Aerial, the tactile Element;

### Sutra (46):—These verily constitute "seeded" meditation.

*Commentary:*—These four conditions have external objects for their seed; consequently the meditation is "seeded" <note>meditation that still relies on an object or support for focus</note>. Among these, that with regard to gross objects, is "argumentative" and "non-argumentative," and that with regard to subtle objects, is "deliberative" and "non-deliberative,"—thus four-fold has meditation been numbered.

### *Sutra (48):*—In that state, consciousness is **truth-bearing**. <note>The term used here is "Ritambhara," meaning it carries or contains the ultimate truth of existence.</note>

*Commentary:*—During the aforementioned condition, the consciousness of the meditator is given the name “truth-bearing;” and this name is applied literally; because such consciousness supports truth alone, without a trace of falsehood; as is declared: 
> “By valid testimony, by inference, and by a respectful practice of contemplation, one develops his consciousness and attains to supreme union <term>Yoga: the state of spiritual liberation and mental control</term>.”

### *Sutra (49):*—has a subject matter different from that of revealed knowledge or inference, because it refers to specific particulars.

*Commentary:*—“Revealed”—refers to knowledge derived from sacred scriptures; this refers to generalities, for certainly the scriptures can never describe every specific detail. Why? Because language (on which revelation is based) does not function through individual particulars but through general categories. Similarly, inference also refers to generalities alone. <note>For example, the logical rule: "Where there is smoke, there is fire" is a general principle, not a description of one specific flame.</note> The typical form of inference is: where there is an approach, there is access, and where there is no approach, there is no access. Inference leads to conclusions only through general rules. Therefore, there can be no specific individuality that is the direct object of either scripture or inference. Nor is this subtle, hidden, and magnificent object of meditation accessible to ordinary sensory perception. Nor, lastly, can we deny the existence—

# YOGA-DARSHANA

Therefore this particular object—whether pertaining to the <term>subtile Elements</term><note>Tanmatras: the five subtle essences of sound, touch, form, taste, and smell that precede physical matter</note> or to <term>Spirit</term><note>Purusha: the pure, eternal consciousness or true self</note>—is amenable to meditative consciousness alone. Therefore the aforesaid consciousness has an object different from those of revealed <note>knowledge from sacred texts</note> and inferential <note>knowledge from logic</note> consciousness, because it appertains to particulars.

**Sutra (50):** The impression or <term>residuum</term><note>Samskara: the mental "groove" or latent habit left behind by an experience</note> due to this is contradictory to other impressions.

**Commentary:** The impression (residuum) arising from meditative consciousness obstructs the residuum of impression produced by the <term>worldly condition</term><note>original: "Vyutthana" — the state of mind when it is distracted or engaged with the external world</note>. And the impressions of the worldly condition having been suppressed, the cognitions due to these are no longer produced; and these cognitions being suppressed, meditation comes forward; then follows meditative consciousness, and in its wake, the impressions produced by that consciousness. Thus every time a new residuum of impressions is produced giving rise to a fresh supply of consciousness, which again in its turn would bring about fresh impressions.

(*Question*): “Wherefore will this excess of impressions not afford power *i. e.* function, or capacity to the <term>internal organ</term><note>Antahkarana: the collective mind, including intellect, ego, and sensory mind</note>”? 

(*Reply*): The impressions brought about by the aforesaid consciousness do not give power to the internal organ; because they are the means of the removal of pain; in fact they serve to separate the internal organ from its functionings; for the functionings of the internal organ has its termination in <term>discriminative wisdom</term><note>The final realization that distinguishes the soul from the material world</note>.

---

What occurs to this? (*Reply*):

**Sutra (51):** On the suppression of this also, all being suppressed, (there is) <term>meditation without a seed</term><note>Nirbija Samadhi: the highest state of meditation where even the "seed" of the meditative thought is dissolved, leading to liberation</note>.

<summary>This page explains how meditative insight creates new mental habits that overwrite worldly distractions, eventually leading to a state of "seedless" meditation where all mental activity ceases. It distinguishes the direct experience of the soul from knowledge gained through logic or books.</summary>

# CHAPTER I—SUTRA 51.

**Commentary:—**And this state serves as an obstacle not only to meditative consciousness, but also to all mental impressions produced by that consciousness. Why is this so? Because the impressions produced by the act of suppression <note>The intentional halting of mental activity</note> always obstruct those produced by meditation. Furthermore, the existence of these impressions, created by the suppressive mind, can be inferred from the experience of the passage of time during the state where the internal organ <term>Antahkarana: the collective functions of the mind, intellect, and ego</term> is suppressed. 

The internal organ eventually resolves itself back into its own permanent Nature <note>original: "Prakriti"</note>, along with all the impressions produced by the waking, suppressed, and meditative states, all of which participate in the process of isolation <term>Kaivalya: the final state of liberation or "aloneness" of the soul</term>. Therefore, such impressions act as obstacles to the further activity of the internal organ, and as such, they do not lead to its continued existence. 

Through this process, the internal organ—now divested of its functions—ceases to act along with the impressions of the isolated state. Upon the suppression of even this final state, the Spirit <term>Purusha: the pure, eternal consciousness</term> abides in its own true form; for this reason, it is called *pure and liberated.*

**Notes (1)** *“Impressions produced by suppression”*—That is to say, those impressions created by the very force that suppresses the aforementioned consciousness.

<summary>This passage explains the final stage of Yoga where even the impressions of meditation are suppressed, leading the mind to dissolve back into Nature and allowing the Spirit to achieve total liberation.</summary>

# SECTION II.
## ON THE MEANS TO MEDITATION.

The Yoga belonging to one of a pacified mind has been expounded. Now begins the consideration of the question: "How would one whose mind is in the waking <note>The "waking" state refers to the ordinary, distracted consciousness of worldly life.</note> state achieve Yoga?"

**Sutra (1):—Self-discipline, Study, and Devotion to God constitute the Yoga of Action.**

*Commentary:—*Yoga is not achieved by those without self-discipline. Such discipline is a means to Yoga because, without it, it is impossible to break through the impurities that are entangled in the meshes of sensory objects and mixed with endless actions, afflictions, and desires. The self-discipline intended here is the undisturbed or unchecked peace of mind which the yogi must strive for—as has been declared in the scriptures. "Study" refers to the repetition of sacred words, such as the **Pranava** <note>The sacred syllable 'Om'.</note> and others, or the study of the sciences treating of Liberation.

> "Devotion to God"—the offering of all actions to the Supreme Teacher or the Venerable One, or the relinquishment of the fruits of those actions.

**Notes:—**"*And self-discipline, etc.*"—The Commentator here distinctly denies the theory of <term>penance</term> <note>The original text uses "pennance," referring here to extreme physical mortification which the author argues is less effective than mental discipline.</note> and similar religious austerities being the primary means to Yoga. In his opinion, a peaceful equilibrium of mind alone can lead to Yoga.

> "Study"—Aphorism 44 of Chapter II would support the second interpretation of the Commentary.

---

->And this Yoga of Action—<-

**Sutra (2):—Is for the cultivation of meditative absorption and for the weakening of distractions.**

*Commentary:—*The Yoga of Action, when practiced, leads to the realization of **Samadhi** <note>A state of intense concentration or meditative absorption where the practitioner becomes one with the object of meditation.</note> and weakens the distractions of the mind.

<summary>This page introduces the "Yoga of Action" (Kriya Yoga), outlining the three preliminary practices—self-discipline, study, and devotion—necessary for worldly individuals to reach higher meditative states.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga of Action, Kriya Yoga, self-discipline, Samadhi, Pranava, devotion to God, mental equilibrium</keywords>

and the distractions, being thus weakened, are bound to have their roots burnt up as it were by the fire of wisdom, and thus to be deprived of their power to produce further effects. Once they have been weakened, the discriminative knowledge of the difference between the material attributes <note>The Gunas: the three fundamental qualities of nature</note> and the Spirit <note>Purusha: the true self or pure consciousness</note>, being untainted by distractions, and thus constituting the subtle consciousness;—has its functions finished, and is ready for dissolution ( *literally*, retrograde progress <note>original: "pratiprasava" — the process of effects returning to their cause</note> ).

**Notes:—(1)** “ *Subtle* ”—because beyond the reach of the senses.
**(2)** “ *Functions* ”—The production of various effects.

Now what are the distractions, and how many are they?
( **Reply** ) :—

### **Sutra ( 3 ) :—Ignorance, Egoism, Desire, Aversion, and Attachment are the five distractions.**

*Commentary* :—“ Distractions ” <note>Kleshas: the innate causes of suffering</note>— that is to say the five misconceptions ( I—8 ). These issuing forth strengthen the *power* (capacity) of the material attributes, establish the various modifications of the mind, lay open the current of cause and effect, and lastly, depending upon mutual aid, bring out the ripening of Karma <note>The fruition or results of past actions</note>.

**Notes—(1)** “ *Mutual aid* ”—Vâchaspati Mis’ra <note>A 10th-century philosopher and commentator</note> explains this as being the mutual dependence of actions and distractions, the one giving rise to the other, by turns. Vijnâna Bhikshu <note>A 16th-century philosopher</note> and others explain it as that between the five distractions themselves—Ignorance producing Desire, Desire Ignorance and so on.

---

### **Sutra ( 4 ) :—Ignorance is the breeding ground for those that follow—( *whether they be* ) dormant, weak, intercepted, or operative.**

*Commentary* :—Here “ Ignorance ” <note>Avidya: spiritual blindness or lack of true knowledge</note> is the “ breeding ground ” *i.e.*, the productive soil of the following :—*i.e.*, Egoism and the rest which are assumed to be of four kinds—dormant, weak, intercepted and operative.

<summary>This page defines the five "distractions" (Kleshas) that hinder spiritual progress and identifies Ignorance (Avidya) as the primary source from which the others—Egoism, Desire, Aversion, and Attachment—arise in various states of activity.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Sutras, Patanjali, Kleshas, Ignorance, Avidya, Egoism, Karma, Vâchaspati Mis’ra, Vijnâna Bhikshu, spiritual distractions</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exposition of the Yoga-Darshana, specifically detailing the various states of mental distractions (Kleshas) as they relate to the path of liberation.</meta>

# YOGA-PHILOSOPHY

Of these, what is <term>dormancy</term> <note>Sanskrit: *Prasupta*; a state where mental afflictions exist only as potential seeds</note>? It is the germinal condition of the distractions abiding in the mind only by their latent potency (*i. e.*, not yet existing in reality). The awakening of these consists in their proximity to their respective objects. For one, however, who has wisdom, and the seed of whose distractions has been burnt up—this does not exist, even on the presentation of the object. For, whence the sprouting of a burnt seed? For this reason, the expert, whose distractions are at an end, is called the “<term>Last-body</term>” <note>original: *Charamadéha*; refers to a person in their final incarnation before achieving total liberation</note>. It is only in such a body, and nowhere else, that distractions attain the fifth condition—wherein the seed has been burnt. In that condition the existing distractions have their productive faculty burnt up, and hence even on the proximity of the objects they have no awakening. Thus has been described “<term>dormancy</term>” as also the non-production of the burnt seeds.

“<term>Weakness</term>” <note>Sanskrit: *Tanu*; a state where distractions are thinned out through spiritual practice</note> is (now) described: Distractions become weak, by being put down by contrary practices. Similarly on being intercepted again and again, they appear again by means of their respective forms—and are thus said to be “<term>intercepted</term>” <note>Sanskrit: *Vicchinna*; a state where one emotion or distraction is temporarily suppressed by the presence of another</note>. How? Because anger is not seen simultaneously with attachment. For certainly anger does not operate at a time when there is attachment. And further, the fact of attachment existing with regard to one object does not lead to the inference of the absence of attachment with regard to (all) other objects. For the assertion that “Chaitra <note>A traditional placeholder name in Indian logic, similar to 'John Doe'</note> is attached to one woman” does not lead to the conclusion that “he has an aversion towards all other women”; all that is meant by the former assertion is that attachment is already in operation with regard to the particular woman, whereas with regard to other women it has only got a potency to become operative at some future time. And such a (distraction endued with a future potency) becomes

<summary>This page defines the various states of mental distractions (Kleshas), explaining how a sage ("Last-body") renders them powerless like burnt seeds, and how emotions like attachment and anger fluctuate between active and intercepted states.</summary>

All these do not go beyond the limits of distraction. Which distraction then, is intercepted, dormant, weak or operative? This will be explained later on. True—but they have been said to be “intercepted etc.” only with regard to their particular phases. As it is suppressed by contrary agencies, so it is rendered manifest by its manifesting causes.

All these distractions are forms of ignorance. Why? Because Ignorance <note>original: "Avidya"</note> pervades through all of them. The form that Ignorance gives to an object, by that form alone do the distractions abide; and they are found to exist simultaneously with misconception, as well as to disappear with Ignorance.

**Notes:** (1) “*It is only in such a body etc.*”: This anticipates the question why the “burnt-seed” <note>A technical term for a state where the seeds of past actions are "scorched" by wisdom and can no longer sprout into new births.</note> was not mentioned as one of the conditions of distraction. The reason given is that the condition in question is found only in the case of the wise one who is in his last body and is not going to be born again,—hence as there is no production of the distraction in this condition, Ignorance cannot be the productive soil for it.

(2) “*All these do not go beyond etc.*”—This is said in anticipation of the objection that as the “operative” ones alone are the distractors, why should the others be called “distractions” at all?

The reply is that as the others too have a latent potency of becoming operative in time, on the presentation of suitable objects, they are also included in the denotation of the word “distraction”.

(3) “*Which distraction then is etc.*”—If all are the same, wherefore the different names?

(4) “*Such a one becomes dormant etc.*”—When a certain distraction is operating, the distraction equipped with a latent future potency becomes dormant with regard to a certain object, weak with regard to another, and intercepted with regard to a third. For certainly it cannot be said to be non-existing; as in that case its appearance at any future time would be impossible.

(5) “*Intercepted*”—“Those afflictions are said to be ‘intercepted’ which remain with their powers overcome by a particularly potent passion, as desire when the prevailing passion is aversion, or aver-

The form of Ignorance is now described :

**Aphorism (5) :—**Ignorance <term>Avidya: the root cause of suffering in Yoga philosophy</term> is the notion of the identity of the eternal, the pure, the joyous and the spirit, with that which is non-eternal, impure, painful and non-spirit.

*Commentary* :—The cognisance of eternality in non-eternal effects *e. g.* 'the permanent earth', 'permanent the heavens together with the moon and stars', 'Immortal the celestial beings.'

Similarly there is a cognition of purity in the impure and disgusting body : it being declared : " on account of the position, the seed, the constituents and also on account of death, and lastly on account of purity being imposed upon it,—the learned have declared the body to be impure." *e. g.* "Beautiful like the fresh moon, this girl appears as if her limbs were made up of honey and ambrosia <note>A mythical drink of the gods that grants immortality</note>, or as if she had emerged from the moon ; and she, having eyes like the blue lotus leaves, by means of her love-ful eyes, enlivens as it were, the living world" :—(in this) what has any 
<margin>!</margin> relation to what ? (4. The whole is altogether an unconnected whole). Thus we find that there is a mistaken notion of purity with regard to impure things. Similar to this is the idea of virtue in vice, and that of useful in the useless.

# SECTION II—APHORISM 5.

The pleasure found in the pain mentioned in the previously cited aphorism is a form of Ignorance.

Similarly, Ignorance is the idea of the spirit <note>The true, eternal self or Purusha</note> existing in that which is not the spirit. This includes external possessions—whether they are living beings or inanimate objects—as well as the physical body, which serves as the foundation for our experiences, and even the mind, which is merely a tool of the spirit. Regarding this, it is written: “Taking these visible or invisible possessions to be his own self, he rejoices in their success, believing that success to be his own; and he mourns their misfortune, believing it to be his own. Such a person is **altogether deluded**.” <note>original: "Panchaśikkháchárya," an ancient teacher of the Samkhya philosophy</note>.

Such is Ignorance <term>Avidya: the primary spiritual error of mistaking the temporary for the eternal</term>. It has four aspects, acting as the root of all suffering and the source of all karmic imprints <note>residua: the subconscious traces left by past actions</note> and their eventual consequences. Like the Sanskrit words *Amitra* <note>Non-friend</note> and *Agoshpada* <note>Not a cow's footprint</note>, the term Ignorance refers to a positive, existing entity. Just as the word ‘Amitra’ does not simply mean the absence of a friend, nor does it mean one specific friend, but rather denotes something entirely opposite—an enemy; and just as the word *Agoshpada* does not mean the *absence of a cow’s footprint*, nor a *single footprint*, but rather denotes a specific type of vast region; in the same way, Ignorance is neither correct understanding nor the mere absence of it. It is a distinct state of mind that stands in direct opposition to true knowledge or wisdom.

**Note:**—(1) “*External accessories.*”—Possessions related to the body, such as parents, spouse, children, furniture, or clothing.

(2) “*Manifested and the unmanifested etc.*”—The "manifested" refers to living connections—parents, children, and siblings; the "unmanifested" refers to inanimate objects like beds and clothes.

(3) The meaning of the final paragraph is that although the word *Avidya* is formed from the parts *a* (not) and *Vidya* (knowledge), it does not mean a simple lack of information. It refers to a specific, incorrect way of perceiving the world. Just as the word *Amitra*, formed from *a* (not) and *mitra* (friend), does not mean the absence of a friend or a single friend (the...

<summary>This page defines Ignorance (Avidya) not as a mere lack of knowledge, but as a positive, deluded state of mind that mistakes the physical body and possessions for the true spiritual self. It uses linguistic analogies to explain that Ignorance is a distinct psychological force and the root of all human suffering.</summary>
<keywords>Ignorance, Avidya, Spirit, Self, Karma, Panchashikha, Sanskrit linguistics, delusion, suffering</keywords>

-># YOGA PHILOSOPHY<-

<note>The text begins with a linguistic analogy regarding how certain words in Sanskrit function as single concepts rather than just the sum of their parts.</note> compound in this being broken up as *Not a Friend from whom* <note>original: "Na Mitro yasmāt"</note>), but simply something opposed to both of these, an enemy; and similarly the word *Not-cow-footprint* <note>original: "Agoshpada"</note> formed by *a* (=not) and *goshpada* (=foot-print of a cow) does not mean what the constituent particles directly denote, but it forms the name of a country.

---

### *Aphorism (6):* —Egoism is the identifying of the power that sees with the power of seeing.

*Commentary:—*Spirit <term>Purusha; the pure consciousness or true self</term> being the “power that sees”, and the mind the “power of seeing” <note>The instrument through which consciousness perceives the world</note>,—the conception of the identity of these two constitutes the distinction of egoism. It is only on the non-discrimination of the two quite unblended and distinct powers of the *enjoyer* <note>The Spirit</note> and *enjoyed* <note>The material world and mind</note> that enjoyment arises; when however, the true nature of the two has been ascertained, there follows isolation <term>Kaivalya; the state of absolute freedom or liberation</term>, and all enjoyment disappears. This has been thus declared:

> “Not perceiving the spirit, which is above mind, and which is distinct from it by reason of its form, character, wisdom and the like,—one forms of the idea of self therein (in the mind) only through stupidity”—(*Panchashikhacharya.*)

*Notes:—*“*Distinct from it etc.*”—the “form” of the spirit is eternal purity as distinct from the impurity of the mind; its “character” is that of a *witness* as apart from the partial nature of the mind; and lastly spirit has wisdom (i.e., the sentient faculty) as apart from the mind which is radically insentient <note>In this philosophy, the mind is considered a subtle form of matter that lacks its own light; it only appears conscious because it reflects the light of the Spirit.</note>.

---

### *Aphorism (7):* —Desire is that which dwells on pleasure.

*Commentary:—*Desire is the hankering after pleasure or its means, preceded by a remembrance of the pleasure, belonging to one who has had experience of that pleasure.

---

### *Aphorism (8):* —Aversion is that which dwells on pain.

*Commentary:—*Aversion consists in the anger, or the desire for removal, of pain or its means, preceded by a remembrance of pain,—belonging to one who has had experience of that pain.

<summary>This page defines three of the five obstacles to spiritual liberation: Egoism (confusing the soul with the mind), Desire (clinging to past pleasures), and Aversion (pushing away past pains).</summary>
<keywords>Egoism, Spirit, Mind, Non-discrimination, Isolation, Desire, Aversion, Panchashikhacharya, Yoga Sutras</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exposition of the five kleshas (distractions/afflictions) from the previous section, specifically focusing on the final distraction, Abhinivesha, and the method for overcoming these obstacles in their subtle form.</meta>

# BOOK II—APHORISM 9.

## *Aphorism (9).*—The well-known distraction, which flows by its own inherent potency and is firmly established even in the learned, is Attachment to Life <note>original: "Abhinivesha"</note>.

In all living beings, there exists a constant, self-centered desire: "May I not cease to exist," and "May I continue to live." Such a desire cannot belong to one who has not previously undergone the experience of death. This is the distraction known as "Attachment to Life," which flows forward through the momentum of its own past impressions <note>original: "residua" or "vasanas"</note>. Even in a tiny worm just born, there is a fear of death—a conscious dread of its own destruction. This fear is not based on direct observation, logical deduction, or the testimony of others in this current life; therefore, it serves as evidence of the agonies of death experienced in previous incarnations.

<margin><unclear>Vijnana Bhikshu</unclear></margin>
Just as this distraction is found in the most ignorant creatures, it is also found among the learned. This is because the aforementioned impressions resulting from the past experience of death's pain are common to both the scholar and the simpleton.

### *Notes:—(1)* 
"The learned" here refers to those who have studied the Sacred Scriptures <note>original: "S’âstras"</note> theoretically, not those who have realized the ultimate Truth. For those who have reached the Truth, every trace of Spiritual Ignorance <note>original: "Avidya"</note> has been consumed by the fire of wisdom; consequently, there can be no distraction, as distraction is merely an effect of Ignorance.

The meaning of the Commentary <note>original: "Bhashya"</note> is summarized by Dr. Mitra as follows: 
> "It (attachment to life) arises from the memory of former experiences. Since no one has experienced the pain of death in this current life, it follows that the fear of death results from the unconscious memory of experiences from a previous state of existence—and this fear is the root cause of the attachment to life."

---

## *Aphorism (10)* These distractions, when they are subtle, should be eliminated by resolving them back into their cause <note>original: "retrogression" or "pratiprasava"</note>.

### *Commentary:—*
Upon the dissolution of the active mind of the Practitioner <note>original: "Yogi"</note>, the five distractions mentioned previously—having had their "seeds" nearly scorched by spiritual practice—disappear along with the mind itself.

<summary>This page defines Abhinivesha (attachment to life) as an innate fear of death derived from past-life experiences and explains that subtle distractions are overcome by dissolving the mind into its cause.</summary>
<keywords>Abhinivesha, attachment to life, fear of death, reincarnation, past-life impressions, Yoga Sutras, Patanjali, mental distractions, dissolution of mind</keywords>

**Notes:—**(1) “ *Dissolution* ”—of the mind into its immediate cause, egoism <note>The sense of "I-ness" or individual identity</note>. This dissolution of the effect into the cause is what is meant by “retrogression;” and it may be added that this is only another name for Absolute meditation <note>original: "Asamprajnata Samadhi," a state of consciousness where the mind is completely still and free from all objects of thought</note>.

->Of those that still continue, and have attained to the condition of the seed.—<-

## **Sutra ( 11 ):—Of these, the functions are to be suppressed by contemplation.**

**Commentary:—**The gross functions of the distractions <note>The five "kleshas" or afflictions: ignorance, egoism, attachment, aversion, and the fear of death</note>, having been attenuated by active yoga <note>The yoga of action, involving discipline, self-study, and devotion</note>, are to be suppressed by contemplative wisdom, till they become Subtile, *i.e.* till they have their seeds nearly burnt up. Just as the dust in a piece of cloth is at first shaken off, and the finer kinds then removed by finer means,—so in the same manner, the gross functions of the distractions have small adversaries, whereas the subtle ones have great adversaries.

**Notes :—**(1) “ *That still continue* ”—that are not yet burnt.
(2) “ *Gross functions* ”—those that are in active operation.
(3) “ *Adversaries* ”—*i.e.* means of suppression or destruction;—the small adversaries of the gross functions are to be—(1) attenuated by Active yoga, (2) have their seeds nearly burnt up by contemplative wisdom (and thus made subtile) and then lastly, (3) suppressed by retrogression—*i.e.* by Absolute meditation.
<sig>8</sig>

## **Sutra ( 12 ):—The residua of actions have their origin in distractions, and are felt in the manifest or the unmanifest life.**

**Commentary:—**Of these, the residua <note>The latent impressions or "karmas" left behind by previous actions</note> of virtuous and vicious deeds give birth to desire, avarice, stupidity and anger; and this residuum is felt either by the manifest birth <note>The current lifetime</note> or by the unmanifest one <note>Future incarnations</note>. Of these that which is brought about by ardent repetition of sacred chants <note>original: "mantras"</note> or by asceticism or by meditation,—or that which is accomplished by a proper attendance on (or

# CHAPTER II—APHORISM 13.

Devotion to the great ones—The Lord, the elemental Gods, the great <term>Rishis</term> <note>Ancient inspired poets or sages who "heard" the eternal truths</note>,—such a <term>residuum</term> <note>original: "vasana" or "samskara"; the latent impressions left by past actions</note> of virtuous actions, bears immediate fruit. In the same manner, the repeated harms that are done to the fearing, the sick and the helpless, or to one who has confidence in the agent, or to the great ascetics—such a residuum of vicious actions also bears immediate fruit. As the <term>Nandisvara Kumara</term> <note>A devotee of Shiva who was transformed into a divine being in a single lifetime</note>, having relinquished the human modification, was transformed into a god, and so also <term>Nahusha</term> the Lord of the gods, having relinquished his own form, was transformed into a beast. Of hellish beings there is no <term>Karmic residuum</term> which is felt in the manifest birth; and for those whose <term>distractions</term> <note>original: "kleshas"; the mental afflictions or obstacles like ignorance and ego</note> have ended, there is no Karmic residuum to be felt in the unmanifest birth.

**Notes.**—*Manifest life is the present life, and unmanifest life is the future life.*

---

### Aphorism (13):—The root existing, its fruition (consists in) birth, life (or age) and experience.

**Commentary:**—It is only when the distractions exist that the Karmic residuum begins its <term>fruition</term> <note>The ripening or result of past actions</note> and not when the root in the shape of distractions has been destroyed. As for example, the rice in the paddy is capable of sprouting up only when it is surrounded by the chaff, and has its seeding faculty undestroyed; and not when the chaff has been removed and the seeding faculty destroyed. Similarly, the Karmic residuum is fit to sprout up into fruition only when hemmed in by distractions, and not when the distractions have been removed, or when its seeding faculty has been destroyed by the fire of wisdom.

This fruition is threefold: Birth, Age, and Experience. In this connection we have to consider the following questions. Is a single action the cause of a single birth, or does a single

# CHAPTER II—SUTRA 13.

On the other hand, that which is felt during the current visible life, being the cause of experience alone, is said to produce a single result; or when it is the cause of both the span of life and the quality of experience, then it is said to produce a two-fold result, as seen in the traditional stories of Nandisvara <note>A devotee of Shiva who was transformed from a human into a divine being in a single lifetime</note> or Nahusha <note>A king who was transformed into a serpent due to a curse, illustrating a drastic change in life-state</note>. The residual longings <term>Vasana: the latent tendencies or "smells" left behind in the mind by past experiences</term> caused by the experience of the results of actions and mental distractions from time immemorial turn the mind into one collective whole; and the mind, being thus filled with various impressions, appears like a fishing net covered in knots. In the case of the mind, these knots are the various residual tendencies described above; thus, these tendencies are said to be preceded by—or caused by—many previous births. It is the Karmic residuum <note>The specific "pot" of karma (Karmashaya) that is ready to ripen</note> alone that is said to be of a single birth. The impressions or tendencies <term>sanskaras: the subconscious imprints that shape character and habit</term>, which cause memory or remembrance, are the residual longings, and they are eternal.

The "one-birth" Karmic residuum is of two types: that which has a certain result and that which has an uncertain result. Of these, the rule of being "of one birth" applies only to the residuum experienced in the current visible life whose result is certain, and not to the residuum whose result is uncertain. This is because, in the case of the latter, there are three possible outcomes: 

1. The destruction of the action that has been performed but has not yet produced results.
2. The merging of secondary actions into a primary, dominant action.
3. The long-term suspension of secondary actions as they are hemmed in by a primary action whose function is certain. 

As an example of the *first* outcome, we see the destruction in the present life of "black" (evil) actions by "white" (good) ones. In this connection, it is declared:

> "In this world, actions are to be considered two-fold; the collection of good actions destroys the evil ones; therefore, desire to perform good actions; the wise ones declare these actions for you in this present birth."

To provide an example of the *second* outcome:

> "There may be little..."

<summary>This page explains how the "Karmic residuum" (the store of past actions) determines the nature of a person's birth, lifespan, and experiences, distinguishing between latent tendencies and active karma. It outlines how good actions can potentially neutralize bad ones before they bear fruit.</summary>

Because the quantity of the good is much, wherein this (evil) is hidden, and which is sure to bring about a slight harm even in heaven.” ( *Panchashikhacharya* <note>An ancient teacher of the Samkhya school of philosophy, often quoted in commentaries on the Yoga Sutras.</note> ). To exemplify the *third* course—“The long continuance of the secondary action hemmed in by the primary one whose fruition is certain”—How? Because it is only for the action experienced in the unmanifest birth <note>Refers to a future life that has not yet been realized or "ripened."</note>, whose fruition is certain, that the cause of manifestation has already been said to be *death*, which is not the case with that which is experienced in unmanifest birth, and whose fruition is uncertain. This latter kind of actions may either be destroyed or dissolved (mixed up with the more important actions), or lastly long continue to exist as hemmed in (by those important actions),—so long as it is not turned towards fruition by its manifesting cause, in the form of some similar action. And it is for this uncertainty of the time, place and cause of the fruition of actions, that the course of action is said to be curious and unintelligible. As the secondary cannot be set aside by the primary, therefore we postulate the “One-birth” <term>karmic residua</term> <note>The latent impressions or "seeds" left behind by actions (karma) that wait for the right conditions to sprout into results.</note>.

**Notes:** (1) “ *Lose all confidence* ” *etc.*—The order of the fruition of <term>Karmic residua</term> being uncertain, the agent could not assure himself whether the evil or the good actions of his would produce results; and hence people would hesitate to give up a presently gratifying evil action for the sake of future happiness.

(2) “ *The residual longings etc.* ”—This anticipates the following objection. ‘If the residual impressions too were “of one birth” like the <term>Karmic residua</term>, then the eternality of these longings (mentioned in aphorism 10 Section 4) would become contradicted’. The sense of the reply is that the mind becoming variegated by the innumerable actions of various kinds, extending from time immemorial, these variegating residual longings must be regarded as belonging to “ **many births** ” and not confined, like <term>Karmic residua</term>, to “ **one birth** ” alone. For if this were not the case, then a celestial or a bestial form after the human one, would not be explicable, as during human life it is

It clarifies that while specific karmic results may be tied to a single birth, the underlying mental longings and impressions are eternal and span many lifetimes.</summary>

(3) “To exemplify the third... How?”—This question is based on the following doubt: “Death has been just said to be the only manifesting cause of the <term>residue of action</term> <note>original: "Karmic residua," the subconscious storehouse of past deeds</note>, and now we find it asserted that the secondary action hemmed in by the primary one can long continue without fruition; and certainly as during this long continuity, many deaths would intervene, the two assertions appear to contradict each other.” The sense of the reply is that the manifestation of the particular set of actions meant here is due not to death, but to a different action similar in nature to the action in question.

*Aphorism (14):*—**They have pleasure or pain for their fruit, according as their cause is virtue or vice.**

*Commentary:* “They” = birth, age, and experience, having <term>virtue</term> <note>original: "dharma," or merit</note> for their cause, have pleasure for their fruit; and when they have <term>vice</term> <note>original: "adharma," or demerit</note> for their cause, then they have pain for their fruit. As this pain consists in contrariness (to the agent’s wishes), so also in the course of the pleasurable enjoyment of an object, there is pain for the <term>yogi</term> <note>one who practices disciplined meditation and spiritual detachment</note>.

*Note:* (1) “*There is pain for the yogi*”—because pain has been defined as something that goes against the agent’s wishes and as sensual pleasure is opposed to the yogi’s wishes; so all sensual pleasure is really pain for him.

*Aphorism (15):*—**To the wise, all is pain—because of the adverse functions of the Attributes, and of the pains of consequence, anxiety, and impression.**

*Commentary:* (1) The experience of pleasure, belonging to all persons, is infused with passion, and dependent upon animated and inanimate means; thus in this case there is the <term>residue of action</term> born of passion; similarly the agent avoids the means to pain, and is deluded (or infatuated),—so

<meta>This page continues the philosophical analysis of the nature of suffering (dukkha) as understood in the Yoga system, specifically focusing on why even pleasurable experiences are considered painful by the wise.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>
-># THE PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA<-

<margin>!</margin>
There exists the <term>Karmic residuum</term><note>The subconscious storehouse of past actions (Karma) that shapes future experiences.</note> born of aversion and mental darkness. As it is stated: "No enjoyment is possible without causing harm to some living beings"—thus, there is a physical Karmic residuum born of such harm. It has already been established that all sensual pleasure is rooted in <term>Ignorance</term><note>Avidya: the spiritual blindness that mistakes the temporary for the eternal.</note>. Pleasure is merely the temporary peace that follows when the senses are satisfied by objects, while pain is the lack of peace resulting from the mind's restlessness. Certainly, the satisfaction of the senses cannot be achieved through repeated indulgence. Why? Because the passions grow stronger through repeated enjoyment, as does the agitation of the senses. Consequently, repeated indulgence cannot be a true means to pleasure. Thus, if a person seeking pleasure becomes obsessed with sensory objects and falls into a swamp of suffering, they are like someone who, fearing the bite of a common scorpion, is instead stung by a deadly serpent. This "pain of consequence" <note>The suffering that inevitably follows once a pleasure ends.</note>, being contrary to one's desires, troubles the <term>yogi</term><note>A practitioner of Yoga who seeks spiritual liberation.</note> even during a moment of pleasure, because he knows it must end in pain.

### II. The Nature of Anxiety and Impressions

Now, what is the nature of the "pain of anxiety"? All people experience anxiety mixed with dislike, whether caused by living beings or inanimate things—this creates a Karmic residuum born of aversion. Furthermore, in the pursuit of pleasure, a person acts through their body, speech, and mind, thereby either helping or harming others. This helping or harming creates a collection of <term>virtue and vice</term><note>Dharma and Adharma: the moral weight of actions.</note>. This specific Karmic residuum arises from greed and delusion; this is what is known as the "pain of anxiety."

What is the "pain of impressions"? The subconscious residue of pleasurable impressions arises from the experience of pleasure, and the residue of painful impressions arises from the experience of pain.

<summary>This text explains the three types of suffering—consequence, anxiety, and subconscious impressions—arguing that sensual pleasure is deceptive because it strengthens desires and inevitably leads to pain.</summary>
<keywords>Karmic residuum, Yoga Philosophy, suffering, sensory pleasure, anxiety, virtue and vice, subconscious impressions, renunciation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical analysis of the nature of suffering (dukkha) and the mechanics of the mind according to the Yoga Sutras, specifically expanding on why the enlightened practitioner perceives all worldly experience as painful.</meta>

# CHAPTER II—SUTRA 15.

Similarly, when either pleasure or pain is experienced as the fruits of one's actions, there follows a further accumulation of <term>Karmic residuum</term> <note>The latent impressions or "seeds" left behind by actions that necessitate future rebirth and experience.</note>. Thus, this everlasting flow of the current of pain—which consists of experiences contrary to one's true desires—makes the <term>yogi</term> <note>A practitioner of yoga seeking liberation.</note> anxious. Why is this so? Because the wise person is as sensitive as an eye-ball. Just as a fine thread of wool placed in the eye causes pain by its mere touch, whereas it would not be felt on other parts of the body, so do these aforementioned pains affect the eye-ball-like <term>yogi</term> alone, and no other ordinary person. 

On the other hand, the ordinary person is an agent who simply endures the pain caused by their own past actions, relinquishes it, and then takes up that pain once again. Such a person is hemmed in on all sides by <term>Ignorance</term> <note>Avidya: the fundamental misconception of the nature of reality.</note>, appearing in the form of mental functions colored by everlasting residual longings. Lastly, this person maintains the idea of "self" and "mine" regarding objects that ought to be avoided (such as the body, spouse, children, etc.). Whenever such an agent is born, they are flooded by <term>threefold pain</term> <note>The traditional classification of suffering: internal (bodily/mental), external (caused by other beings), and environmental/divine (natural disasters/fate).</note>, brought about by both extrinsic and intrinsic causes. Thus, seeing their own self as well as all other beings trapped in this everlasting current of pain, the <term>yogi</term> takes refuge in proper <term>Discrimination</term> <note>Viveka: the faculty of distinguishing between the eternal Self and the temporary material world.</note>, which has the power to destroy all pain.

For the further reason of "the adverse character of the functions of the <term>Attributes</term> <note>Gunas: the three fundamental qualities of nature—Sattva (purity), Rajas (activity), and Tamas (inertia).</note>, all is pain to the wise." The <term>Attributes</term> of the <term>Buddhi</term> <note>The higher intellect or discerning faculty of the mind.</note>—consisting of enlightenment, action (or energy), and idleness—depend upon mutual interaction. They give rise to cognitions that are quiescent, passionate, or delusive, depending on which of the three <term>Attributes</term> is dominant. Because the functioning of these <term>Attributes</term> is fickle, the mind is said to be constantly and quickly modifying. The *forms* as well as the *functions*, when in excess, are contradictory to one another; whereas those in their ordinary condition cooperate with those that are in excess. Thus, these <term>Attributes</term>, depending upon one another, bring about the ideas of pleasure, pain, and—

<summary>This page explains that while ordinary people are numbed to the cycle of suffering, the yogi is as sensitive as an eye-ball to the inherent pain of worldly existence. It introduces the concept that the shifting balance of the three Gunas (Attributes) within the intellect creates a constant state of flux and dissatisfaction.</summary>

# ->YOGA-PHILOSOPHY<-

delusion; therefore each of them has the character of **all the three** <note>Referring to the three Gunas or qualities of nature: lucidity, activity, and inertia</note>; their difference depending upon the predominance and subserviency (of one or the other of them). And thus all is pain to the wise.

Of this aggregate of pains, the productive seed is <term>Ignorance</term> <note>Avidya: the fundamental misunderstanding of the nature of reality</note>; and the cause of the absence of this is <term>Right Discrimination</term> <note>Viveka: the ability to distinguish between the eternal self and the temporary world</note>. As the science of medicine has four parts—viz: the disease, the cause of the disease, cure, and medicine,—so this science also has four parts—viz: <term>metempsychosis</term> (birth) <note>Samsara: the continuous cycle of birth, death, and rebirth</note>, the cause of this, <term>Liberation</term>, and the means of liberation; and of these, <term>Metempsychosis</term>, abounding in pain, is to be avoided; the connection of **Matter and Spirit** is the cause of what is to be avoided; the removal of the connection consists in absolute and final separation; and the cause of this separation is <term>Right Discrimination</term>. In this connection, the form of the avoiding agent cannot be said to be either avoided or accepted; for if it were avoided, that would lead to the theory of self-annihilation; and if it were accepted, then it would lead to the "**cause**" theory; (consequently) by denying both (avoidability and acceptability) we land on the theory of the "**eternal**", which constitutes right discrimination.

**Notes (1)** "*Animate and inanimate*"—wife and children, and beds and clothes etc.

**(2)** "*Infatuated*"—on account of not being able to discard them.

**(3)** "*No enjoyment without killing etc*"—Cf. the "**five places of slaughter for a householder**" <note>original: "पञ्चसूना गृहस्थस्य" (pañcasūnā gṛhasthasya). Refers to the five places where a householder unintentionally kills living beings: the hearth, the grinding stone, the broom, the mortar and pestle, and the water pot.</note> such as the death of insects etc. caused in cooking and so on.

**(4)** "*It has been declared.........Ignorance*"—supply "**by us, at the time of explaining the four forms of Ignorance.**"

**(5)** "*Pleasure consists etc*"—wise people do not wish for that which gives only temporary pleasure; what they strive after is permanent pleasure in the end, and not after the temporary pleasure which ends in pain—Cf. the **Song of the Lord** <note>original: "Bhagavadgîtâ"</note>—"**From the contact of the senses with their objects...**" <note>original: "विषयेन्द्रियसंयोगात्" (viṣayendriyasaṃyogāt). A reference to Gita 18.38, describing pleasure that seems like nectar at first but turns to poison.</note> etc. Pleasure consists in contentment, and it is continued dissatisfaction alone which renders even pleasures painful. Cf. **Bhartrihari** <note>A famous 5th-century Indian philosopher and poet known for his works on ethics and renunciation.</note>—

It argues that the ultimate goal is the eternal separation of Spirit from Matter to end the cycle of suffering.</summary>

# SECTION II—APHORISM 15.

He becomes poor whose desire is vast; but when the mind is fully satisfied, who is wealthy and who is a pauper? <note>original: "स भवति धनहीनो यस्य तृष्णा विशाला मनसि च परितुष्टे कोर्थवान् को दरिद्रः ॥" This Sanskrit verse emphasizes that true wealth is contentment, not material possession.</note>

" *The pain of anxiety* "—Vachaspati Misra <note>A famous 9th-century commentator on Indian philosophical systems.</note> remarks that the commentator does not go into the details of this because it is too well known to need any dilatory expounding; and more so because the pain of anxiety is similar to that of " *Consequence* " mentioned before,—the reason for mentioning them separately is that the pain of anxiety differs from that of consequence in as much as the former is painful all along, in the beginning, in the middle and in the end, whereas the latter is painful only at the end.

(8) " *Passions grow by repeated enjoyment* "—Compare **Manu** <note>Referring to the Laws of Manu, an ancient legal and ethical text.</note>—

->Desire is never extinguished by the enjoyment of desired objects;<-
->rather, it grows stronger, like a fire fed by clarified butter.<-
<note>original: "न जातु काम : कामानामुपभोगेन शाम्यति हविषा कृष्णवर्त्मेव भूयस्वाभिवर्धते ॥"</note>

(8) " *The forms and functions in their excess etc.* "—The " **forms** " are the eight forms of <term>Buddhi</term> <note>The intellectual faculty or higher mind.</note>—viz: (1) virtue, wisdom, dispassion, power, vice, ignorance, passion and imbecility; and the functions are three—viz : pleasure, pain and delusion. The sense is that it is only when all of these are in the height of their power and equally ready for function, that they contradict one another—For Example: when both virtue and vice are in power, then they contradict each other; if however one of them is in its ordinary form, then it co-operates with the stronger one. This has been added in anticipation of the objection that a single entity cannot be pleasurable and painful as well as delusive. The meaning is—"There is hardly any one thing which is not made of these three qualities, as obviously there is nothing which is free from being the source of pleasure, pain and delusion to different parties, and ( at times ) by turns to the same party." ( M. N. Dvivedi ). All objects are made up of the three Attributes <note>The Gunas: Sattva (goodness), Rajas (activity), and Tamas (inertia).</note>; the only difference being that the object that is pleasurable at first sight is abounding in the quality of goodness and so on. [ In this connection, the reader must look up the <term>Sânkhya—Tattva—Kaumudi</term> <note>A key commentary on Sankhya philosophy by Vachaspati Misra.</note>, under <term>Kârikâs</term> <note>Verses or memorial stanzas.</note>: XII and XIII ].

(9) " *The form of the avoiding agent etc.* " : If the form of the agent—i.e. the self—were avoided or suppressed, there would result the annihilation of self, and certainly no sane person would strive after such self-annihilation. Secondly, if it were accepted (effected); then it would follow that being a caused entity, it must have a cause, and be an effect, and thus transient and destructible; and this theory would do away with Liberation which means only Immortality; and certainly no immortality would be possible for the *caused* self. Thirdly if both the above alternatives were denied, then would self come to be eternal.

[ " The object of this aphorism is to establish that everything connected with worldly life is painful, and the yogis should therefore shun them all. To prove this it is first shown that all joyous actions are followed by pain. There must be a change after the enjoyment...

<summary>This page explains why worldly pleasures are ultimately painful due to anxiety and the conflicting qualities of the mind, while arguing for the eternal nature of the self in the context of liberation.</summary>

Then all actions and all impressions leave behind <term>residua</term><note>Sanskrit: *samskaras*, the subconscious imprints left by past experiences</note> in the field of the thinking principle, and they revive sensations of pleasure and pain. The conclusion is a state of pessimism, from which the only relief is to be had by totally cutting himself off from all worldly affairs. ---

->The four-fold nature of this Science is now explained:<-

## *Aphorism* (16): Avoidable is the pain not yet come.

*Commentary:*—The pain that has passed has already been spent up by Experience, and as such cannot come within the range of avoidability. The pain that is present is, during the moment of its existence, ready for experience, and as such cannot at any other moment be avoided. For this reason it is that pain alone which is not yet come, that gives pain to the <term>eye-ball-like yogi</term><note>A metaphor for the practitioner's extreme sensitivity to subtle suffering, just as a tiny speck of dust causes intense pain to the eye but is unnoticed on the skin</note>, and none else; and it is this pain that is to be warded off.

->The cause of that which is to be avoided is now laid down:<-

## *Aphorism* (17):—The conjunction of the Spectator and the Spectacle is the cause of that which is to be warded off.

*Commentary:*—"The Spectator" = the Spirit <note>Sanskrit: *Purusha*, the pure, unchanging consciousness</note>, the cogniser of the <term>Intellect</term><note>Sanskrit: *Buddhi*, the highest faculty of the mind that makes decisions</note>; "The Spectacle" = All the properties that exist in the "goodness" <note>Sanskrit: *Sattva*</note> of the Intellect. Such a spectacle, resembling a magnet, and operating by mere proximity, through its visibility comes to be the Spirit's "*own*"—(the spirit being) the Lord, of the form of (pure) consciousness. (Because) The <term>Pure Intellect</term><note>Sanskrit: *Sattvic Buddhi*, the mind in its most clear and luminous state</note>

<summary>This page explains that Yoga focuses on avoiding future suffering, identifying the root cause of pain as the false entanglement between pure consciousness (the Spectator) and the material mind (the Spectacle).</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Philosophy, suffering, future pain, Purusha, Buddhi, Sattva, Spectator and Spectacle, Rajendralal Mitra</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion on the relationship between the Seer (Spirit) and the Seen (Intellect/Nature), specifically focusing on the cause of suffering as defined in the Yoga Sutras.</meta>

# CHAPTER II—APHORISM 17

(The spiritual consciousness) becomes the object of experience and action (of the Spirit); and acquiring its existence through another form, becomes dependent upon another, though by itself it is independent, on account of its being for another’s purpose <note>This refers to the concept of 'Pararthatva', where nature exists solely for the sake of the Spirit's liberation</note>. Of these two, the spectator and the spectacle, the connection brought about for a purpose, is the cause of that which is to be avoided *i.e.*—of pain. And it has been declared: 

> “By avoiding the cause in the shape of connection therewith (with the Intellect) this would be an absolute remedy for pain”—(Panchaśikha) <note>Panchaśikha was an ancient teacher of the Samkhya school of philosophy</note>. 

Why? Because, we know the remedy for the cause of pain, the avoidable—for example, the soles of the feet being capable of being pierced, and the thorn being capable of piercing, the remedy hereof lies in not putting the foot on a thorn, or if putting it on it, doing so with shoes. One who knows these three facts, and has recourse to the remedy, does not experience the pain caused by the pricking of the thorn. Why? Because of the capability of recognizing the threefold nature of the situation. Similarly with regard to the case in question, the Attribute of goodness <term>Sattva: the quality of light, purity, and clarity</term> is the object of pain caused by that of foulness <term>Rajas: the quality of passion, activity, and pain</term>, the causer of *pain*—Why? Because the action of *paining* must fall on an *object*; and the action of paining (in the present instance) falls on the object of the attribute of Goodness; and not on the Cogniser (the Spirit) who is non-modifiable and devoid of action. Because the Spirit has objects presented to himself (by the Intellect), therefore when the attribute of goodness (the spiritual consciousness) is afflicted, the Spirit too, on account of His dependence on its form, becomes afflicted.

***

**Notes:—**(1) “*Such a spectacle resembling a magnet etc.*”:—It has already been explained under aphorism 4, Section I, how the Pure Intellect <note>original: "Sattvic Buddhi"</note>, though untouched by the sentient faculty, yet through its clearness, taking in the reflection thereof, appears as if endowed with intelligence, and as such experiences the various objects of sense. And it is for this reason that the spectator enjoys the pleasure etc. presented to himself by the consciousness which has taken the form of the particular—

->9<-

<summary>This page explains that suffering arises from the false identification between the unchanging Spirit and the active Intellect, using the analogy of a thorn and a shoe to illustrate how understanding this connection allows one to avoid pain. It clarifies that while the Spirit is inherently actionless, it appears to suffer because it reflects the disturbances occurring within the "Sattvic" or pure part of the mind.</summary>

<page-num>66</page-num>
-># THE PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA<-

objects of sense. And such a spiritual consciousness becomes his own form, as it were. Because it becomes the object of the spectator's experience and action, on account of its existing at that time, *in another form*; and so though naturally independent, it becomes dependent on the spirit, on account of its being for another's purpose. The author has added “in another form,” in anticipation of the objection that spiritual consciousness being naturally-illuminative, cannot be the object of any experience etc. The sense of the reply (implied by the epithet *<term>Anyasvarūpēna</term><note>original: "Anyasvarūpēna"; meaning "by another form" or "in a different state"</note>* etc.) is that if the consciousness were really of the form of intelligence, then only would it be self-illuminative; but as a matter of fact it “has its existence in a form which is other than that of intelligence”—i.e. the non-intelligent form—its apparent intelligence being due to the reflection therein of the spirit's intelligence. And as such it is quite fit to become the object of the spirit's experience.

(2) “*The attribute of goodness is the object of pain*” etc.—This is in anticipation of the following objection:—“In the example cited we have three members—(1) the pierced foot (2) the piercing thorn, and (3) the remedy, the wearing of shoes. But in the case in question, we do not find three members, because it is the <term>Buddhi</term><note>The higher intellectual faculty or "will" in Samkhya-Yoga philosophy</note> alone which is both the afflictor and the afflicted (as the pain is caused by the foulness of the <term>Buddhi</term> and is experienced also by the <term>Buddhi</term>, whose afflictions the spirit takes upon himself); and so there is no parallelism, between the instance and the point at issue”. The sense of the reply is that here also we have three members—(1) the afflictor, in the shape of foulness <note>referring to the quality of Rajas or passion</note>, (2) the afflicted in that of Goodness <note>referring to the quality of Sattva</note>, and (3) the remedy, <margin>| | !</margin> the separation of <term>Buddhi</term> from the Spirit—That is to say, though <term>Buddhi</term> is one, yet it is threefold, being made up of the three Attributes <note>The Gunas: Sattva (goodness), Rajas (activity), and Tamas (inertia)</note>. The reasons for not making the spirit, the *afflicted*, are next given; the chief of which is that, being unmodifiable, the spirit can never be the substrate of any action; and as such the action of 'pain' falls not upon Him, but on the modifying <term>Buddhi</term>; but in as much as the spirit depends for his experience, on the form of <term>Buddhi</term> wherein his intelligence is reflected,—He appears as if He himself were the *afflicted*.

**Sutra (18):—The spectacle is of the nature of illumination, activity and inertia; it consists of the elements and the sense-organs; it is for the purpose of experience and emancipation.**

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Yoga Sutras, specifically Book II, Sutra 18, defining the nature of the "Spectacle" or the objective world and its relationship to the three fundamental qualities of nature.</meta>

<page-num>67</page-num>
# BOOK II—SUTRA 18.

**Commentary:—**"**Goodness**" <note>original: "Sattva"</note> is of the nature of "**illumination**"; "**Foulness**" <note>original: "Rajas"</note> of the nature of "**activity**," and "**Darkness**" <note>original: "Tamas"</note> of the nature of "**inertia**." Thus, these Attributes <note>The three Gunas or fundamental qualities of nature</note> principally colored by one another, having the properties of (and capable of) connection and separation, with their respective forms accomplished by mutual help—and though mutually subservient yet, having their respective capabilities unmixed—capable of wielding their capabilities with regard to homogeneous and heterogeneous effects—having their respective proximity (functions) manifested at the time of their respective predominance (activity or operation)—and though *subservient qualities* <note>original: "gunas"</note> yet, having their existence inferred as included in the primary—having these faculties employed in the accomplishment of the spirit's ends—helping by mere proximity, and thus resembling a piece of magnet—and even in the absence of any predisposing causes (such as virtue and the rest), following in the wake of any one of themselves (the one that happens to be the predominating attribute with regard to the effect in question)—come to be denoted by the name of ***Primordial Matter*** <note>original: "Pradhâna"</note>; and this is also called the "**spectacle**" <note>The objective world of experience</note>.

This "**Spectacle**" "**consists of the elements and the sense-organs**";—that is, modifying in the form of the elements, that is, the earth and the rest, in their gross as well as subtle forms; and similarly modifying into the form of the sense-organs—the ear and the rest, in their gross as well as subtle forms. This is not without a purpose; on the contrary, it operates only with a view to a distinct purpose; consequently, the **spectacle** is "**for the purpose of the experience and emancipation**" of the Spirit <note>original: "Purusha"</note>. Of these, "**experience**" consists in the ascertainment of the forms of the desirable and the undesirable, not differentiated; and "**emancipation**" <note>Liberation or Kaivalya</note> is the ascertainment of the nature of the experience. Besides these two, there is no other perception. As is declared:—

> "Even on the existence of three active Attributes, he (the unwise one) recognizes the various natural dis-

(Question:) How are these two, experience and emancipa-

Intellect, are referred to the Spirit, because He is the enjoyer
of the fruits of both. Bondage is the non-accomplishment of
the soul’s purpose, belonging to the Intellect; and the accom-

Spirit; because he is the enjoyer of the fruits of these.

(3) “*At the time of their respective predominance*”—If the effect to
be produced be a divine body then the predominating attribute is that

of a bestial body, that of darkness; in each case the secondary causes
being the other two attributes. “**Primary Cause**” <gloss>Pradhâna</gloss> = “**State of being primary.**” <gloss>Pradhânatva</gloss>

Now begins the consideration of the determination of the different forms of the Attributes, which constitute the "Spectacle" <note>The manifest world of experience presented to the Spirit</note>.—

### **Aphorism (19):**—The specific and the unspecific, the solely mergent, and the non-mergent—are the stages of the Attributes.

**Commentary:**—Of these, Ether <note>Akasa</note>, Air, Fire, Water, and Earth—the five elements—are the *specific* (directly evolved effects) of the *unspecific* sound, touch, color, taste, and odor respectively. Similarly, the ear, the skin, the eye, the tongue, and the nostril—the sensory organs—as also speech, the hands, the feet, and the two excretory organs—the five organs of action—and lastly the universal *mind*—(all these eleven) are the specific effects of the unspecific form of Self-consciousness <note>Asmita or Ahamkara</note>. This sixteen-fold specific modification belongs to the Attributes. The "unspecific" are six: namely, the rudimentary sound, the rudimentary touch, the rudimentary color, the rudimentary taste, and the rudimentary odor—having respectively one, two, three, four, and five characteristics—these are the five "unspecific" elements; and the sixth is
