constitutes his poverty. This case is exactly similar (to that of the apparent birth and death of the Attributes.)

The solely mergent <note>original: "Alinga" or the Great Principle, Mahat</note>, existing in close proximity to the non-mergent <note>original: "Alinga" or Prakriti, the undifferentiated root-nature</note>, and originating therein, becomes divided therefrom—because of the infringibility of the order of sequence; for certainly the sequence is never broken; similarly the six unspecific modifications <note>the five subtle elements and the "I-maker" or ego</note> originating in the solely mergent become divided therefrom in accordance with the fixed order of sequence of the modifications. In the same manner, the elements and the sense-organs, originating in these unspecific ones, become divided therefrom. As has been declared above: "There is nothing beyond the specific"—*i. e.*, of the specific there is no further modification into any other object. Later on, we shall explain their modification into a condition characterised by virtue.

**Notes:—**(1) "*Having, one, two, &c.*" The sound has the characteristic of sound alone, touch that of touch and sound, colour that of colour touch and sound, taste that of taste and the preceding three, and lastly odour of odour and the preceding four.

(2) "*Mahat itself &c.*" <note>The "Great Principle" of cosmic intelligence</note>: 'Itself' is added in order to show the relative importance of *Mahat*.

(3) "*Beyond existence &c.*": The meaning is that the three attributes in equilibrium (which is Nature or *Prakriti*) are not capable of fulfilling the soul's ends, and as such Nature is said to be "beyond existence"—*i. e.*, without the capacity spoken of above; nor is it merely a hypothetical assumption, not really existing, hence it is said to be "beyond non-existence."

(4) "*Devadatta &c.*"—Just as the improvement or otherwise of the cattle is an attribute to the owner (who is not directly connected with the improvement or otherwise); so the origin and dissolution of the various modifications appear as if belonging to their cause, the Attributes.

(5) " *The infringibility of the order of sequence*"—The order is thus laid down in the Verses on Sankhya Philosophy <note>original: "Sânkhya-Kârikâ"</note>: <term>From Nature comes the Great Principle; from that, the I-maker (ego); and from this, the set of sixteen</term> <note>original: "प्रकृतेर्महांस्ततोऽहंकारस्तस्माद्गणश्च षोडशकः" (Prakṛter mahāṃs tato'haṃkāras tasmād gaṇaś ca ṣoḍaśakaḥ)</note> (From Nature, the Great Principle; from the Great Principle, self-consciousness; and from this

<summary>This page details the hierarchical evolution of matter and consciousness in Sankhya philosophy, explaining how the "Attributes" transition from a primal state into specific physical and sensory realities. It emphasizes that while the soul appears affected by these changes, the transformation actually occurs within the sequence of natural modifications.</summary>
<keywords>Prakriti, Mahat, Sankhya-Karika, Aphorism, Attributes, evolution of matter, unspecific modifications, self-consciousness, Devadatta analogy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical analysis of the relationship between the material world (the spectacle) and the pure soul (the spectator), following the discussion of the evolution of the intellect (Buddhi).</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>
-># PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA<-

last the sixteen <note>original: "the sixteen modifications or products of nature"</note>); and since this order can never be broken, the "solely-mergent," **Intellect** <note>original: "Buddhi (mahat)"</note> is the immediate modification of the Primordial matter.

---

The "**spectacle**" <note>The objective world or nature (Prakriti)</note> has been described; now commences the determination of the form of the "**spectator**" <note>The pure conscious self or soul (Purusha)</note>.

**Aphorism (20):—The spectator is absolute sentience, and though pure, still beholds ideas presented by the intellect.**

*Commentary:—* "**Absolute sentience**" = that is, the faculty of perception pure and simple, unalloyed with qualifications (illumination); this is the power that perceives consciousness itself. He is neither similar nor dissimilar to it. He is not similar. Why? Because consciousness, which relates to objects that are sometimes known and sometimes unknown, is subject to change; its objects—a cow, a jar, and the like—being sometimes known and sometimes unknown, prove that consciousness is modifying. The fact of the **Spirit** <note>The true Self or Purusha</note> having every object always known to him implies his unchanging character. Why? Because, (1) consciousness, which is also the object of the Spirit, cannot be both the perceiver and the thing perceived at the same time. Thus is established the fact of every object being always known to the Spirit; which further proves his unchanging nature. And further (secondly) consciousness exists for another's purpose, because it operates in conjunction with the sense-organs, the objects of the senses, and self-consciousness; the Spirit, on the other hand, exists for his own purpose. And (*thirdly*) again consciousness, being capable of perceiving all objects (whether they be peaceful, passionate, or dark), is composed of the **three Attributes** <note>original: "Gunas" (Sattva, Rajas, and Tamas), the fundamental qualities of nature</note>, and as such, is lacking in true sentience; the Spirit, on the other hand, is the spectator of these three Attributes. For these reasons the Spirit is not similar to consciousness. Then let it be dissimilar. No; it is not altogether dissimilar

<summary>This page defines the "Spectator" (the Soul or Spirit) as pure, unchanging consciousness, distinguishing it from the "Intellect" which is subject to change and composed of material qualities. It argues that the Spirit is the ultimate perceiver for whom the mind and senses operate.</summary>

<keywords>Yoga Philosophy, Spectator and Spectacle, Spirit, Intellect, Absolute Sentience, Three Attributes, Purusha, Buddhi, Consciousness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical commentary on the nature of the Seer (Spirit) and the Spectacle (Nature), specifically addressing how the Spirit perceives through the intellect without being changed by it.</meta>

<page-num>73</page-num>
<header>PART II—APHORISM 21.</header>

either. Why? Because “though pure, still it beholds intellected ideas”—*i. e.*, because it perceives the ideas belonging to consciousness; and thus *perceiving* appears similar to it, though really it is not so; as has been declared. “The faculty of the experiencing agent <note>The Purusha or Pure Spirit</note>, unmodifying and non-transferable, appears as if transferred to the modifying object <note>The Intellect or Buddhi</note>, and falls in with the function thereof; and (the same faculty),—endued only with an imitation of the Intellectual function which has been coloured by the touch of sentience,—and as such really untainted by the Intellectual functions,—is called the function of *knowledge*”—*Panchasikhacharya* <note>An ancient teacher of the Sankhya school of philosophy</note>.

**Notes:—“Though pure, still beholds &c.,” cf I—4.**

---

### **Aphorism (21):—Only for his purpose is the being of the spectacle.**

**Commentary:**—The spectacle becoming the object of the Spirit whose form is sentience pure and simple,—the being *i.e.*, the form of the spectacle is for his purpose <note>The "purpose" refers to the Spirit's experience and eventual liberation</note>. On the accomplishment of experience and emancipation, the (insentient) form of the spectacle, attaining to its existence through another form (the sentient form of the spirit reflected therein), is no longer seen by the Spirit. The destruction of the form would lead to the destruction of the spectacle; but it is not destroyed.

**Notes : (1) *The “destruction &c.”*** The objection means that the spectacle being one and the same for all spirits (on account of Nature being one), if it is destroyed in the case of one, it must be so in the case of all, and this would create the anomaly of the emancipation of a single spirit bringing about that of all others. The reply is that the spectacle *is not destroyed* for the emancipated Spirit; he only ceases to perceive it; and his sentience not being reflected in the Intellect, he ceases to feel any pleasure or pain &c., the results of his connection with the Attributes of the Intellect. Cf. *Sankhya—Karika* <note>A foundational text of the Sankhya school</note>.

<note>original: "दृष्टा मयैत्युपेक्षकएको दृष्टाऽहमित्युपरमत्यन्या इति ॥"</note>
“One (the Spirit) thinks, 'I have seen her,' and becomes indifferent; the other (Nature) thinks, 'I have been seen,' and ceases her activity.”

<sig>10</sig>

<summary>This page explains that while the Spirit remains pure and unchanging, it appears to take on the qualities of the intellect to gain knowledge. It also clarifies that when one individual achieves liberation, the material world (the spectacle) does not vanish for everyone else, but simply ceases to be perceived by that liberated soul.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical analysis of the relationship between the Seer (Spirit) and the Seen (Nature) within the Yoga system, specifically addressing why the material world persists even after an individual achieves enlightenment.</meta>

# YOGA PHILOSOPHY

Why?

**Aphorism (22):—Although it is dissolved for one whose purpose has been fulfilled, it is not dissolved for others, because it is common to many.**

**Commentary:—**Even though the "spectacle" <note>original: "drisya"; the objective world or nature</note> is dissolved in relation to the specific Spirit <note>original: "Purusha"; the pure consciousness or true self</note> who has achieved its goal, it is not entirely destroyed because it remains common to other spirits. Although it has ceased to exist for the "expert soul" <note>one who has attained liberation</note>, it has not yet fulfilled its purpose for those who are "inexpert" <note>those still bound by ignorance</note>. For these latter souls, it remains an object of perception; thus, it continues to maintain its existence through its relationship with others. In this way, the powers of the Seer and the Seen are eternal, and their everlasting connection is established. As it has been stated: "Since the objects that possess certain qualities are eternal, the connection between those qualities themselves is also eternal."

---

The next aphorism is introduced to define the nature of the "conjunction" <term>Samyoga: the temporary union between consciousness and matter</term> just mentioned:

**Aphorism (23):—Conjunction is the cause of realizing the true nature of both the power of the object and the power of its owner.**

**Commentary:—**The Spirit, who is the Lord <note>the master or owner of experience</note>, becomes joined with the spectacle for the purpose of perception. Therefore, the grasping of the objective world through this union is called *experience*; conversely, the realization of the true nature of the spectator is called *emancipation* <note>original: "Kaivalya"; liberation or absolute freedom</note>. Since this conjunction finds its ultimate end in perception, perception is said to be the cause of their eventual separation; and since "non-perception" <note>ignorance or lack of discrimination</note> is the opposite of perception, it is identified as the cause of the conjunction. In this context, perception itself is not the cause of liberation, but rather the absence of non-

<summary>This page explains that while the material world disappears for an enlightened soul, it persists for others, and defines the union of Spirit and Nature as the necessary means for both worldly experience and eventual liberation.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Sutras, Purusha, Prakriti, liberation, enlightenment, dualism, perception, Samyoga, spiritual ignorance</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical inquiry into the nature of bondage and liberation, specifically focusing on the definition of 'non-perception' as the root cause of the soul's entanglement with matter.</meta>

<page-num>75</page-num>
# CHAPTER II—SUTRA 23.

Perception leads to the absence of bondage; and this is *emancipation* <note>The Sanskrit term is 'Moksha', referring to the soul's release from the cycle of rebirth</note>. And because the existence of perception leads to its negation—non-perception, the cause of bondage—therefore the cognition of perception is said to be the cause of Isolation <note>original: 'Kaivalya'; the state of absolute independence and freedom for the soul</note>.

Now, what is this ‘non-perception’? 
(1) Is it a privileged office of the Attributes <note>original: 'Gunas'; the three fundamental qualities of nature: balance, activity, and inertia</note>? 
or (2) is it the non-production of the *principal* mind which has presented objects to its sentient Lord <note>The 'Lord' here refers to the individual soul or 'Purusha'</note>—that is, the want of perception in his spectacle? 
or (3) is it the fact of the attributes resting in the purpose (experience and emancipation)? 
or (4) Ignorance with its mind being suppressed (that is, becoming similar to its cause, Nature), is the productive seed (in the form of Latent Impressions <note>original: 'Vâsanâ'; the subconscious inclinations from past actions</note>) of its mind (and thus in this case non-perception being the residual longing of Ignorance)? 
or (5) on the destruction of the impression <note>original: 'Sanskâra'</note> of fixity <note>original: 'sthiti'</note>, the manifestation of the impression of motion? In this connection it has been declared:

> “The Primary Matter <note>original: 'Pradhâna'; the unmanifested state of nature</note>, continuing in its fixity, and as such not producing any modifications, would become the non-Primary Matter (that is, the non-cause, the inefficient cause); similarly continuing in its mobility, its modifications would become eternal, and thus too would become non-Primary Matter (because the character of the Primary Matter consists in producing modifications and thence affording experience for the spirit, and in time dissolving it, and bringing about his emancipation). It is only when it operates both ways (both by fixity and mobility), that it is called Primary Matter, not otherwise. The same consideration holds respecting other causes.” 

Or (6) the power of the spectacle (experience etc.) alone is non-perception—so say some; on the strength of the Scripture <note>original: 'Sruti'; specifically the Vedas or Upanishads</note>: “The operation of the Primary Matter is for the information of the soul.”

The spirit capable of apprehending all that is to be cognised, does not perceive anything, prior to the operation of Nature; and

It emphasizes that the Primary Matter (Pradhâna) must fluctuate between stability and change to fulfill its purpose of providing both experience and eventual liberation for the soul.</summary>

then too the spectacle <note>The "spectacle" refers to *Prakriti* or the material world/nature</note>, which is capable of producing all effects, is not perceived; and this is non-perception. (7) Some people assert that non-perception is the property of both (the spectator spirit, and the spectacle). In this case the perception of the spectacle, though constituting its very nature, is yet recognised as the property of the spectacle only through the cognition of the spirit (because without the reflection of the sentience of the spirit, no conscious perception is possible); similarly though the perception does not constitute the nature of the spirit, yet it appears to be the property of the spirit, through the cognition of the spectacle (because without the reflecting spectacle no perception is possible). (8) Lastly, some people assert non-perception to be the consciousness of the perception.

All these alternatives are mere fancies of the scientific mind. In this connection, the great number of alternatives has a common object in reference to the conjunction of all the souls with the attributes <note>The "attributes" or *Gunas* are the three fundamental qualities—balance, activity, and inertia—that make up the material world</note>.

### *Aphorism* (**24**) :—its cause is **Ignorance**;

**Commentary:**—that is, the residual longings of misconceived ideas. <term>Spiritual consciousness</term> <note>original: "Chitta"</note>, coloured by the residual longings of misconceived notions, attains to the effectual condition of <term>spiritual discrimination</term> <note>The ability to distinguish between the eternal self and the temporary material world</note>, and again returns (to its operations) with full-pledged powers. When however it ends in spiritual discrimination, then it attains its highest limit of effectiveness and having its office thus fulfilled it ceases to act; and hence being devoid of perception, does not return to its operation, there being no cause of bondage.

A certain person (the Nihilist <note>The "Nihilist" likely refers to a follower of the Madhyamaka school of Buddhism, who argues that if the mind is suppressed, nothing remains.</note>) jokes at emancipation through the example of an impotent husband:—"The impotent person being exhorted by his young wife in this manner—My dear, my sister has children, wherefore have I not got any?—replies to her thus: 'when I am dead, then will I produce children for thee.' In the same manner the knowledge when present not accomplishing the suppression of the mind, what hope is there of its accomplishing it when it itself will have been destroyed?" In reply to this says a certain sect of the masters (of our Science <note>The "Science" refers to Yoga philosophy.</note>): "The suppression of the consciousness itself constitutes emancipation, the said suppression resulting from the absence of the cause of non-perception,—this non-perception, the cause of bondage, disappearing on (right) perception."

Thus then it is the suppression of the mind that constitutes emancipation; and as such, wherefore this inopportune mental aberration (of the Nihilist.)

**Com:—**From the absence of non-perception, there is absence of the conjunction of the spirit and spiritual consciousness,—that is to say, the absolute cessation of bondage. This is "avoidance," and "it is the isolation of the spectator" —that is, the non-mixture of the spirit, the non-conjunction

Now, what is the means for getting at this avoidance?
*Aphorism (26):—* **Undisturbed discriminative knowledge is the means of avoidance.**

> *Commentary:—* “**Discriminative knowledge**” is the recognition of the difference between the Spirit <note>original: "Purusha"</note> and the Attributes <note>original: "Gunas"</note>. And this knowledge becomes disturbed by the non-cessation of false knowledge. When however this false knowledge has its seeding faculty burnt up, and as such ceases to procreate, then flows the lucid current of the discriminative knowledge belonging to Goodness (<term>sattva</term> <note>The quality of purity, light, and harmony</note>), free from the impurities of pain, and abiding in supreme wisdom, the high pedestal of <term>Vasikâra</term> <note>The highest stage of dispassion where one is no longer thirsting for sensory objects</note> Dispassion (*see above*). And this undisturbed discriminative knowledge is the cause of avoidance, whence follows the burning up of the seeding faculty of false knowledge, which thus ceases to procreate. This is the highway of Emancipation, the means of the avoidance (of pain).

Such is the fourfold effected end of consciousness. The end of mind again is threefold: (1) The office of spiritual consciousness has been fulfilled; (2) the attributes <note>original: "gunas"; the fundamental qualities of nature—stability, activity, and clarity</note> devoid of rest like stones fallen from the mountain-top, turned towards dissolution into their cause, disappear together therewith; and of these dissolved ones there is no more production, for want of any purpose—(3) in this state, the spirit, outgrowing all attributive connection, pure and shining in his own pristine form, becomes isolated <note>original: "kaivalya"; the state of absolute freedom or liberation</note>.

The spirit, witnessing this sevenfold consciousness, to its utmost stage, is called “Adept.” And even on the retrograde procreation <note>the process of the mind dissolving back into its primal cause</note> of the mind, he is emancipated and becomes “adept,” because of his having gone beyond the range of the attributes.

As the accessories come to be practised, the impurity becomes gradually attenuated; and as this decay, so is the

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the effects of Yoga practice on consciousness and the classification of causality in Indian philosophy.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
# PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA

->YOGA-DARASANA<-
<note>original: "Yoga-Darsana," referring to the systematic study or "vision" of Yoga philosophy.</note>

The increase of the brightness of consciousness depends upon the order of the decay of impurities; and this brightness goes on gradually increasing “till discriminative knowledge results”—that is to say, until the recognition of the true nature of the Spirit <note>original: "Purusha," the pure eternal consciousness</note> and the Attributes <note>original: "Gunas," the three fundamental qualities of nature: harmony, activity, and inertia</note>.

The practice of the accessories of Yoga is the cause of the disjunction of impurity, just as an axe is the cause of the separation of the object being cut; and it is the cause of the attainment of discriminative knowledge, just as virtue is the cause of pleasure; and in no other way can it be considered a cause. How many are these causes said to be in the sciences? There are nine—for example:

1. <term>**Originative cause**</term> <note>original: "Utpattikāraṇa," that which brings something into existence.</note>
2. <term>**Fixitive cause**</term> <note>original: "Sthitikaraṇa," that which maintains or stabilizes a state.</note>
3. <term>**Manifestive cause**</term> <note>original: "Abhivyaktikaraṇa," that which makes something already present visible or known.</note>
4. <term>**Modificatory cause**</term> <note>original: "Vikāra-karaṇa," that which changes one thing into another.</note>
5. <term>**Cognitive cause**</term> <note>original: "Pratyayakaraṇa," that which leads to a mental concept or realization.</note>
6. <term>**Approaching cause**</term> <note>original: "Āptikaraṇa," that which leads to the attainment of a goal.</note>
7. <term>**Disjunctive cause**</term> <note>original: "Viyogakaraṇa," that which separates or removes something.</note>
8. <term>**Differentiative cause**</term> <note>original: "Anyatvakaraṇa," that which creates a distinction or change in state.</note>
9. <term>**Upholding cause**</term> <note>original: "Dhritikaraṇa," that which supports the existence of another.</note>

To exemplify these severally:
(1) The mind is the cause of consciousness; <insert>!</insert>
(2) The soul’s purpose is the cause of the mind, and food is the cause of the body;
(3) Light is the cause of the consciousness of <insert>form or colour</insert>;
(4) Other objects are the cause of the mind's changes, and fire is the cause of the food to be cooked;
(5) The knowledge of smoke is the cause of the knowledge of fire;
(6) The practice of yogic accessories is the cause of discriminative knowledge;
(7) The same practice is the cause of the removal of impurity;
(8) The goldsmith is the cause of the gold's transformation; and similarly of <insert>a single mental function</insert> [= <term>mental modification</term> <note>original: "vritti," a wave or ripple in the mind-stuff</note>], Ignorance is the cause of its stupefaction; aversion is the cause of its painfulness, desire is the cause of its pleasurableness, and true knowledge is the cause of its impartiality;
(9) The body is the cause of the sense-organs, and *vice versa*, the great elements are the cause of the bodies, and these latter uphold one another—the animal, the human, and the divine bodies upholding one another.

These are the nine causes. As occasion presents itself, these are to be applied to other objects also. But the causal efficiency of the practice of the accessories of yoga—

<summary>This page explains how Yoga practice leads to discriminative knowledge by removing impurities and provides a detailed nine-fold classification of causality in Indian philosophy.</summary>

is only twofold (that is, the *disjunctive* <note>original: "vibhāga"; referring to the separation of spirit from matter</note> and the *procuring* <note>original: "prāpti"; the attainment of liberation</note>, as mentioned above, before the enunciation of the forms of causality).

The accessories of yoga are next described:

**Aphorism (29):—Restraint (or Forbearance), Observance, Posture, Regulation of Breath, Abstraction, Concentration, Contemplation, and Meditation—are the eight accessories.**

*Commentary:*—We shall now describe in due order, the form and practice of these.

---

**Aphorism (30):—Forbearance consists in harmlessness, truth, abstinence from theft, continence and freedom from avarice.**

*Commentary:*—Of these **"harmlessness"** <note>the Sanskrit term is "Ahimsa"</note>—in no way and at no time wishing ill to any living being. The following restraints and observances have all of them abstinence for their root, and being included in its accomplishment, are performed only for the proper performance thereof;—that is, they are practised only in order to clarify the aforesaid abstinence. It has been thus declared: "As the Priest <note>original: "Brâhmaṇa"; a member of the highest Hindu caste, traditionally a scholar or teacher</note> performs many penances, so does he become alienated from the cause of harm to others and thus brings about the same harmlessness duly clarified."

**"Truth"**—consists in word and mind corresponding to facts—that is word and mind both tallying with objects as seen, inferred or heard (from authentic sources). A word is spoken for the transference of one's thought to another, and as such if it is not deceptive or mistaken, or devoid of comprehension (unable to be comprehended by the listener), (then it is

<summary>This page introduces the eight limbs of Yoga and begins a detailed explanation of the first limb, Forbearance (Yama), specifically focusing on non-violence and truthfulness.</summary>

true);—it is so only when it is used for the good of others, and not for their evil. If being spoken as such (as a truth) it leads to the injury of others, then it would not be truth; it would be a sin; and by this semblance of virtue (the agent) would suffer endless trouble. Therefore one ought to tell the truth with a due consideration of the good of all beings. <note>The author emphasizes that truth is not merely factual accuracy, but must be guided by the principle of non-harm (Ahimsa).</note>

Theft consists in the acceptance of objects from others, in a manner contrary to the prescribed forms (of gift). The abstinence from this in the shape of absence of desire therefor, is <term>Non-stealing (Astéya)</term>.

“Continence”—the control of the secret generative organs. <note>Refers to Brahmacharya, or the conservation of vital energy.</note>

“Freedom from avarice” consists in the non-acceptance of any objects, on account of a due sense of the discrepancies therein, in the shape of trouble attendant upon its acquirement, protection, waste or consumption, attachment, and malevolence (towards other competitors). <note>This refers to Aparigraha, or non-possessiveness.</note>

Notes :—“Truth”—Compare the Laws of Manu:

> ->Speak the truth, speak what is pleasant, but do not speak an unpleasant truth.<-
> ->And do not speak a pleasant falsehood; this is the eternal law.<-
<note>original: "सत्यं ब्रूयात् प्रियं ब्रूयान् न्रूयात् सत्यमप्रियम् ॥ प्रियञ्च नानृतं ब्रूयात् एष धर्मः सनातनः ॥"</note>

---

These, however, are—

### ***Aphorism* (31) :—the Great Vow, when in all stages, the above are not conditioned by (are irrespective of) class, place, time and convention.**

*Commentary:* ---of these, harmlessness conditioned (or limited) by class—the killing of fish, and nothing else, by fishermen (which is wrongly tolerated by people). “Conditioned by place”—as ‘I will not kill in a sacred place’; “conditioned by time”—‘I will not kill on the 14th day of the fortnight <note>Referring to specific lunar phases or holy days in the Hindu calendar.</note> or on other

<summary>This page defines the ethical restraints of non-stealing, continence, and non-possessiveness, concluding that these virtues become a "Great Vow" when practiced universally without exceptions for status, location, or timing.</summary>

<header>CHAPTER II—SUTRA 32.</header>

sacred days; and lastly the harmlessness “conditioned by convention,” [belonging to one who is above the three kinds of “slaughter” just mentioned],—as “I shall kill (animals) for the sake of the gods and the Brahmanas <note>The priestly class in Hindu society</note>, and not otherwise;” or again killing is allowable for the warrior caste, in battle, and nowhere else. Harmlessness and the rest, not conditioned by these—class, place, time and convention—are to be always practised, in all stages—*i. e.*, with regard to all objects; and thus being free from any sort of restriction, they are called the Great Penance <note>original: "Mahavrata"; the supreme universal vow</note>, belonging to all stages.

### **Sutra (32):—The observances are—purification, contentment, penance, study and devotion to the Lord.**

***Commentary:—***Of these, “purification” the *external*, consists in the removal of impure objects by means of clay and water &c., and the *internal*, in the removal of the impurities of the mind. “Contentment”—the absence of any desire for acquiring things other than those that are near at hand. “Penance”—the bearing of the “pair of opposites,”—such as hunger and thirst, heat and cold, sitting and standing, complete and formal silence (the former consisting in not expressing one’s ideas even by gestures, and the latter in mere absence of articulate speech); and the penances proper, are the *Krichhra* <note>A specific form of bodily purification involving fasting</note>, the *Chândrâyana* <note>A fast regulated by the waxing and waning of the moon</note> &c., “Study”—the study of the sciences dealing with emancipation, or the repetition of the *Pranava* <note>The sacred syllable 'Om'</note>. “Devotion to the Lord”—the offering of all actions to the supreme and venerable. “Seated on the bed or wandering on the road, if he is calm on having his doubts and misgivings removed, and looking upon the destruction of the seed of metempsychosis <note>The transmigration of the soul or reincarnation</note>,—such a one would be the eternally emancipated, the experiencer of the joys of immortality.” In this

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the practice of Yoga, specifically focusing on overcoming mental obstacles and moral failings through the technique of 'Pratipaksha Bhavana' or pondering the opposites.</meta>

<page-num>84</page-num>
-># PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA<-

In this connection it has been declared: “From that comes the realization of the individual self <note>original: "retrogade sentience," referring to the inward-turning consciousness</note> and the removal of obstacles” (*Yoga-sutra*, I-29).

Of these, when Restraints <term>Yamas: ethical rules like non-violence</term> and Penances <term>Niyamas: personal observances like purity</term>—

**Sutra (33):—are obstructed by questionable thoughts, the constant pondering of the opposites is necessary.**

*Commentary*:—When the seeker <note>original: "Brâhmaṇa," here referring to a spiritual aspirant</note> is obsessed by questionable thoughts—harmfulness and the rest (to be described in the next aphorism, such as “I will kill my enemy,” “I shall tell a lie,” “I shall take his property,” “I will be unfaithful with his wife,” “I will seize his holdings”)—he becomes obstructed by the burning fever of these questionable thoughts leading to evil ways. The student <note>original: "neophyte"</note> is to ponder over the opposites thereof—that is, he should think thus: "Having been scorched in the terrible fire of the cycle of rebirth <term>metempsychosis: the transmigration of souls</term>, I have taken refuge in the virtues of yoga, through charity and love towards all beings; so if I return to these questionable paths after having once given them up, then I would be acting like a miserable dog—returning to what I have discarded, just as a dog licks up its own vomit." The same process is to be applied to the other aphorisms as well (that is, those treating of purification and the rest).

**Sutra (34):—The questionable thoughts are violence and the rest;—(and whether these are) done, caused to be done, or approved of; (whether) motivated by greed, anger, or delusion; (whether) slight, moderate, or excessive,—they always result in endless pain and ignorance—thus is the pondering upon their opposites.**

<summary>This page explains the Yoga-sutra's method for overcoming negative impulses by consciously reflecting on their harmful consequences and viewing them as a return to a discarded, lower state of being. It categorizes negative actions by their intensity and motivation, emphasizing that all forms of harm lead to suffering.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga-sutra, ethical restraints, non-violence, mental discipline, cycle of rebirth, greed, anger, delusion, moral philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Yoga Sutras, specifically focusing on the classification of negative actions (vitarka) and the practice of "pondering the opposites" (pratipaksha bhavana) to overcome them.</meta>

# CHAPTER II—SUTRA 34.

**Commentary:** Slaughter <note>original: "himsa" or violence</note> is threefold, being either done personally, caused to be done by others, or abetted <note>approved of or encouraged</note>—each of these again is threefold: (1) caused by covetousness, that is, for the sake of the flesh or the skin; (2) by anger—that is, thinking the object to be one's injurer; (3) by delusion—that is, thinking that virtue will result therefrom (such as the slaughter in sacrifices). Covetousness, anger, and delusion too are threefold: being slight, moderate, or excessive. Thus there are 27 forms of slaughter. The *slight, etc.*, again are each threefold: the slight-slight, the moderate-slight, and the excessive-slight; and again the slight-moderate, the moderate-moderate, the excessive-moderate; and lastly the slight-excessive, the moderate-excessive, and the excessive-excessive. Thus slaughter becomes eighty-one-fold. Again, in accordance with decision, doubt, and collectivity: (1) when it is decided that a fish is to be killed; (2) when it is doubtful whether the fish or the goat is to be killed; and (3) when both of them are to be killed—it becomes innumerable, because of the innumerability of living beings. The same divisions are to be applied to untruth and the rest <note>referring to the other four Yamas or ethical restraints: falsehood, theft, incontinence, and greed</note> also.

> “These questionable thoughts <note>original: "vitarka," referring to harmful impulses</note> have pain and ignorance for their endless fruits”—thus is to be the pondering upon their opposites. For example, the killer first of all cripples the powers of the animal to be slaughtered (by binding it fast to some pole, etc.), then gives it pain by letting fall some weapon, and then deprives it of its life even. Then, from the crippling of the animal's power results the crippling of the powers of the killer's own resources <term>accoutrements: the people and objects that support one's life</term>, both animate (wife, etc.) and inanimate (bed, food, etc.); from the animal's pain results his own misery in hell, and during future brute existence <note>rebirth as an animal</note> (the necessary consequence of slaughter); and the loss of life leads to his continuous existence on the brink of death (on account of some incurable disease) during which state, though wishing to be dead, he still breathes, on account of there being a fixed time for the fruition of pain <note>the period during which one must endure the karmic consequences of past actions</note> (as death would save him from further suffering).

<summary>This page details the complex categorization of violence (slaughter) into eighty-one or even infinite variations based on intent and intensity, while explaining the karmic consequences of such actions.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Sutras, non-violence, karma, vitarka, slaughter, covetousness, delusion, fruition of pain</keywords>

<page-num>87</page-num>
# CHAPTER II—SUTRA 40.

*Sutra (38):*—**When continence <note>original: "Brahmacharya"; refers to the preservation of vital energy and self-restraint</note> is firmly established, there is the attainment of vigor.**

*Commentary:*—By the attainment of this, the Yogi <note>a practitioner of Yoga</note> raises to perfection his irrepressible qualities and powers; and having reached this perfection, he is able to transmit wisdom to his disciples.

---

*Sutra (39):*—**When non-covetousness <note>original: "Aparigraha"; the virtue of non-possessiveness or greedlessness</note> is firmly established, there arises knowledge of the "how" and "wherefore" of one's births.**

*Commentary:*—One should add the phrase "accrue to him." The Yogi asks: “Who was I? How was I? What is this present birth? How does it function? What shall I be in the future? How shall I be?”—Such longings to understand the nature of his past, present, and future incarnations are naturally satisfied through this practice.

---

->Now we speak with regard to the Observances <note>original: "Niyamas"; the second limb of Yoga consisting of personal disciplines</note>.<-

*Sutra (40):*—**From the practice of purity <note>original: "Saucha"; cleanliness of both body and mind</note>, there arises a distaste for one’s own body and a cessation of physical contact with others.**

*Commentary:*—By feeling a sense of detachment toward his own body, the Yogi begins the process of purification; however, upon discovering the body to be full of imperfections and impurities, he loses all worldly attachment to it and thus becomes a "self-controlled seeker" <term>Yati: a person who has renounced worldly life to practice asceticism</term>. Furthermore, there is a "non-intercourse with others"—observing the true nature of the physical form and desiring to transcend his own body (finding it cannot be truly purified even when washed with earth and water), how could such a person seek physical contact with the inherently impure bodies of others?

---

# YOGA PHILOSOPHY <note>original: "YOGA-DARASANA"</note>

And also

### **Aphorism (41):—Purity, cheerfulness, one-pointedness, mastery over the senses, and readiness for the vision of the Self—all these arise from the refinement of the mental essence.** <note>original: "Sutra... thinking principle (Lit. Goodness)" refers to Sattva-buddhi, the highest quality of the mind.</note>

*Commentary*:—From the practice of internal and external purification results the "purity" of the mind; from that comes a natural cheerfulness; from that comes the power of concentration; from that comes the mastery over the sensory organs; and finally, the mind becomes fit for the direct perception of the Soul. All these benefits follow once the habit of purity is firmly established.

---

### **Aphorism (42):—From contentment, unsurpassed happiness.**

*Commentary*:—As it has been stated in the sacred texts: "Whatever worldly pleasure one might desire, or whatever great celestial joy exists in the heavens—none of these are worth even a sixteenth part of the happiness that comes from the total ending of desire."

---

### **Aphorism (43):—From self-discipline, as impurities are destroyed, come extraordinary powers of the body and the senses.** <note>original: "penance" (tapas) refers here to disciplined practices like fasting or heat-endurance that refine the physical and mental form.</note>

*Commentary*:—As soon as the practice of self-discipline is perfected, it burns away the covering of impurity. Once this veil is removed, the practitioner gains extraordinary bodily powers (such as the ability to become weightless <note>original: "Buoyancy"</note> and others) and heightened sensory powers (such as hearing or seeing across vast distances).

---

### **Aphorism (44):—From sacred study, communion with one's chosen deity.** <note>original: "study" (svadhyaya) includes the recitation of mantras and the reading of spiritual scriptures.</note>

**Commentary:—**To one given to study, the Gods, the sages and the <term>perfected ones</term> <note>original: "perfectons" (Siddhas); beings who have attained semi-divine status and supernatural powers through spiritual practice</note>, all become perceptible, and are ready to assist in his actions.

### Aphorism (45):—From devotion to the Lord, the accomplishment of meditation.

**Commentary:—**Meditation is accomplished for one who has offered up all his connections to the Lord; and by this accomplishment, he rightly knows all that is good for him, in other places, in other bodies and at other times; thence his consciousness perceives all things rightly.

Restraints and observances, together with the attendant powers, have been described; we now speak of Posture and the rest.

### Aphorism (46):—Posture is that which is steady and easy.

**Commentary:—**And there are: the (1) *Lotus posture*, (2) *Heroic posture*, (3) *Decent posture*, (4) *Auspicious posture* <note>original: "Svastikásana"; named after the symbol of well-being</note>, (5) *Staff posture*, (6) *Supported posture*, (7) *Bedstead posture*, (8) *Seated heron posture*, (9) *Seated elephant posture*, (10) *Seated camel posture*, (11) *Evenly balanced posture*, (12) *Steady and pleasant posture*—that is, in accordance with one's pleasure, and so forth.

### Aphorism (47):—By slackening of effort, and the contemplation of the infinite.

# YOGA-PHILOSOPHY <note>original: "Yoga-Darsana"</note>

***Commentary:—*** "Supply—" "it (posture) is accomplished." On the cessation of effort, posture is accomplished; and there is no quivering of the body. Or, the mind contemplating on the Infinite <note>The "Infinite" here refers to the cosmic serpent Ananta, who supports the earth with effortless stability.</note> accomplishes Posture.

---

***Aphorism (48):—*** <note>original: "Sutra"</note> **From that, one is no longer troubled by the pairs of opposites.**

***Commentary:—*** By the mastery of posture, the Practitioner <note>original: "Yogi"</note> is not distressed by the pairs of opposites—such as heat and cold, pleasure and pain, and so forth.

---

***Aphorism (49):—*** **Following this, Breath Control—the cessation of the movements of inhalation and exhalation.**

***Commentary:—*** Once posture is mastered, there follows the Regulation of Breath <note>original: "Pranayama"</note>, which consists in the suspension of the natural flow of inhalation (drawing in the breath) and exhalation (releasing the breath).

And that again

***Sutra (50):—*** **is an external, internal, or suspended movement—regulated by location, duration, and count—becoming prolonged and subtle.**

***Commentary:—*** The suspension of breath after exhaling is the external movement; the suspension after inhaling is the internal movement; the absence of both movements is the third, the suspended or stationary function, achieved through a single effort. Just as a drop of water placed on a heated stone slab shrivels and vanishes from all sides, so too is the sudden stillness of both inhalation and exhalation.

<summary>This page describes the transition from mastering physical posture to the practice of breath control, explaining how stability leads to immunity from physical discomforts like heat and cold.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Sutras, Asana, Pranayama, breath control, pairs of opposites, meditation, physical stability</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of Pranayama (breath control) from the previous section, specifically detailing the metrics of the third stage and introducing the fourth stage of practice.</meta>

# CHAPTER II—APHORISM 51.

All these three are “regulated by place”—meaning, “This particular location is its focus”; “regulated by time”—as defined by specific durations; and “regulated by number”—meaning the first cycle consists of a specific count of inhalations and exhalations. In the same way, the second cycle of the restrained breath consists of a specific count; and likewise the third. Whether the breath is gentle, moderate, or intense, it follows this regulation by number. This <term>Regulation of Breath</term><note>Sanskrit: Pranayama</note>, when practiced, becomes either prolonged or refined.

---

### Aphorism (51): The fourth type of breath control transcends the distinction between the external and internal spheres.

**Commentary:** The “external sphere” (regulated by place, time, and number) having been mastered, and the “internal sphere” (regulated in the same manner) also having been mastered—both having become prolonged and refined—there follows a fourth kind of <term>Regulation of Breath</term>. This fourth stage is preceded by the others and consists of mastering the various phases of stopping the breath. 

The third type of breath control is a sudden interruption of breath that is not directed toward any specific object; it is regulated by place, time, and number from the moment it begins, becoming prolonged and refined. In contrast, the fourth type is an interruption that follows the mastery of the first two stages. It is achieved by mastering the various phases of breath through a deep awareness of the movements of exhalation and inhalation. This is the primary difference between the third and fourth types: the third is achieved by a single effort without focusing on a specific object, while the fourth is achieved through long-continued effort and the recognition of the breath's movement.

**Note:** 
The 1st, the *External* or Exhalation, is called **Rechaka**.
The 2nd, the *Internal* or Inhalation, is called **Puraka**.
The 3rd is the 1st kind of **Kumbhaka** <note>Breath retention</note>.
And the 4th is the 2nd kind of **Kumbhaka**.

---

<summary>This page defines the metrics of breath control—place, time, and number—and distinguishes between the third and fourth stages of Pranayama, focusing on the transition from effortful restraint to refined mastery.</summary>
<keywords>Pranayama, breath control, Rechaka, Puraka, Kumbhaka, inhalation, exhalation, meditation metrics, Yoga Sutras</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of the Yoga Sutras, specifically focusing on the benefits of breath control and the definition of sensory withdrawal.</meta>

<page-num>92</page-num>
# THE PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA

**Aphorism (52): Then the veil covering the light is destroyed.**

*Commentary:* For the practitioner of Yoga <note>Yogi</note> who practices the regulation of breath <term>Pranayama: the formal practice of controlling the breath to influence the flow of vital energy</term>, the karmic action that veils true knowledge is destroyed. This is described as follows: "The web of great delusion that covers the illuminating quality of goodness <term>Sattva: the quality of purity, light, and harmony</term> turns the mind toward harmful deeds." This action, which covers the light and causes the cycle of rebirth, is weakened by the practice of breath regulation and decays moment by moment. As it has been declared: "There is no spiritual discipline <term>Tapas: literally 'heat' or 'penance'; rigorous self-discipline</term> superior to the regulation of breath; for from it results the removal of impurities and the shining forth of knowledge."

---

->And also,<-

**Aphorism (53): The mind gains the fitness for focused concentration.**

*Commentary:* This result also comes from the regulation of breath alone, in accordance with the earlier aphorism: "Or the mind reaches stability by the expulsion and retention of breath" (*Yoga Sutras*, Book I, Verse 34).

---

->Now, what is Sensory Withdrawal?<-

**Aphorism (54): Sensory withdrawal <term>Pratyahara: the fifth limb of yoga, involving the retreat of the senses from external objects</term> is the imitation, as it were, by the senses of the mind's own nature, by withdrawing themselves from their respective objects.**

*Commentary:* When the senses no longer apply themselves to their respective objects <note>such as the eye seeking color or the ear seeking sound</note>, there results an "imitation, as it were, of the original nature of the mind." This means that when the mind is stilled, the senses also become stilled. They do not require any other external methods of control. <note>The author argues that once the mind is mastered, the senses follow automatically, like bees following a queen.</note>

<summary>This page explains how breath control removes the mental veil of ignorance and prepares the mind for concentration, while defining sensory withdrawal as the state where the senses mimic the stillness of a controlled mind.</summary>

<keywords>Pranayama, breath regulation, Sattva, purity, Pratyahara, sensory withdrawal, mental discipline, Yoga Sutras of Patanjali</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the limbs of Yoga, specifically transitioning from the practice of Pratyahara (Abstraction) to its result: the mastery of the senses.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>
# CHAPTER II—APHORISM 55.

But upon the suppression of the mind, the suppression of the senses follows directly. Just as bees fly along with the king of the bees and rest when he has rested, so when the mind is suppressed, the senses become mastered—and this constitutes Abstraction <note>original: "Pratyahara"; the withdrawal of the senses from external objects</note>.

---

**Aphorism (55):—From that comes the complete mastery of the senses.**

**Commentary:—**“The mastery of the senses consists in the lack of longing for various sensory objects, such as sound and the rest”—so say some. Longing is attachment, that which draws the practitioner away from their spiritual progress. Others say that sensory engagement is allowable if it does not contradict the sacred scriptures <note>referring to the Vedas or Shastras</note>; thus, engagement free from scriptural violation constitutes mastery. Others again say that mastery is the optional communion with objects—that is, the senses only turn toward and touch objects when the practitioner desires it. Others assert that the mastery of the senses consists in the perception of sound and the rest while remaining free from pleasure and pain, achieved through the absence of attachment and aversion. 

However, Jaigishavya <note>An ancient mythical sage and master of Yoga</note> says it is the total absence of sensation due to intense concentration. From that practice of abstraction follows this highest form of mastery—namely, the suppression of the mind. Once the senses are mastered in this way, the Yogi <note>A practitioner of Yoga</note> does not require any further methods or external efforts to maintain control, unlike other lesser forms of sensory restraint.

---

<vocab>Aphorism, Abstraction, Mastery, Concentration, Mind, Senses, Yogi, Jaigishavya</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the highest form of sensory control (Pratyahara), comparing the relationship between the mind and senses to a king bee and his swarm. It concludes that true mastery is the automatic result of mental suppression rather than forced effort.</summary>
<keywords>Pratyahara, sensory mastery, Jaigishavya, mental suppression, Yoga Sutras, detachment</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the internal limbs of Yoga, specifically defining the collective practice of Sanyama and its application across different levels of consciousness.</meta>

# CHAPTER III—APHORISM 6.

These three—Concentration, Contemplation, and Meditation—together constitute *Sanyama* <note>Sanyama: A technical term for the combined application of the three highest stages of yoga practice on a single object.</note>:

**Aphorism (4):—The three together constitute Sanyama.**

*Commentary:*—The three means having a single object are called **Sanyama**, which thus is the technical name for the three taken collectively.

---

**Aphorism (5):—From success therein results the lucidity of consciousness.**

*Commentary:*—By mastering **Sanyama**, the lucidity of meditative consciousness results. As **Sanyama** becomes established, so does meditative consciousness become clear and luminous.

---

**Aphorism (6):—It is applied by stages.**

*Commentary:*—**Sanyama** is applied to a subsequent stage only when it has mastered the preceding one. **Sanyama** can never attain to the final (highest) stage by jumping over the intermediate stages without conquering the latter. (The condition of the perceived object being the lowest stage, that of the organ of perception the intermediate stage, and that of the perceiver, the final stage). And without its attaining to the highest stage, how can there be lucidity of consciousness? For one who has, through divine favor, conquered the higher stages, it is not necessary to practice **Sanyama** with regard to the preceding ones. Why? Because the objects of the lower stages will have already been understood through the higher ones. In the knowledge of the order of the stages—that this stage preceded that—yoga is the only guide. Why? Because it has been declared: “Yoga is to be recognized by yoga; from yoga does yoga proceed; one who is not confused by yoga, he ever delights in yoga.” <note>original: "Yogena yogo jñātavyo yogo yogāt pravartate | atapramattastu yogena sa yoge ramate ciram ||" This verse emphasizes that the practice itself is the teacher of the practitioner.</note>

---

<summary>This page defines Sanyama as the unified practice of concentration, contemplation, and meditation, explaining that it must be mastered in sequential stages to achieve true mental clarity.</summary>
<keywords>Sanyama, Concentration, Contemplation, Meditation, Yoga Sutras, consciousness, stages of yoga, spiritual mastery</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of the Yoga Sutras, specifically focusing on the transition from external practices to internal mental states and the mechanics of the "seedless" or abstract state of meditation.</meta>

# YOGA PHILOSOPHY <note>original: "YOGA-DARSANA"</note>

### *Aphorism (7):*—The three are more intimate (directly effective) than the former ones.

*Commentary:*—The three—Concentration, Contemplation, and Meditation <note>These refer to Dharana, Dhyana, and Samadhi, the internal limbs of Yoga</note>—are more directly effective in accomplishing concrete meditation than the former ones, such as Restraint and the rest <note>Referring to the first five external limbs like moral restraints and postures</note>.

**Notes:** "**Intimate**"—"**Internal limb**" <note>original: "Antaranga"</note>—that is, more closely related or directly effective.

---

### *Aphorism (8):*—It is still foreign to the seedless (Abstract).

*Commentary:*—The preceding intimate triad of means is foreign to the seedless (abstract) meditation. Why? Because Abstract meditation appears only when these three are absent <note>Seedless or "Nirbija" Samadhi is a state beyond even the highest mental focus, where the tools of meditation are themselves transcended</note>.

### *Aphorism (9):*—Suppressive (or interceptive) modification is the conjunction of the mind with the moment of suppression, on the overthrow and prevalence of the distractive (waking) and suppressive impressions (respectively).

*Commentary:*—The distractive impressions, being the properties (material effects) of the mind, are not identical with cognition, and as such are not suppressed by the suppression of the cognition. In fact, they always outlive the cognitions of which they are the impressions; it is only the material cause whose suppression causes that of the effect also. The suppressive impressions also are properties of the mind. The (respective) "overthrow and prevalence" of these—that is, when the distractive impressions are wasting away and the suppressive impression is gaining ground—then the moment of suppression conjoins with the mind. Thus, the constant change of impressions occurring in the mind is suppressive modification. <note>This describes the psychological process where the habit of "stopping" the mind begins to outweigh the habit of "wandering."</note>

<summary>This page distinguishes between the internal practices of meditation and the ultimate "seedless" state, while explaining the mechanics of how the mind transitions from distraction to a suppressed, quieted state.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Sutras, internal limbs, Antaranga, seedless meditation, Samadhi, mental impressions, suppressive modification, Patanjali</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of the transformations of the mind during deep meditation, specifically focusing on the transition from a distracted state to a focused one.</meta>

# SECTION III—APHORISM 12.

And then the mind has only the impressions left behind—as has been explained under suppressive (abstract) **Meditation (I. 18).** <note>This refers to the state where active thoughts are stilled, leaving only the latent mental "seeds" or tendencies.</note>

**Notes: (1)—** Dr. Mitra interprets the phrase as *suppressed by the suppression of the opposite* <note>original: "pratyanirodhena niruddhah"</note>, and connects it with the following *mental properties* <note>original: "chittadharmah"</note>. But the translation follows the interpretation of Vachaspati Misra <note>A 10th-century commentator known for clarifying complex Yoga philosophy</note>, who thus introduces the passage: "The impressions caused by distractive cognitions (that is, those of the waking state) would cease on the cessation of the waking state, and as such for its cessation we would not stand in need of the suppressive modification." The sense of the reply is given in parenthesis above.

---

### *Aphorism (10)*:—Its flow becomes tranquil from residual habit.

***Commentary:***—From suppressive residual tendencies <note>term: residua (latents impressions or "samskaras" left in the mind by past actions)</note> results the tranquil flow of the mind, which depends on the expertness in the practice of suppressive residua. When those residua become dulled, the residua having suppression for their property are overthrown by those whose property is distraction.

### *Aphorism (11)*:—The destruction and enlivenment (respectively) of the multifunctionality and concentration of the mind is (its) *meditative modification*.

***Commentary:***—Multifunctionality <note>The mind's tendency to flit between many different objects</note> is a property of the mind, and so is Concentration; the meaning is that there is destruction or decay of multifunctionality and enlivenment or rise of Concentration; both of these are correlated to the mind which holds them as its properties. Thus this mind, correlated to its co-natural (or identical) properties—the aforesaid decay and rise—becomes collected or pacified; and this is its “meditative modification.”

---

### *Aphorism (12)*:—Then again, the concentrative modification of the mind is that in which both the repressed and the revived are equally recognised.

***Commentary:*** For a person whose mind has reached a state of peace, the previous thought is considered 'repressed,' while the subsequent thought that resembles it is 'revived.' The meditative mind, which connects these two states, remains the same—that is, it is collected or concentrated. Therefore, this represents the 'concentrative modification' <note>Sanskrit: Samadhi-parinama; the process by which the mind moves toward total absorption</note> of the mind, where these thoughts are viewed as its inherent properties.

**Sutra (13): This also explains the modifications of property, time, and condition within the elementary substances and the sense-organs.**

***Commentary:*** “This”—referring to the previously described changes in the mind—serves to explain the changes that occur in physical elements and the organs of perception regarding their *property*, *time*, and *condition*.

Among these, the “modification of property” <note>Sanskrit: Dharma-parinama</note> involves the disappearance of distracting qualities and the emergence of focused qualities within the object that possesses them (namely, within their underlying causes).

The “modification of time” <note>Sanskrit: Lakshana-parinama</note> refers to the transition through the three temporal stages. This process of suppression first leaves behind the stage of *futurity* <note>The state of potentiality before a quality manifests</note> without losing its essential ***property-ness***, and then joins with the stage of the *present*, where its true nature is revealed. This is its second stage; yet even here, it is not entirely disconnected from the *past* and *future*. Similarly, the state of distraction (the ordinary waking state), which also possesses these three temporal stages, leaves behind the stage of *the present* without losing its ***property-ness*** and joins with the stage of the *past*. This is its third stage, though it remains related to the *present* and the *future*. In the same way, once distraction is fully realized and thereby leaves its—

<summary>This page explains Sutra 13 of the Yoga-Darsana, detailing how the mind and physical matter undergo three types of transformation: changes in their properties, their temporal state (past, present, future), and their overall condition.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga-Darsana, Sutra 13, modification of property, modification of time, modification of condition, elementary substances, sense-organs, property-ness, futurity, present, past, Samadhi, mental states</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical analysis of the three-fold transformations (parinama) of matter and mind, specifically focusing on the transition between time-states and conditions.</meta>

# SECTION III—APHORISM 13.

The limitation of *futurity* <note>The state of an object or thought before it manifests</note>, without losing its essential nature as a property, becomes connected to the limitation of the *present*. In this state, it manifests in its true character and begins its functional operations. This is its second limitation; yet even here, it does not fully disconnect from the limitations of the past and the future. The same principle applies to the states of mental suppression <note>nirodha: the process of stilling the mind</note> and distraction <note>vyutthana: the wandering or active state of the mind</note>.

In the same manner, the “transformation of condition” <note>avastha-parinama: the change in the intensity or strength of a state</note> occurs when, during the moments of mental suppression, the suppressive impressions <term>residua: subconscious imprints or 'samskaras' left by past experiences</term> are strengthened, and the distracting impressions are weakened. This constitutes the transformation of the condition of the properties.

Among these processes:
*   The transformation of the **object** possessing the properties occurs through these properties.
*   The transformation of the **properties** occurs through the limitations of time <note>past, present, and future</note>.
*   The transformation of the **limitations** occurs through the varying conditions <note>strength or weakness of the state</note>.

Thus, the function of the fundamental qualities <term>attributes: the 'gunas' or the three primary forces of nature—stability, activity, and clarity</term> does not exist even for a single moment without these transformations of property, time, and condition. In other words, nature is constantly producing changes because the nature of these qualities is eternally mobile. The cause of this continuous operation is the subordinate relationship the qualities have to one another as they shift in dominance.

Therefore, in both the physical elements and the sense-organs, there are three kinds of transformations due to the distinction between a property and the object that holds it. In reality, however, there is only one fundamental transformation. This is because a property is merely a form of the object possessing it. Consequently, the descriptions above are simply ways of considering certain changes in the object through its properties. Hence, throughout the past, present, and future states, what happens to a property within its host object is merely a change in its manifestation or form, and not a change in the underlying substance <note>The core 'matter' or 'prakriti' remains the same while its appearance shifts</note>.

<summary>This page explains the three-fold transformation of objects and the mind—by property, time-state, and condition—arguing that while forms change, the underlying substance remains constant.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Sutras, transformation, properties, time-states, mental suppression, subconscious impressions, fundamental qualities, nature of matter</keywords>

# YOGA-PHILOSOPHY <note>original: "Yoga-Darsana"</note>

As for example, to a golden vessel on being broken, and made to undergo a change, the only change that happens is that of form, and not that of the gold itself.

Says another (the Buddhist <note>original: "Bauddha"</note> holding the complete identity of the property and the object possessing it): 

> “The object is nothing more than the property, in as much as it does not go beyond the former material; for if the object continued its existence through the various modifications (of the property), then it would in reality become unchangeable.”

(To this we make the following reply:)—There is no harm in this. Why? Because, we do not assert its absoluteness. Thus all the three worlds recede from manifestation (i.e., recede from *present* activity and become the *past*), simply because of the denial of the (unchangeable) eternity (of mundane objects). And though receded (become past), still it exists, because of the denial of its destruction; its minuteness is due to its correlation (i.e., resolution into its cause); and from its minuteness results imperceptibility. The property, having the modification of time, existing in the various limitations (<term>lakshaṇa</term> <note>The specific temporal character or "mark" of an object, such as being past, present, or future.</note>, of time), becoming *past*, and thus becoming conjoined with the limitation of the *past*, is not disjoined from the limitations of the *present* and the *future*; similarly the *future*, joined to the limitation of the *future*, is not disjoined from those of the *past* and *present*; and lastly the *present*, joined to the limitation of the *present*, is not disjoined from those of the *past* and the *future*. (That is to say every individual limitation is accompanied by the other two). Just as, because a man is attached to a certain woman, it does not follow that he has an aversion for other women.

Here, in the modification of time (limitation), all the limitations occurring together, there would result an

<summary>This page explores the Yoga school's defense against Buddhist arguments regarding the nature of objects, arguing that while forms change across past, present, and future, the underlying substance remains existent though imperceptible.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga-Darsana, Buddhism, lakshana, temporal modification, substance and property, metaphysics of time</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion of the three transformations of matter, specifically focusing on the "limitation" or "time-state" (lakshana) of properties within the Yoga system.</meta>

<page-num>101</page-num>
# SECTION III—APHORISM 13.

If it is argued that there is an overlapping or confusion of these time-limitations <note>The objector suggests that if past, present, and future exist within a property, they must be mixed together confusedly</note>, we offer the following reply: Once the nature of a property is established, we must describe its various states; for certainly its specific character belongs to that property not only in the present, but also in the past and the future. If this were not so, the mind would not retain its capacity for attachment as a property, simply because that attachment is not visible when the mind is occupied by anger. Furthermore, it is impossible for any single individual state to relate to all three time-limitations simultaneously; rather, these states manifest sequentially, brought forth by their specific causes. As has been stated: "**The intense manifestation of forms and functions are in opposition to one another, but the underlying qualities work in harmony with these intense manifestations**" <note>original: "The excessive manifestation, of forms and those of functions are opposed to one another, but the genuine ones act in consort with the excessive manifestations"</note> (see this quotation under Section II, Aphorism 15). Therefore, no such confusion of states exists as the objector suggests.

Just as the activity of attachment toward a specific object does not mean attachment has ceased to exist elsewhere—the reality being that it remains related to other objects in a general, latent way—the same principle applies to the time-limitation (*<term>lakshana</term> <note>The temporal state of a property: past, present, or future</note>*). It is the property itself, not the underlying substance possessing the property, that undergoes these three temporal states. These properties—whether manifested (*present*) or unmanifested (*past and future*)—reach various conditions of strength or weakness. They are said to have changed, but this change is only in their external condition, not in their fundamental substance. 

->Consider these analogies:<-

Just as a single digit represents a hundred when placed in the hundreds column, a ten in the tens column, and a unit in the units column; and again, just as a single woman is called a mother, a daughter, or a mother-in-law depending on the relationship, while the woman herself remains the same person.

<summary>This page explains how a property remains the same even as it moves through the states of past, present, and future, using analogies of mathematical place value and social roles to show that change in condition does not mean a change in essence.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Sutras, lakshana, temporal states, property vs substance, manifestation, Patanjali, philosophy of time</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion regarding the nature of change and time in the Yoga system, specifically addressing objections to the theory of transformation.</meta>

# YOGA PHILOSOPHY

Against the theory of the “modification of conditions,” some people have raised the objection that in that case there would be an unchangeable eternality <note>The Sanskrit term is *kūṭastha-nityatā*, referring to an unchanging, rock-like permanence that the objectors claim would result if properties only shifted between time-states without truly changing.</note> for objects and their properties; because, a property is said to be in the *future* when, being removed from the range of the activity of the limitation, it does not carry out its own operations; when it carries on its operations, it is said to be in the *present*, and lastly, when it has ceased after having operated, it is said to be in the *past*.—And thus we find that the limitations and the conditions of the properties and the objects having them are unchangeably eternal. This is the objection urged.

(We reply):—This is not a sound objection; because though the object having an attribute is eternal, yet there is quite a variety of the forms of the suppression (or overthrow) of the attributes themselves; just as the destructible (suppressible) agglomeration <note>The physical world composed of the five elements like earth and water.</note> (earth and the rest) is a mere property of the indestructible (irrepressible) sound, odour etc. <note>The subtle elements or *Tanmatras* which serve as the blueprint for physical matter.</note> (rudimentary elements); similarly the repressible <term>mergent</term> <note>original: "Linga" — the intellect or *Buddhi* which "merges" back into nature during dissolution.</note> is a mere property of the irrepressible Attributes <note>The three *Gunas*: Goodness (Sattva), Passion (Rajas), and Darkness (Tamas).</note> Goodness and the rest. Thus their modification is attributed to these (*i.e.*, the properties Intellect, Earth etc.). 

Here we have the following instance: The object clay, acquiring a property other than that of a lump, becomes modified by the property into the form of a jar; and this form of the jar, relinquishing the future limitation, attains to the present one, and thus becomes modified by limitation; and lastly, the jar momentarily becoming new and old, acquires the modification of condition. The change of the properties of an object is a condition; and the change in the limitation of the property is also a condition; thus the change in matter is only one (*i.e.*, the modification of condition); but this has been shown in its various divisions (*i.e.*, though there is in fact only one modification, that of condition, still the other two—that of property and limitation—have been explained separately).

<summary>This page addresses a philosophical challenge to the Yoga theory of change, explaining how objects like clay transform through different states (past, present, future) while their underlying substance remains constant. It argues that all physical and mental transformations are ultimately variations of a single process of modification.</summary>

All these modifications of property and limitation and condition never outgrowing the form of the object, the modification is one alone, and this includes all the other particular ones.

Now what do you mean by “modification”? <term>Modification (Parinama)</term> <note>The Sanskrit term 'Parinama' refers to the transformation or evolution of a substance into a new state.</note> consists in the appearance of fresh properties for a certain object, following on the cessation (or repression) of the former ones.

**Notes : (1)** “*It becomes manifested in its true colour*”—The suppression, that was future ere this, becomes the present now; the one kind of suppression does not cease to be suppression in order to make room for the other suppression.

**(2)** “*Subordinate character of the attributes.*” We find in daily life the servants always acting for their master; similarly the fact of goodness <note>original: "Sattva"</note> &c. being of the same subordinate nature (like the servant), accounts for their constant activity.

**(3)** “*And not any change of the constituent matter.*” because if the change took place in the matter, then the object undergoing a momentary change, would never be recognised as the same.

**(4)** “*If the object continued its existence through the various modifications &c.*” If the object were so, then it would be equally related to the past and the future as well, and thus would result its eternality and unchangeableness, like that of the sentient faculty; and certainly this will not be palatable to the <term>Bauddha (Buddhist)</term> <note>Refers to the Buddhist philosophical school, which generally argues for the momentariness of all things, contrasting with the Yoga view of underlying continuity.</note> objector either.

**(5)** “*We do not assert its absoluteness.*” If like that of sentient faculty we also asserted the absolute eternality of the objects, then would the objection apply to us; as it is however, we do not make any such assertion. In fact we go so far as to assert the cessation of the activity of all the three worlds, to say nothing of particular objects.

**(6)** “*Denial of its destruction,*”—The reasoning is this:— What is non-existent never operates, as <term>sky-flowers</term> <note>A classic Indian philosophical metaphor for something that is impossible or non-existent, like a flower growing in the empty sky.</note>; the three worlds however do operate; and as such they can never be said to be non-existent. Thus though we deny their absolute eternality, yet as they are never entirely destroyed, this modified eternality may be asserted of them.

(8) "Otherwise, the mind would not have attachment etc. It is only after anger has passed off that the mind is found to have attachment; and thus if attachment did not latently exist there in its future state, whence its later appearance? The sentient faculty is not so; and hence it is said to be absolutely eternal.

### Sutra (14):—The subject is that which is correlated to
->the properties of tranquility, activity, and latency<-
->(indescribability).<-

*Commentary:*—The property is only the faculty of the subject, characterized by capability; and its existence being inferred from the variety of its effects, it is found to be manifold with regard to the one subject.

<margin>Measure:</margin>Of these the present (active) property, its own activity, becomes differentiated from the other properties—the tranquil (past) and the latent (future) ones. When however it is correlated only in a general form, then consisting merely of the form of the subject, wherefrom should it differentiate? Thus the properties of the subject are three—the tranquil, the active and the latent. Of these the *tranquil* are those that have receded after having done their work; those that are operating are the *active*; <u>both of these precede the future</u>; the past precedes the present. Wherefore are not the present ones said to precede the past ones? Because among these two there is no order of precedence; as the order of precedence that we find between the past and the future is not found in the

# CHAPTER III—SUTRA 14.
