...case of the past with regard to the present. Therefore there is nothing that precedes the past. For this reason the future precedes the present (this in accordance with the theory of the continued existence of the effect latent in the cause, before its manifestation). <note>This refers to the doctrine of *Satkaryavada*, which suggests that an effect already exists within its cause before it becomes visible.</note>

Now which are the *latent* ones? (Reply) Everything having the character of everything. With regard to this it is declared: (3) “Among the immovable objects we find the universality of the effects in the form of the different tastes, belonging to Water and Earth; similarly of the immovables with regard to the movable, and *vice versa*.” Thus the genus <note>The essential category or type.</note> not being suppressed (or else the various effects would not be recognized), everything has the nature of everything. Though this is so, yet their manifestation is not simultaneous, because of the manifestation being conditioned by place, time, and form. That which is correlated to these manifested and unmanifested properties, and which has both the general (belonging to the subject) and the special character (of the property),—such a correlative is the subject. 

One (the *Yogachara*) <note>A school of Buddhist philosophy that emphasizes "Mind-Only" or Idealism.</note> however who asserts the property to be unconnected (with anything else, because he denies the existence of any subject other than the properties of cognition and the like), for him there would be no experience. Why? Because the actions performed by a certain idea (or cognition) cannot transfer its effect to the other idea (for according to these theorists there is nothing except ideas, and these are momentary); and again there could be no memory of the action; for an object seen by one (idea) cannot be remembered by another. Because of the recognition of objects (previously perceived), there must be a subject correlative (to the various modifications of the object), which is recognized as the same, after its properties have undergone a change. Therefore it cannot be rightly held that all that exists is mere unconnected property.

**Notes (1) "*Existence being inferred etc.*"**—The meaning is that within a single subject, the property of potentiality is seen to be manifold—this potentiality lying hidden within its cause.

**(2) "*When however it is correlated etc.*"** This addition establishes the distinction of the unmanifested cause.

**(3) "*Among the immovable objects etc.*":**—In the combined transformations of Earth and Water—such as trees and similar plants—we find various kinds of taste and other qualities; the transformation of the immovable within the movable is found in the modifications of air, water, etc., within the animal body; the transformation of the movable within the immovable is seen in the growth of a pomegranate tree when it is nourished with blood <note>A reference to ancient Indian horticultural practices where animal products were used as fertilizer to enhance fruit growth</note>.

## Aphorism (15).—The change of sequence is the cause of the variety of transformations.

*Commentary:*—"The transformation of a single subject should be only one"—this being argued, we reply that the change of sequence <note>original: "order"</note> is the cause of the variety of the transformations. As for instance, the proper sequence is: *clay dust*, *lump of clay*, *the clay jar*, *the shards of the broken jar*, and *the small pieces of clay*. The proper sequence is the appearance of the subsequent property immediately after the one that precedes it—for example, as the lump disappears, the jar appears—such is the sequence of the transformation of properties <term>Dharma-parinama: the change of the external form or quality of a substance</term>. 

The sequence of the transformation of time-states <note>original: "limitation"</note>—such as the present state of the jar following its future state, or the past state of the lump following its present state; as for the past, there is no sequence because only where there is a continuous succession can there be a sequence; but there is no further succession for the past; therefore, sequence exists only for the first two time-states (the present and the future). Similarly, there is the sequence of the transformation of condition—for example, the oldness of the jar following its newness; and this oldness, being revealed by the sequence of the series of moments, eventually reaches its final manifestation. This is the third type of transformation, distinct from those of property and time-state.

<summary>This page explains the three types of transformations (property, time-state, and condition) that objects undergo, using the metaphor of clay changing from a lump to a jar to illustrate how sequence dictates physical reality.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Sutras, transformation, sequence, causality, time-states, properties, clay jar metaphor, Indian philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of mental properties and introduces the specific powers gained through advanced meditation (sanyama).</meta>

# SECTION III—APHORISM 16.

These various sequences, which take their form based on the distinction between the underlying substance <note>original: "subject"</note> and its qualities <note>original: "property"</note>—certain qualities also become substances themselves when compared to other qualities. However, when there is a sense of identity between the quality and the substance—and thus the quality is referred to simply by naming the substance—then the sequence appears to be a single unity. 

The qualities of the mind are twofold: the perceived and the unperceived. Of these, the former are the acts of consciousness <note>original: "cognitions"</note>, and the latter are the mental states themselves. These unperceived qualities, whose objective nature is understood through logical inference, are seven in number: 

(1) **Suppression** <note>The state of deep meditation inferred from the disappearance of all mental fluctuations</note>, 
(2) **Virtue and Vice** <note>Inferred from the resulting experiences of pleasure and pain</note>, 
(3) **Subconscious Impressions** <note>Inferred from the existence of memory</note>, 
(4) **Constant Change** <note>Inferred from the inherent mobility of the fundamental qualities of nature</note>, 
(5) **Vitality** <note>The effort to live, inferred from the act of breathing</note>, 
(6) **Movement** <note>Action inferred from the activity of the various senses</note>, and 
(7) **Potentiality** <note>The latent power of effects existing within their causes</note>. 

These are the seven qualities of the mind that exist beyond direct perception.

Therefore, when these three types of change are per—

# YOGA-DARSANA

<note>Yoga-Darsana: The philosophy or "vision" of Yoga, specifically referring to the Yoga Sutras of Patanjali.</note>

...ceived, the <term>yogi</term> <note>yogi: a practitioner of yoga who has achieved a high level of spiritual discipline</note> accomplishes the knowledge of the past and future related thereto.

---

-> *Sutra (17).—The word, the object denoted by it, and its idea are commingled with one another, on account of their being mistaken for one another; hence by <term>sanyama</term> <note>sanyama: the combined practice of concentration, meditation, and total absorption on a single object</note> over the distinct provinces of each of these, (arises) the comprehension of the cry of all creatures.* <-

*Commentary:*—The organ of speech has its purpose solely with the letter pronounced; the organ of hearing has the modification of sound for its object; and the word finally is perceptible through the apprehension of the letters taken collectively. The letters, not coming in simultaneously, and hence not aided by one another, do not affect the word; and as such not giving rise to (any idea), but each of them appearing and disappearing by turns—are severally said to be incapable of forming the word. Every one of the letters, forming a word, —capable of a host of denotations (when taken severally), yet when correlated with the accompanying letters, and hence becoming manifold, become so placed that each of them is confined to the particular word by the other [i.e., the single letter *ga* <note>original: "ga" - a Sanskrit consonant</note> has the capability of denoting *cow* <note>original: "gau"</note>, *multitude* <note>original: "gaṇa"</note>, *white/pale* <note>original: "gaura"</note> &c., so long as it is taken by itself; but as soon as the letter *ga* is joined with the letters *au* <note>original: "au" - a Sanskrit vowel</note>, &c., it becomes manifold; and each of the two letters serves to confine the other to the particular word in question. Thus (we see that) the various letters, depending on the order of succession become characterised by the convention of meaning, and though capable of all sorts denotations, yet becoming subordinated to the *ga* and the *au*, denote the particular object with the dewlap &c. (i.e. the cow). Thus the cognition of the unity of these,—

<summary>This page explains Sutra 17 of the Yoga-Darsana, which describes how a Yogi can understand the sounds of all living beings by separating the distinct components of language: the sound, the intended object, and the mental concept.</summary>

there being the expression of the meaning of a sentence in a word, and that of the meaning of the word in a sentence, there is a confusion and) the word is to be broken up (into its constituent parts) and then explained as to which part of it expresses the verb, and which the nominative (as the word <term>śrotriya<note>A Sanskrit term for one who is learned in the Vedas.</note></term> is broken up into “one (nominative) who studies (verb) the <term>Vedas<note>The oldest and most sacred scriptures of Hinduism.</note></term> (accusative). Or otherwise, in the case of *asvah* (meaning by itself, both *horse* and *thou breathedst*) and *ajâpayah* (=*the milk of the she—goat, and also, thou hast suppressed*), there being a confusion of the name and the verb (as shown by the two meanings of each), how could these be explained either with regard to the verb (if it were taken as a name) or to the nominative (in case it were taken as a verb)? the distinct province of these word, meaning and idea—e.g., “the house is whitening”—the word signifying the verb (*śveta*); and “the house is white”—here the word (*śveta*) signifies the noun (so much for the word); (secondly) its meaning is that consisting in the verb or in the noun; and (thirdly) the idea is that which apprehends the aforesaid meanings. Why? Because in convention the idea **has** only one form, being based on the identity of the signification—“this is that,”—the “white object” being the object of the word as well as of the idea. [ That is to say, in the above example the idea is that of the house in front being *a white one*, whatever may be the words in which the idea is expressed ]. And this object under its modifications is accompanied neither by the word, nor by the idea. Similarly the word and the idea do not accompany each other. The consciousness of the distinct province of each being in the form—‘the word is different from the meaning which again is different from the idea.’

## *Sutra* (18): **By the mental presentation of the impressions, the knowledge of previous births.**

*Commentary*:—Impressions <note>original: "Samskaras" — the latent mental traces left by past experiences</note> are of two kinds...(1) Those in the form of the residual longings, the cause of memory and the afflictions, and (2) those in the form of virtue and vice, leading to fruition <note>the "ripening" or results of past actions</note>. Both of these kinds, accomplished in the former birth, are the properties of the mind, imperceptible, like modification, movement, suppression, power, life (the properties of the mind spoken of above, III, 15). The <term>Sanyama</term> <note>the combined practice of concentration, meditation, and deep absorption</note> over these is capable of rendering the impressions apparent; nor is such presentation possible without a cognition of their time, place and cause (of the previous birth). Thus the knowledge of the previous birth accrues to the <term>yogi</term> <note>a practitioner of yoga</note> through the mental presentation of the impressions. In the case of others too there is a similar knowledge of another's birth, following on the mental presentation of their impressions. In connection with this we have a story:—Discriminative knowledge accrued to the revered <term>Jaigishavya</term> <note>a legendary sage and master of Yoga</note>, possessing, through the mental presentation of the impressions, a knowledge of the orderly modification of his birth extending over ten <term>Pralayic</term> <note>referring to Pralaya, the periodic dissolution of the universe</note> creations. The revered <term>Avatya</term>, thus spoke to him: 

> "Through ten <term>Kalpic</term> <note>referring to a Kalpa, an immense period of time or a "day of Brahma"</note> creations having your consciousness unrepressed, you must have come across the pains due to birth in a hellish or a brutish womb; and being born again and again among men and the gods, which of the two, pleasure and pain, did you find in a greater amount?"

<term>Jaigishavya</term> thus replied: 

> "I, having my consciousness unrepressed through ten cycles, and coming across the pain due to hellish and bestial births, and being born again and again

<summary>This page explains Yoga Sutra 3.18, which describes how a practitioner can gain knowledge of past lives by focusing their meditative power on latent mental impressions. It introduces a dialogue between the sages Jaigishavya and Avatya regarding the suffering experienced across multiple cosmic cycles of rebirth.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Sutras, Patanjali, past lives, reincarnation, Samskaras, Sanyama, Jaigishavya, suffering, cosmic cycles</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the nature of suffering and the attainment of supernatural powers through meditation, specifically the ability to perceive the thoughts of others.</meta>

<page-num>112</page-num>
-># PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA<-

“Among gods and men, whatever I experienced, all I think to be solely pain.” Says the revered **Âvatya**: “This unsurpassed pleasure of contentment consisting in mastery over Nature that thou hast... dost thou include this also under pain?” Replied the revered **Jaigîshavya**: “This pleasure of contentment is excellent as compared with sensuous pleasure; in comparison with isolation <note>original: "Kaivalya," the state of ultimate liberation or spiritual independence</note> however, this also is pain. Because this pleasure of contentment is a property of <term>Buddhi</term> <note>The higher intellectual faculty or discerning mind</note>, and as such abounds in the three attributes <note>The Gunas: qualities of passion, dullness, and goodness that make up the material world</note>; and all consciousness partaking of the three attributes, has been put under the head of ‘what should be avoided.’ Then, contentment is said to be pleasure because the strings of desire being of the nature of pain, on the removal of these, there is unrestricted pleasure.”

---

### ***Aphorism* (19):—With reference to cognition, the knowledge of another’s mind.**

*Commentary*:—From the <term>Sanyama</term> <note>A technical term for the combined practice of concentration, meditation, and deep absorption</note> over cognition—that is, from the mental presentation of the cognition of others—results the knowledge of other’s minds.

---

### ***Aphorism* (20):—But not along with its object, that not being the object.**

*Commentary*:—The **Yogi** knows the mind of another, as attached to something; but he does not know that “it is attached to that particular object.” Because that which is the object of another’s mind, is not the object of the **Yogi’s** mind; it is only another’s cognition (or mind) that is made the object of the mind of the **Yogi**.

**Notes**:—(1) This aphorism is explained by **Vijnâna Bhikshu** <note>A 16th-century philosopher and commentator on Yoga</note> as a part of the **Commentary** <note>original: "Bhâshya"</note>.

---

<header>CHAPTER III—APHORISMS 21-22.</header>

### **Aphorism (21):—Through meditative constraint <note>original: "Sanyama"; the combined practice of concentration, meditation, and absorption</note> on the form of the body, its visibility is suspended. When the connection with the light of the eyes is thus severed, there is disappearance.**

*Commentary:*—By performing meditative constraint regarding the physical form of the body, the capacity for that form to be perceived is suppressed. When this visibility is suspended, the link between the body and the light of another person's eyes is broken; from this, the <term>Yogi</term> <note>a practitioner of Yoga</note> becomes invisible. The disappearance of sound and the other senses <note>touch, taste, and smell</note> are to be understood in the same way.

---

### **Aphorism (22):—Action is of two types: active and dormant. By meditative constraint over these, one gains knowledge of the end; this knowledge also comes from portents.**

*Commentary:*—The <term>Karma</term> <note>the law of cause and effect resulting from actions</note> that determines the span of a lifetime is of two kinds: the *active* <note>quickly manifesting</note> and the *dormant* <note>slowly manifesting</note>. *Active* karma is like a wet cloth spread out on the ground, which dries very quickly. In contrast, *dormant* karma is like that same cloth tied in a tight bundle, which takes a long time to dry. To use another example, *active* karma is like fire in dry straw aided by the wind, consuming it rapidly; *dormant* karma is like that same fire placed in small, scattered spots throughout a heap of straw, taking a long time to burn through. In this manner, the karma that governs a single lifetime is either fast-acting or slow-acting.

By performing meditative constraint over these two types of karma, one gains knowledge of the exact time of death. This knowledge is also gained "by portents"—omens which are of three types: bodily, elemental, and celestial. An example of a 'bodily' portent is when a person closes their ears and fails to hear the internal sounds of the body <note>such as the sound of digestion or the pulse</note>, or when they see no light after closing their eyes. Similarly—

<sig>15</sig>

<summary>This page describes how a Yogi can achieve invisibility by disconnecting their physical form from the sight of others and explains how meditation on one's karma can reveal the timing of one's death.</summary>

'Elemental' portents occur when one beholds the attendants of the God of Death; or when one suddenly sees his deceased ancestors; and 'celestial' portents occur when one suddenly beholds the heavens, or the Perfect Ones <note>Siddhas: enlightened beings with supernatural powers</note>, or anything contrary to the ordinary course of events. By these portents as well, one knows his end to be near at hand.

---

### **Sutra ( 23 ):—In friendliness and the rest, superhuman powers.**

*Commentary:—*Friendliness, mercy, and gladness are the three Emotions or feelings. Of those, by the feeling of friendliness towards all happy creatures, one acquires the power of friendliness. By feeling mercy for the distressed creatures, he acquires the power of mercy. And by feeling gladness towards the righteous, he acquires the powers of gladness. The <term>Sanyama</term> <note>The combined practice of fixed attention, meditation, and perfect concentration</note> proceeding from these emotions results in such powers as have their faculties unrepressed. With regard to vicious persons, one should maintain indifference rather than a specific feeling; thus, in this case, there is no meditation, and as such, no power proceeds from indifference because of the absence of any <term>Sanyama</term> with reference to it.

---

### **Sutra ( 24 ):—In powers, the power of the Elephant and the like.**

*Commentary:—*From <term>Sanyama</term> over the power of the elephant, results elephantine strength; from that over the power of *Garuda* <note>The mythical king of birds and vehicle of Vishnu, known for immense speed and strength</note>, a Garuda-like power; and from that over the power of the wind, a wind-like power.

---

<header>CHAPTER III—SUTRAS 25-26.</header>

## **Sutra ( 25 ) :—From the application of the light of the (luminous) disposition, (results) the knowledge of the subtle, the concealed, and the distant.**

***Commentary:—***The Extremely Luminous disposition of the mind <note>referring to *Jyotishmati*, a state of serene clarity</note> has already been explained (in Book I, Sutra 36). The yogi, by applying the light of this disposition (that is, the inner light of higher perception) to objects that are subtle <note>too small to see</note>, concealed <note>hidden by barriers or "intercepted"</note>, or distant, acquires direct knowledge of these objects.

---

## **Sutra ( 26 ) :—From meditative constraint <term>Sanyama: the combined practice of concentration, meditation, and absorption</term> on the Sun, comes the knowledge of the different worlds.**

***Commentary:—***The details of this cosmic geography are as follows: There are seven worlds. From the lowest hell <note>original: "Avîchî"</note> to the summit of the sacred mountain <note>original: "Meru," the axis of the world</note> is the Earth (1). From the mountain to the Polar Star <note>original: "Dhruva"</note> is the Starry Region occupied by the planets and stars (2). Above that is the fivefold Celestial Region (3)—the region of the king of gods <note>original: "Mahendra"</note> being the third of these; the fourth being the region of the Lord of Creatures <note>original: "Prâjapatya"</note> or the Luminous Region <note>original: "Maharloka"</note>; and the threefold region of the Creator <note>original: "Brâhma"</note>—consisting of the Productive Region <note>original: "Janaloka"</note>, the Region of Austerities <note>original: "Tapoloka"</note>, and the Region of Truth <note>original: "Satyaloka"</note>. 

These are described in ancient texts as follows: “The three-layered world of the Creator, then the great world of the Lord of Creatures, then the Heaven of the king of gods, then the Sky with the stars, and lastly the Earth with its various creations.” 

Located below the lowest point of the habitable world are the six great Hellish Regions: the Great Time, the Terrible, the Howling, the Great Howling, the Black Thread, and the Utter Darkness <note>original: "Mahâkâla, Ambarîsha, Raurava, Mahâraurava, Kâleya Sutra, and Andhatâmisra"</note>. These are situated respectively within the elements of Earth, Water, Fire, Air, Space, and Darkness. In these realms, various beings are born with long lifespans to experience the consequences of their own past actions through pain. Beneath these are the seven Nether Worlds: the Great Bottomless, the Liquid Earth, the Bottomless, the Well-established, the Dissolved, the Under-bottomless, and the Abyss <note>original: "Mahâtala, Rasâtala, Atala, Sutala, Vitala, Talâtala, and Pâtâla"</note>. # YOGA-PHILOSOPHY <note>original: "YOGA-DARŚANA"</note>

The Earth, consisting of the seven continents, is the eighth <note>The "eighth" likely refers to its position within a specific cosmological hierarchy or its relation to the surrounding celestial spheres</note>; in the centre of which is the Land of the Mountains, the Golden Meru, whose peaks are those of silver, emerald, rock-crystal, gold, and jewels. The Southern region of the sky here is of the colour of the blue lotus through the colour of the emerald peak; the Eastern white, the Western clear, the Northern golden. On the Northern side of this is the *Rose-apple* <note>original: "Jambu"</note> tree, whence this particular continent is called the *Rose-apple Island* <note>original: "Jambudwipa"</note>. On account of the movement of the Sun, it is **always touched by night and day.** To the North of this are three mountains, four thousand miles high, having blue and white peaks. Within these mountain-ranges are three countries, each 72,000 Miles—Ramaṇaka, Hiranmaya and the Northern Kuru <note>original: "Uttara Kuru"</note>. To the South are the Mountains Nishadha, Hemakuta and the Snowy Mountains <note>original: "Hima"</note>, extending over 4,000 Miles; within these are three countries each 72,000 miles—The Harivanśa, Kimpurusha and the Land of Bharat <note>original: "Bhârata," referring to India</note>. To the East of Meru are the Bhadrâswas extending up to the Malyavan mountains; and to the West are the Ketumâlas extending up to the Gandhamâdana mountains. In the centre is the Ilavrita. Thus are the 8,000 miles on each side of Meru surrounded by its half (wherefore Meru becomes their centre). Such is the *Rose-apple Island* extending over **200,000 miles**; surrounded by the salt Ocean, twice its size. Thenceforth follow the other continents—S'âka, S'uka, Krauncha, S'âlmala, Magadha, and Pushkara—each twice the size of the other; as also the seven Oceans—like heaps of oil-seed, interspersed with several mountains—of Sugarcane-juice, Wine, Butter, Curd, Gruel, Milk, Pure Water. Thus the seven continents,—resembling so many bangles, surrounded by the seven Oceans, together with the mountains visible and invisible, reach the extent of 4,00,00,00,000 miles.

All this with its position fixed, is contained in the Egg <note>The "Brahmanda" or World-Egg, a common Vedic concept for the closed universe</note>; which is the atomic part of Matter, as the firefly in the sky. Among the nether regions, in the Oceans, and among the

<summary>This page describes the traditional Hindu cosmology of the Earth, detailing Mount Meru as the central axis surrounded by concentric continents and oceans of various substances like milk and wine. It concludes by situating this vast geography within the "World-Egg," emphasizing its smallness relative to the totality of matter.</summary>

# YOGA PHILOSOPHY <note>original: "YOGA-DARSNA"</note>

There are four **Divine Beings**—the **Infallibles** <note>original: "Achyutas"</note>, the **Pure Abodes** <note>original: "Suddhanivasas"</note>, the **Luminous Truths** <note>original: "Satyabhas"</note>, and the **Conscious-Unconscious ones** <note>original: "Sanjnasanjis"</note>. These have no houses of their own, and abiding in themselves, living one over the other, commanding Nature, live so long as the creation lasts. Of these, the **Infallibles** rejoice in the bliss of argumentative meditation <note>referring to Savitarka Samadhi, where the mind still reasons about the object</note>, the **Pure Abodes** in the bliss of contemplative meditation <note>Savichara Samadhi</note>, the **Luminous Truths** in the contemplation of pure bliss; and the **Conscious-Unconscious ones** rejoice in that of pure Egoism <note>Asmita, the pure sense of "I am"</note>. These four exist in the three worlds (i.e. are not liberated <note>original: "mukta"</note>).

In fact all these seven worlds belong to the **Creator** <note>original: "Brahmâ"</note>. The disembodied ones and those that have been resolved into Nature however continue in the line of Emancipation and have no place in the ordinary world.

All this is to be directly perceived by the practitioner <note>original: "yogi"</note>, by **Perfect Constraint** <term>Sanyama: the combined practice of concentration, meditation, and total absorption</term> in the Sun; as also by that in others. The **Perfect Constraint** is to be practised so long as these are not perceived.

---

**Aphorism** <note>original: "Sutra"</note> **(27):—In the moon, the knowledge of the Starry Regions.**

*Commentary*: Through **Perfect Constraint** in the moon the practitioner is to know the position of the Stars.

---

**Aphorism (28):—In the Polar Star, the knowledge of their motions.**

*Commentary*:—By **Perfect Constraint** in the Polar Star one is to know the motion of the stars. By **Perfect Constraint** applied to the upper firmament one is to know them.

---

**Aphorism (29):—In the circle of the navel, the knowledge of arrangement of the body.**

# CHAPTER III—APHORISMS 30-33.

*Commentary:—*By practicing <term>Sanyama</term> <note>Combined meditative absorption, including concentration, meditation, and deep integration</note> on the navel-center, one gains knowledge of the physical arrangement of the body. Within the body, there are three humors—Wind, Bile, and Phlegm <note>The three 'doshas' or biological energies in Ayurvedic medicine</note>; and there are seven constituent substances—skin, blood, flesh, sinews, bone, marrow, and semen—listed here in order from the outermost layer to the innermost.

**Aphorism (30):—By focusing on the pit of the throat, one achieves the cessation of hunger and thirst.**

*Commentary:—*Below the tongue is a thin membrane, below which lies the throat, and further down is the "pit." By practicing <term>Sanyama</term> on this point, hunger and thirst no longer trouble the <term>yogi</term> <note>A practitioner of yoga</note>.

---

**Aphorism (31):—By focusing on the "tortoise-tube," one achieves steadiness.**

*Commentary:—*Below the aforementioned throat-pit, there is a channel or tube shaped like a tortoise (positioned similarly to a serpent or an alligator). By practicing <term>Sanyama</term> on this tube, the <term>yogi</term> attains a perfectly steady physical and mental posture.

---

**Aphorism (32):—By focusing on the light at the crown of the head, one gains a vision of the Perfect Ones.**

*Commentary:—*Beneath the skull, there is an opening radiant with light. By practicing <term>Sanyama</term> on this light, the practitioner receives a vision of the "Perfect Ones" <note>Siddhas; enlightened beings or masters who have attained perfection</note> who travel in the space between the Earth and the Heavens.

---

**Aphorism (33):—Or, through spontaneous intuition, one knows everything.**

*Commentary:—*Intuitive intelligence is the "Deliverer"—it is the precursor to the highest discriminative knowledge, just as the Dawn precedes the Sunrise. Upon the arising of this spontaneous intuitional insight, the <term>yogi</term> comes to possess knowledge of all things.

---

<header>THE PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA.</header>

### *Aphorism (34):*—Through meditation on the heart, one gains knowledge of the mind.

**Commentary:**—Within this "City of God" <note>original: "Brahma"; referring to the human body as a divine dwelling</note> there is a small space, a house shaped like a lotus—therein consciousness resides. From the <term>Sanyama</term> <note>a technical term for the combined practice of concentration, meditation, and total absorption</note> performed upon that center, there results a direct knowledge of the nature of the mind.

### *Aphorism (35):*—Experience is a concept that fails to distinguish between the quality of goodness and the Spirit, which are entirely different; since this experience exists for the sake of another, knowledge of the Spirit arises from <term>Sanyama</term> on that which exists for itself.

**Commentary:**—The quality of <term>Sattva</term> <note>the principle of light, purity, and harmony</note> belongs to the <term>Buddhi</term> <note>the higher intellect or discerning faculty</note>. Being of the nature of illumination, it suppresses the qualities of <term>Rajas</term> <note>activity or passion</note> and <term>Tamas</term> <note>darkness or inertia</note>. This leads to the realization of the difference between the quality of <term>Sattva</term> and the <term>Purusha</term> <note>the pure, eternal Spirit or True Self</note>. The Spirit, being essentially pure and consisting solely of consciousness, is absolutely unlike the changing quality of the intellect. 

"Experience" is the confused idea that these two—which are actually unconnected—are the same. This happens because all objects are presented to the Spirit through the intellect. This experience of the intellect exists for the "purpose of another" <note>meaning the intellect serves the Spirit, rather than existing for its own sake</note> and is therefore something that can be observed. By performing <term>Sanyama</term> on that which is distinct from the intellect—the Spirit's own nature as pure intelligence—there arises knowledge of the Soul itself. The Soul is not perceived by the intellect; rather, it is the Soul that perceives the thoughts based on its own nature. As it has been stated in the scriptures: "By what means is one to know the Knower?" <note>original: "Brihad Upa."; referring to the Brihadaranyaka Upanishad, one of the oldest philosophical texts of India</note>.

**Note:**—(1) "The Soul is not perceived..."—this is because it is Consciousness that perceives the unfeeling matter of the intellect, not the other way around.

<summary>This page explains how meditation on the heart reveals the workings of the mind and details the philosophical distinction between the pure Spirit (Purusha) and the refined intellect (Sattva).</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Sutras, Sanyama, Purusha, Sattva, Buddhi, Spirit, Consciousness, Heart Meditation, Brihadaranyaka Upanishad</keywords>

### *Aphorism (36)*:—From that <note>original: "tataḥ"; referring to the practice of Samyama on the heart or the self</note> proceed intuitional cognition, hearing, touch, sight, taste, and smell.

*Commentary*:—From intuition <note>term: Pratibha; a flash of spiritual illumination or spontaneous insight</note> proceeds the knowledge of the subtle, hidden, distant, past, and future objects; from hearing proceeds the perception of celestial sounds; from touch the perception of celestial touch; from sight the awareness of celestial form; from taste, the awareness of celestial taste, and from smell the awareness of celestial fragrance;—these are produced continuously.

---

### *Aphorism (37)*:—These are obstacles in the way of meditation; but perfections in the distracted (waking) state.

*Commentary*:—These—namely intuition and the other powers appearing in one of a meditative mind—become obstacles; because they interfere with discriminative knowledge <note>term: Viveka-khyati; the ultimate realization of the difference between the Self and the material world</note>; but appearing in those of distracted minds <note>original: "vyutthāna"; the ordinary state of consciousness where the mind wanders</note>, these are regarded as great perfections <note>term: Siddhis; supernatural accomplishments</note>.

---

### *Aphorism (38)*:—From the loosening of the cause of bondage, and by a knowledge of the pathways of the mind, proceeds the entrance of the mind into another body.

*Commentary*:—The confinement of the fickle and unsteady mind within the body is due to the force of karmic residues <note>term: Karma-ashaya; the latent stock of past actions that determines one's current life</note>. The loosening of this karma, which is the cause of bondage, is brought about by the power of meditation. The knowledge of the method of the mind's movement is also produced by meditation. Upon the exhaustion of karma, and through the knowledge of the workings of his own mind, the yogi <note>term: Yogi; a practitioner of yoga who has attained high levels of concentration</note> projects his mind into other bodies after withdrawing it from his own. The mind thus in—

### **Sutra (39):—From the subdual of the *<term>udâna</term> (the upward-moving breath)*, there comes the power of levitation and non-contact with water, mud, thorns, and the like.**

**Commentary:**—The function of the collective senses, characterized by the *<term>Prâna</term> (vital life-force)* and the rest, constitutes Life. Its action is fivefold: (1) The *<term>Prâna</term>*, extending from the heart and proceeding through the mouth and the nostrils; (2) the *<term>Samâna</term> (the balancing breath)*, extending to the navel, so called from its tendency to equalize or level; (3) the *<term>Apâna</term> (the downward breath)*, extending to the soles of the feet, so called from its declining or descending tendency; (4) the *<term>Udâna</term> (the upward breath)*, extending to the head, so called from its ascending tendency; (5) the pervading *<term>Vyâna</term> (the diffusive breath)*. Of these, *<term>Prâna</term>* is the chief. From mastery over *<term>Udâna</term>* results non-contact with water, mud, thorns, etc., as also the ability to ascend at the time of death. The practitioner attains to this in the character of a *<term>Vaśî</term> (a master of self)*.

---

### **Sutra (40):—From mastery over the *<term>Samâna</term>*, comes effulgence.**

**Commentary:**—One who has subdued the *<term>Samâna</term>* burns <note>This refers to a radiant aura or a "blazing" spiritual light</note> by the fire raised out of his own body.

### Sutra (44):—Mastery over the elements, from the *Perfect Discipline* <note>original: "Sanyama"; a combined practice of concentration, meditation, and absorption</note> with reference to grossness, character, subtlety, concomitance and usefulness.

*Commentary:—*The specific qualities sound and the rest, belonging to the Earth etc., together with the properties of ***shape*** and the rest, are named “gross;” this is the first form of the elements. The second form is their respective generic characteristic:—For example, shape for the Earth, viscidity for the Water, heat for Fire, velocity for Air, omnipresence for *Ether* <note>original: "Âkâsa"; the most subtle element</note>,—all this is what is included in the term “character.” The specific forms of these generic ones are Sound and the rest. As has been declared: “Of these (elements) <margin>ri ?</margin><note>The following phrase is underlined in the original text.</note> included in one class (that of ‘Element’), the only differentiation is through their distinguishing properties.” In this system, Substance is the aggregate of the generic and specific characteristics. Because the aggregate exists in a two-fold manner—(1) One in which the distinct names of the constituent members are suppressed—as ‘body,’ ‘tree,’ ‘forest,’ ‘crowd’ etc.; and (2) that in which the constituent members are mentioned—as ‘both men and gods,’—of which one portion are the ‘men’ and the other the ‘gods;’ and it is only by means of these two portions, that it is called an “aggregate.” Again the aggregate may be through difference as well as non-difference, (1) as ‘the grove of mangoes’,

<meta>This page continues the philosophical analysis of the five forms of the elements and the yogic mastery over them, following the discussion of "gross" form and "differentiation."</meta>

<page-num>125</page-num>
# PADA III—SUTRA 44.

Consider the phrase 'a crowd of Brahmanas' <note>The priestly or scholarly class in Vedic society</note> (where the genitive case always implies a difference between the group and the individuals); or consider the term "mango-grove" or 'Brahmana-crowd' (where there is no perceived difference between the collective whole and its individual members). This concept is further divided into two types: (1) those whose individual parts are physically separated from one another, and (2) those whose individual parts are not separated. Examples of the first type include a 'forest' or a 'crowd' (where the members making up the group exist as distinct entities); examples of the second type include a 'tree' or a 'body' (where the parts are integrated into a single unit). Of these, Substance belongs to this second kind of integrated whole—this is the view held by <term>Patanjali</term> <note>The traditional author of the Yoga Sutras</note>. This concludes the explanation of "form."

Now, what is the subtle form of these elements? (The reply is:) The Rudimentary Elements <note>original: "Tanmatras," the subtle essences of sound, touch, sight, taste, and smell</note>, from which the gross physical elements arise. The only fundamental part of these is the atom, which consists of both general and specific characteristics; thus, these form a collective whole where the parts coexist as one. This same principle applies to all rudimentary elements. This is the third form, known as "Subtlety."

Now begins the consideration of the fourth form of the elements: The attributes <note>original: "Gunas," the three fundamental qualities of nature</note>—being defined by the nature of illumination <note>Sattva</note>, activity <note>Rajas</note>, and inertness <note>Tamas</note> as they relate to their respective effects—are referred to by the term "concomitance."

Their fifth form is "usefulness" or fruition; this refers to the fact that these attributes exist for the purpose of providing experience and eventual spiritual liberation <note>original: "Apavarga"</note>. Because these attributes are inherent in the elements (both gross and rudimentary) as well as in physical objects (such as a jar or a cow), all things are understood to have a purpose.

Thus, through <term>Sanyama</term> <note>The combined practice of concentration, meditation, and total absorption</note> directed at these five forms, one achieves both the perception and the mastery of them. By mastering these five elemental forms, the <term>yogi</term> <note>A practitioner of yoga</note> becomes the "conqueror of the elements"; and from that mastery follows...

<summary>This page explains the five progressive forms of matter—Gross, Form, Subtlety, Concomitance, and Usefulness—and how a yogi gains mastery over the physical world through meditation on these stages.</summary>

### **Aphorism (45):—From that mastery proceed the power of Minification and the rest; as also perfection of the body, and the indestructibility of its functions.**

**Commentary:—**Of these, **Minification** <note>original: "Attenuation" or "Anima"; the power to become as small as an atom</note> is that whereby the **Yogi** <note>A practitioner of Yoga</note> becomes atomic; **Levity** or **Buoyancy** <note>original: "Laghima"</note> is that whereby he becomes light; by **Magnification** <note>original: "Illimitability" or "Mahima"</note> he becomes great; by **Reach** <note>original: "Prāpti"</note> he touches the moon with his finger-tip; “**Irresistible Will**” <note>original: "Prakamya"</note> is the non-frustration of desires, whereby he sinks into and rises to the surface of the earth as if it were water; by “**Mastery**” <note>original: "Vashitva"</note> he becomes the subduer of the elements and other objects, being himself unsubdued by others; by “**Supremacy**” <note>original: "Ishatva"</note> he becomes the dispenser of the origin, dissolution, and arrangement of the elements; “**Efficacy of Will**” <note>original: "Yatrakamavasayitva"</note> is the truthfulness of one's determination—that is, the arrangement of the elements and their causes in accordance with his will or determination. Though having the power, he does not change the fundamental nature of things (he does not turn the sun into a moon and so on). Why? Because these things have been determined by the will of the Primeval Perfect Being (**God**) of irresistible will. These are the eight Perfections or Occult Powers.

“The perfection of the body” will be explained in the next aphorism.

“The non-obstruction of its functions”—the earth does not obstruct, by its solid shape, the actions of the yogi’s body, inasmuch as he enters even into stone; the viscid water does not wet him; nor does the hot fire burn him; nor does the fast wind move him; and lastly, even in the open **Ether** <note>original: "Akasa"; the most subtle element, often translated as space or sky</note>, he can have his body hidden, insofar as he is invisible even to the Perfected Beings.

<keywords>Yoga Sutras, Siddhis, supernatural powers, elements, mastery of nature, Yogi, meditation, invisibility</keywords>

### PADA III—SUTRA 46-47.

### **Sutra (46):—Beauty (symmetry of form), Loveliness, Strength, and Adamantine Toughness (of the body) constitute Bodily Perfection.**

*Commentary:—*(The Yogi is) beautiful, lovely, and bright, possessing superb strength and a body as hard and indestructible as a diamond <note>The original term "adamantine" refers to the qualities of a diamond—unbreakable and enduring.</note>.

### **Sutra (47):—From <term>Sanyama</term> <note>Sanyama is the combined practice of concentration, meditation, and total absorption.</note> with regard to perception, nature, egoism, concomitance, and usefulness, results mastery over the sense-organs.**

*Commentary:—*Sound and the other objects of the senses, in both their general and specific forms, constitute that which is perceived. The function of the senses in relation to these objects is *perception*. This does not consist of perceiving the general form alone; for how could the mind perceive an object's specific details if they had not first been grasped by the senses?

"Nature"—the substance of the sense-organ—is the collection of "coexisting constituents" <note>The fundamental building blocks that make up a thing.</note> made of both specific and general effects of the <term>Buddhic Sattva</term> <note>The highest quality of intelligence, characterized by purity, light, and harmony.</note>, which is essentially made of illumination.

The third form is self-consciousness, consisting of "egoism"—the general state of which the individual senses are the specific expressions.

The fourth form consists of the three Attributes <note>The Gunas: Sattva (light), Rajas (activity), and Tamas (inertia).</note>, which are the nature of Illumination, Activity, and Inertness. These involve the process of ascertainment (the primary function of <term>Buddhi</term> or the higher intellect), of which the sense-organs and self-consciousness are merely modifications.

The fifth form is the inherent character of these Attributes—the fact that they exist solely to serve the purpose of the soul <note>The philosophical idea that the material world exists only to provide experience and eventual liberation for the conscious self.</note>.

By gradually performing <term>Sanyama</term> over these five forms, the yogi subdues each of them individually. From this fivefold conquest comes complete mastery over the senses.

# YOGA-PHILOSOPHY

*Sutra* (48):—**From that mastery proceed fleetness of body equal to that of the mind, perception independent of the physical organs, and complete mastery over the primary cause of matter.**

*Commentary:*—The acquisition of the ability to move the body with supernatural speed is "fleetness like that of the mind."

"Perception independent of the physical organs" is the functioning of the senses of the <term>Incorporeal</term> <note>original: "Videha." Refers to a yogi who has attained a state of consciousness independent of the physical body</note> in accordance with the time, place, and object desired by him.

"Mastery over the primary cause of matter" is supremacy over all the transformations of Nature.

These three perfections are called "Honey-like" <note>original: "Madhu-pratika." A stage of spiritual attainment where the yogi's wisdom is as sweet and nourishing as honey</note> and proceed from the previously mentioned mastery over the five sense-organs.

---

*Sutra* (49):—**To one who possesses only the distinct knowledge of the difference between the quality of Purity and the Spirit, there comes supremacy over all states of existence and omniscience.**

*Commentary:*—To one who rejoices in the supreme radiance (the High <term>Mastery</term> <note>original: "Vashîkara." The highest state of dispassion or control over desires</note>) of the intellect's purity, freed from all taint of Passion and Ignorance;—and who thus dwells solely in the knowledge of the distinction between Purity and the Spirit—to such a one comes "supremacy over all states of existence." This means that all types of qualities, whether active or passive, submit themselves to the absolute vision of their Lord, the <term>Knower of the Field</term> <note>original: "Kshetrajna." A technical term for the Soul or Spirit as the conscious witness of the body and the world</note>. "Omniscience" refers to the simultaneous, intuitive knowledge of all universal qualities as they exist in their past, present, and future states. This is the <term>Sorrowless</term> <note>original: "Vishoka." A state of perfection free from all suffering and mental obscuration</note> attainment. By reaching this, the yogi becomes all-knowing and moves freely, as the bonds of mental afflictions and distractions have fallen away.

---

<page-num>129</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER III—SUTRAS 50-51.</header>

## Sutra (50):—From indifference even toward that [omniscience], follows Isolation, once the seed of evils has been destroyed.

> **Commentary:—**When the causes of suffering and the effects of past actions have decayed, the <term>yogi</term> <note>A practitioner of Yoga</note> reflects thus: “This awareness of the distinction between the self and the world is merely a quality of 'Goodness' <note>Sattva: the quality of light, purity, and intelligence</note>, which is among the things to be eventually transcended; the Soul itself is unchanging and pure, distinct from this Goodness.” Then, in this detached practitioner, the seeds of suffering become unproductive—like a burnt grain of rice that can no longer sprout—and they disappear along with the mind's fluctuations. Once these have vanished, the Soul never again experiences the three-fold pain <note>Pains caused by oneself, by other living beings, or by natural forces</note>. Thus, the "Isolation" <note>Kaivalya: the ultimate state of liberation and independence</note> of the Soul consists in its absolute separation from the material attributes whose purpose has been fulfilled. These attributes were previously manifested as the functioning of karmic troubles; now, the soul abides in its own nature—that is, in pure Consciousness.

---

## Sutra (51):—There should be an avoidance of association with, and pride in, celestial temptations, for there is a possibility of the recurrence of evils.

> **Commentary:—**There are four classes of <term>yogis</term>: (1) The <term>Prathama-kalpika</term> <note>A beginner or neophyte in the first stage</note>, (2) the <term>Madhu-pratika</term> <note>One who has reached the "honeyed" state of insight</note>, (3) The <term>Prajna-jyoti</term> <note>One whose light of wisdom is fully ignited</note>, and (4) The <term>Atikranta-bhavaniya</term> <note>One who has transcended the need for further practice</note>. Among these, the first is the one whose light of intuition has just begun to function. The second possesses "truth-bearing wisdom." The third is the master of the elements and the sense-organs; he has secured what he has already achieved and is equipped with the means to achieve what remains (such as the "sorrowless" state <note>original: "vishoka"</note>). The fourth is the one who has moved beyond the need for further experience; his sole remaining purpose is the dissolution of the mind's activity, and he possesses the sevenfold ultimate knowledge.

<sig>17</sig>

<summary>This page describes the transition from high spiritual attainment to total liberation (Isolation), warning that even celestial temptations must be ignored. it also categorizes practitioners into four distinct stages of advancement.</summary>
<keywords>Isolation, Kaivalya, spiritual liberation, four types of yogis, celestial temptations, pure consciousness, Yoga Sutras, Patanjali</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the advanced stages of meditation, specifically addressing the temptations faced by a practitioner when approached by celestial beings.</meta>

# YOGA-PHILOSOPHY

To the <term>Brahmana</term> <note>A seeker of the highest reality or a member of the priestly class</note> who has directly perceived the <term>Madhumati</term> <note>Literally "honeyed"; the stage of "sweet" or "honey-like" wisdom where the mind is perfectly clear</note> stage, the gods—perceiving the purity of his goodness—invite him to enjoy celestial powers: "Well! Come and enjoy yourselves here! Desirable are these pleasures! And desirable is this girl! This drug stops old age and death! Airy is this conveyance! These are the <term>Kalpa-trees</term> <note>Wish-fulfilling trees of the celestial realms</note>! Sacred is the celestial <term>Ganga</term> <note>The river Ganges in its heavenly form</note>! Perfect are the great <term>Rishis</term> <note>Inspired sages or seers</note>! Excellent and agreeable are the <term>Apsaras</term> <note>Celestial nymphs or water spirits known for their beauty</note>! Supernatural the Eye and the Ear! Adamantine the body! Thou hast acquired a right to all this by thy qualities—so come and have recourse to this undying, undecaying abode, loved of the Gods."

Being thus invited, the <term>yogi</term> <note>A practitioner of yoga</note> is to think over the faults of attachment in this manner: "Being broiled in the fire of <term>metempsychosis</term> <note>The cycle of death and rebirth; the transmigration of the soul</note>, and passing through the dark abyss of birth and death, I have somehow or other acquired this light of <term>yoga</term>, the dispeller of the darkness of troubles. Of this light, the airy objects of sense born of desire are the opponents. So how am I, having acquired this light, to be deceived by a mirage-like longing for sensuous objects, and thus cause to burn within myself the dying embers of the fires of rebirth? So goodbye to you all, dreamy sensuous objects, the desire of poor people!" In this determined attitude, the <term>yogi</term> is to sit for meditation. 

Having avoided association, he is also to avoid pride—that is, he is not to take pride in the fact of his being invited even by the gods. Because if he is steeped in this pride and considers himself safe, he will not perceive himself being caught by the hair by Death. And then negligence—which looks for any hole or discrepancy in his conduct and is only suppressible by constant watchfulness—will find the aforementioned loophole and enliven the sleeping afflictions. Thence comes the possibility of evils. Thus, for one who avoids both association and pride, the contemplated end will be strengthened, and the ultimate goal to be contemplated will come to the front.

<summary>This page describes the "Madhumati" stage of yoga, where the practitioner is tempted by gods with celestial pleasures and must reject them through detachment and humility to avoid falling back into the cycle of rebirth.</summary>
<keywords>Madhumati, celestial temptation, detachment, pride, rebirth, yoga meditation, Apsaras, Rishis</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical commentary on the Yoga Sutras, specifically focusing on the nature of time and the practice of Sanyama (focused integration) to achieve higher discernment.</meta>

### Sutra (52) :—From **Sanyama** <note>Sanyama: A technical term in Yoga referring to the combined practice of concentration, meditation, and total absorption on a single object.</note> over the moments and
### their order, the knowledge born of discrimination.

**Commentary:—**As the smallest division of matter is the atom, so the smallest division of time is the *moment*—which is the time taken by the motion of an atom from one place to another; and the uninterrupted flow of this is “order.” Of moment and its order there is no aggregate; therefore Day, Month etc. are only aggregates of so many cognitions (each coming in one moment). Thus Time is devoid of reality, being a mere creation of the mind; but being correlated to the verbal cognitions, it appears as a distinct entity to ordinary people in the distracted state. The Moment thus becoming objective (i.e., an entity in itself) becomes also related to the (assumed) order; this order consists in the non-interruption of two moments,—to this (order) the time-knowing yogis <note>Yogis: Practitioners of Yoga who have attained advanced levels of spiritual insight.</note> give the name 'Time.' Two moments cannot occur together; for no order is possible for any two things occurring simultaneously. Order is the sequence of the following moment from the preceding one. <margin>!</margin> **Therefore the present is the only moment**, there being none either past or future; and as such there is no collective term for it (as *Time*). The future and the past moments (those that are popularly so called) are to be explained as referring to the different modifications <note>Modifications: Changes in the state or form of matter/mind.</note>. Thus by means of a single moment does all the world experience modifications; and all the properties are mere encumbrances on this single moment.

By the **Sanyama** over this moment and its order, there is direct perception of these, and thence proceeds the knowledge born of discrimination.

---

### the (difference of) two similars, when that dif-

# YOGA-PHILOSOPHY

<note>original: "Yoga-Darsana"</note>

...the difference is not marked out by kind, character, or position.

*Commentary*:—Between two similar objects resembling each other in position and character, the basis for distinguishing them exists in their difference of kind—as in the case of the **cow** and the **horse**. When there is similarity of position and kind, then the distinction exists in their character—as between the **Kalakshi cow** <note>A cow with black eyes or specific markings</note> and the **Swastimati cow** <note>A cow marked with the auspicious swastika sign</note>. Of two fruits of the same kind and character, the ground of difference lies in the difference of their position—<gloss>one being higher</gloss> and the other lower <gloss>within a tree</gloss>. If, however, <gloss>at the moment of</gloss> encountering the first, the mind of the observer is absorbed in the other, and as such the <gloss>first fruit also</gloss> is mentally moved to the place of the second, then the <gloss>order of position</gloss> becomes impossible to explain by ordinary means; but it can be understood through <term>discriminative knowledge</term> <note>Sanskrit: viveka-khyati; the ability to distinguish the true self from the material world</note> which is free from all doubt. How? The place occupied by the first fruit is different from that occupied by the second fruit; and these two fruits differ by the recognition of their respective positions; and the ground of difference between these two is the recognition of those positions. By this example, it is shown that the <term>Yogi</term> <note>An advanced practitioner of Yoga</note> recognizes the difference between two <term>atoms</term> <note>Sanskrit: paramanu; the smallest indivisible particles of matter</note> similar in kind, character, and position, by the direct perception of the time and place related to the first atom; the perception of the place of the second atom is due to the impossibility of the former place being occupied by the second atom at the same time as the first.

Others, on the other hand, describe the process in this way:—
> "It is the final specific property that produces the knowledge of difference. But in this case also, the ground of difference is the difference in position and character, as also the difference in shape and kind. The difference in time is accessible to the perception of the Yogi only."

Hence it has been declared:

<summary>This page explains how a Yogi uses discriminative knowledge to distinguish between two seemingly identical objects or atoms by perceiving subtle differences in their space and time coordinates.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga-Philosophy, discriminative knowledge, Yogi, atoms, perception, space and time, Kalakshi cow, Swastimati cow</keywords>

# SECTION III—APHORISMS 54-55.

->“ There is no real difference, in the absence of the differences of shape and kind ” says *Varshaganya*. <note>Varshaganya was an ancient teacher of the Samkhya school of philosophy.</note><-

---

**Aphorism (54):**—The knowledge born of discrimination is liberating, all-encompassing, eternally-present, and instantaneous.

**Commentary:**—“ Liberating ”—This refers to knowledge that is intuitive or not derived from worldly experience <note>original: "non-experiential"; see Section III, Aphorism 33</note>. “ *All-encompassing* ”—there is nothing that cannot be perceived by this knowledge.

“ *Eternally-present* ”—knowing everything in all forms and conditions and at all times: past, present, and future.

“ *Instantaneous* ”—perceiving every thing in every way, at the same moment.

Such is the knowledge born of discrimination <term>Viveka-khyati: the ability to distinguish between the pure consciousness and the material world</term>, in its complete form. The light of yoga is a part of this, so long as it is achieved by resting on the “honeyed” stage <note>The "honeyed" stage (Madhumati) refers to a high level of spiritual attainment where the mind is as sweet and pure as honey</note>.

To one who has acquired discriminative knowledge or to one who has not, there accrues:

<gloss>Does the purity of the material mind-stuff become like the purity of the pure consciousness?</gloss>
**Aphorism (55):**—Liberation occurs when the purity of the Intellect and the Pure Consciousness are equal.

**Commentary:**—When the Intellect <term>Buddhi: the highest faculty of the material mind</term> is washed clean of all taints of Passion and Ignorance <note>original: "Foulness and Darkness," referring to the qualities of Rajas and Tamas</note>, and has its activity restricted only to recognizing the difference between the intellect and the soul, the seed of all suffering is burnt up. Then the Intellect becomes similar to the Soul in its purity. The purity of the Soul <term>Purusha: the true, unchanging self</term> consists in the absence of all imposed worldly experience. Under such circumstances, Liberation <term>Kaivalya: the state of absolute independence and isolation from suffering</term> results for the perfect as well as the imperfect—for one who has discriminative knowledge, and for one who has not <note>This suggests that once the seeds of karma are destroyed, the state of liberation is inevitable</note>. Upon the attainment of wisdom by one who has burnt up the seeds of trouble, there is no need for anything else. Per-

# YOGA-DARSANA

The perfections arising from meditation, as well as the highest wisdom, are all contained within what is called <term>Sattwic purity</term> <note>Sattwic refers to the quality of light, goodness, and clarity in Indian philosophy; here it denotes a mind free from passion or lethargy</note>. In actual practice, however, it is wisdom that destroys the lack of spiritual perception—or ignorance—and once that ignorance is suppressed, the resulting mental afflictions cease to arise. The absence of these afflictions leads, in turn, to the absence of <term>karmic fruition</term> <note>The "ripening" or results of one's past actions that normally bind a person to the cycle of rebirth</note>. In this elevated state, the fundamental attributes of nature, having completed all their necessary functions, no longer present themselves to the soul’s view. It is in this very cessation that the <term>Soul’s Isolation</term> <note>Original: "Kaivalya." This is the ultimate goal of Yoga: the state where the soul is completely detached from the material world and the mind</note> consists. In this state, the Soul becomes truly isolated and shines forth in the pure radiance of its own original, eternal form.

<summary>This page describes the final liberation of the soul, explaining how spiritual wisdom removes ignorance and karmic consequences to achieve a state of absolute isolation and self-radiance.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga-Darsana, Sattwic purity, Karma, Kaivalya, Soul's Isolation, spiritual wisdom, liberation</keywords>

<header>PART IV—APHORISM 2.</header>

# Section IV.

## ON ABSOLUTE LIBERATION. <note>original: "Kaivalya," often translated as Isolation or Independence; the final goal of Yoga where the soul is freed from matter.</note>

**Aphorism (1):—**Supernatural powers are produced by birth, medicinal herbs, sacred chants <note>original: "mantras"</note>, self-discipline, or Deep Concentration.

***Commentary:—***The Power resulting from birth is brought about by a change in the physical body. "*By herbs:*" for example, the medicinal elixirs found in the realm of the Demons <note>original: "Asuras," beings often in conflict with the gods; "Patala" refers to the subterranean or netherworld regions.</note>. "*By sacred chants:*" such as the attainment of "**Minuteness**" <note>original: "Anima," the power to become as small as an atom</note>, which allows for the ability to travel through the sky. "*By self-discipline:*" the fulfillment of one's will; for example, being able to take on any desired form, the practitioner of yoga <term>Yogi: one who practices the system of Yoga to achieve spiritual liberation</term> moves about here, there, and everywhere, according to his own unhindered will. The Powers resulting from Deep Concentration have already been described in the previous section.

**Notes:—**(1) "*Change in the physical body*"—Sometimes the actions of a practitioner in their earthly life are so highly developed that they are reborn directly among the celestial beings with a divine body; in such cases, powers like minuteness and the rest come to them naturally.

(2) "*Have been described*"—referring to the third section of this work.

->Regarding the transformation of the body and the senses into a different state:<-

**Aphorism (2):—**The transformation into another state results from the overflow of the natural tendencies of the material cause. <note>original: "prakrity-apurat," the filling in or pouring in of creative nature.</note>

***Commentary:—***Once the previous form has dissolved, the production of the subsequent change is due to a re-arrangement of the fundamental particles. The material causes of the body and the senses, in facilitating their modifications or effects through this transformation, depend upon instrumental causes such as merit <note>original: "dharma"</note> and other moral qualities.

**Notes:—**"*Overflow of the material cause*"—The material causes of the body are the five elements (Earth, Water, Fire, Air, and Ether), and the material cause of the senses is the "I-sense" or Self-consciousness; the 'transformation' refers to the way these elements rearrange themselves to form a new type of existence.

<summary>This page explains the five sources of supernatural powers—ranging from birth and alchemy to meditation—and describes how the physical body and senses can transform into new states through the rearrangement of their underlying material elements.</summary>

<keywords>supernatural powers, Siddhis, rebirth, mantras, self-discipline, meditation, Patala, Anima, bodily transformation, material cause, Prakriti, self-consciousness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion on the transformation of the physical body and senses through the rearrangement of material particles, specifically addressing the role of moral actions in this process.</meta>

<page-num>136</page-num>
-># YOGA PHILOSOPHY<-
<note>original: "YOGA-DARSANA"</note>

mentioned in the <term>aphorism</term><note>A concise statement of a scientific principle or philosophical truth; here referring to the Yoga Sutras.</note> consists in the rearrangement of the various particles making up such causes. It is this <term>transmutation</term><note>The action of changing or the state of being changed into another form.</note>, brought about by the past actions (virtuous or otherwise, of the agent), which leads to the change of the kind of body and the sense-organs. For example, various deeds tend to transmute the particles of the elements and so on, in such a manner as to bring about a celestial body for the agent.

**<term>Sutra</term><note>A rule or aphorism in Sanskrit literature.</note> (3):—To the material causes, the instrument is non-efficient; from it proceeds the piercing of the covering—as in the case of the husbandman.**

*Commentary:*—The instruments in the shape of virtue and so on, are no helps to the mutations of the material causes; for certainly the cause is not urged by the effect. How then? “*From it proceeds the piercing of the covering and so on.*” As the husbandman—desiring to irrigate, from a full supply of water in one field, another field in the same plane, or in one lower or higher—does not carry the water by means of his hands, but only cuts through the <term>bunds</term><note>An embankment or dam used to retain water in a field.</note>, on the opening of which the water of itself flows through the adjoining field;—so in the same manner, virtue pierces the covering of the material causes, in the shape of vice, on the removal whereof, the material causes of their own accord, supply their various modifications. Or again, as the same husbandman in the same field is not capable of supplying either liquid or solid ingredients to the root of the paddy; what he does is to remove from the field all foreign weeds and plants, on the removal whereof the ingredients of themselves enter into the paddy-roots; so in the same manner, virtue is only the cause of the suppression of vice—purity and impurity being diametrically opposed to each other—and it is no cause in the operation of the material cause. In this connection <term>Nandiswara</term><note>A figure in Hindu mythology who was transformed from a human into a divine being through intense devotion.</note> and others, should be quoted as examples. On the other hand, vice also only tends to suppress virtue; and then follows the change into impurity—*for example* <term>Nahusha</term><note>A king in Hindu mythology who was cursed to become a serpent due to his pride.</note> changed into an <term>Ajagara serpent</term><note>A large python or boa.</note> and the like.

<summary>This page explains how moral virtue acts as a catalyst for physical transformation by removing obstacles, much like a farmer opening a dam to let water flow naturally. It emphasizes that virtue does not "create" the change but allows the inherent material causes to manifest new forms, such as celestial or animal bodies.</summary>

When the practitioner of yoga <note>original: "yogi"</note> creates for himself many bodies, do all of these share one and the same mind, or does each possess a separate mind? The reply follows:

->*Aphorism (4)*:—The created minds <note>original: "internal organs" or "cittas"</note> proceed from the sense of "I-am-ness" <note>original: "Asmita" or Self-consciousness</note> alone.<-

*Commentary*:—The practitioner produces these created minds using only the sense of "I-am-ness," which is the root cause of the individual mind. Consequently, each created body possesses its own separate mind.

---

->*Aphorism (5)*:—Despite the diverse tendencies of the many, the directing mind is one.<-

*Commentary*:—“How can the actions of many separate minds follow the direction of a single mind?”—Thinking thus, the practitioner establishes one primary mind that urges the others to action. From this, the coordinated diversity of action follows.

**Notes**:—(1) > “In the preceding aphorism, the question having been solved as to how a single individual can provide thinking principles for many bodies, the question arises as to how can many thinking principles act in concert and preserve the unity of the creator? If this unity be not admitted, there would be so many independent individuals, each following the bent of his own mind; and the idea of one individual acting as many without any loss of individuality would be lost. This doubt is removed by the explanation that the thinking principles provided to the many are in reality one, and entirely controlled by the primary thinking principle (that which is related to the hidden <term>occult: supernatural or beyond ordinary perception</term> power displayed), of which they are mere sparks”—Rajendra Lal Mitra—“*The Yoga Aphorisms of Patanjali*”

---

->*Aphorism (6)*:—Of these, the mind born of meditation is free from latent impressions.<-

*Commentary*:—The created minds are of five kinds, as the supernatural powers may be due to (1) birth, (2) medicinal herbs, (3) incan-

### Aphorism (7):—The actions of the Yogi are neither white nor black; those of others are of three kinds.

**Commentary:—**The classes of actions are fourfold—(1) the black, (2) the white-black, (3) the white,—and (4) the non-white-non-black. Of these the black kind belongs to the evil-minded. The white-black, is performable by external means; and in connection with this, the <gloss>✓</gloss>accumulation of karmic residua is due to the infliction of pain and doing of kindness to others; the white one belongs to those given to penance, study and contemplation; this kind, being confined solely to the internal organ, cannot be performed by external means, and as such, is not due to the infliction of pain on others. The "Non-white-non-black" belongs to the Renunciates <note>original: "Sanyasis" — those who have reached the fourth stage of life, abandoning worldly ties</note>, whose troubles are at an end and who are equipped with the final or highest body only. Of these, to the Yogi belongs the non-black 
<margin>black</margin>
because he has renounced all desire for fruition <note>The "fruit" or result of an action</note>—, and the non-white—because of non-acceptance. Those of other beings, are of the former three kinds.

# CHAPTER IV—APHORISMS 8-9.

(3) "**Because he has renounced etc.**"—He is untouched by the effects of virtuous deeds because he has renounced all desire for their effects in the shape of future aggrandisement; and he is free also from the effects of the vicious actions because such he never performs.

### Aphorism (8):—From that <insert>the threefold action</insert> comes the manifestation of the subconscious impressions which are compatible with the ripening of that specific karma.

*Commentary:—* "**From that**" *that is,* from the threefold action <note>referring to white, black, and mixed actions described previously</note> "**which are compatible with its ripening**"—*that is*, suitable to the particular results of actions of specific kinds; that is to say, only those <term>residua<note>original: "vasanas"; the latent potencies or "smells" left in the mind by past experiences</note></term> that assist the fulfillment of the action are manifested. For a celestial or godly action, in its function, can never lead to the manifestation of hellish, animal, or human impressions; on the contrary, what is manifested or awakened thereby are only the impressions that conform to a celestial existence. The same principle holds true regarding hellish, animal, and human actions.

*Notes:—* (1) *Of particular kinds*—*that is*, actions that are either virtuous or otherwise.

### Aphorism (9):—Because memory and subconscious impressions are identical in form, there is an uninterrupted connection between them, even when separated by species, location, and time.

*Commentary:—* The beginning of the results of an action leading to a birth as a cat, for example, is always awakened or manifested by the specific cause which leads to that state;

<summary>This page explains how specific past actions (karma) trigger only the relevant subconscious impressions (vasanas) suitable for a particular rebirth, ensuring continuity of character even across different lifetimes and species.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion of the Yoga Sutras, specifically focusing on the mechanics of karma and the continuity of subconscious impressions across multiple lifetimes.</meta>

<page-num>140</page-num>
<header>THE PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA.</header>

and as such, if this fruition—even though removed or interrupted either by hundreds of species or by great distance, or by hundreds of cycles—again happens to become manifested only through its own special manifested causes; then it becomes vivid by taking hold of the <term>residua</term> <note>original: "vasana"; the subconscious imprints or "smells" left behind by past actions</note> brought about by the previous experience of feline birth. Why? Because, of these (<term>residua</term>), the only manifesting cause is an analogous action, even though they may be removed therefrom; and in this manner there is an uninterruptibility or continuity of relation (between the <term>residua</term> and its analogous manifesting action). Why? Because “of the uniformity of memory and the impressions”. (That is to say)—As the experiences, so the impressions; and these latter are exactly similar to the <term>Karmic residua</term> <note>The latent potentiality of past deeds waiting to ripen</note>; and lastly as the <term>residua</term>, so the memory (or Instinct). Thus we see that memory or instinct is due to impressions removed therefrom by class, locality and time. This memory again produces impressions. Thus these impressions due to memory are manifested by the force of the attainment of the function of <term>Karmic residua</term>. Consequently, even of the interrupted (actions and fruition) the sequence is established, on account of the uninterruptibility of the causal relation.

---

Notes:—(1) “ *These are exactly similar to the Karmic residua* ”—The purport is that, as the <term>apurva</term> <note>literally "not preceded"; the "Unseen Force" or merit/demerit that bridges the gap between an act and its future result</note>, the Unseen Force, though produced by transitory actions, is yet lasting and capable of bringing about its effects after a time; so in the same manner impressions, though produced by fleeting experiences, are yet lasting.

(2) “ *attainment of the function &c.* ”—i.e., by the rousing or vivifying energy of the karmic residua.

(3) “ According to the preceding aphorism, the residua of the former births are the causes of manifestation of certain effects; but since concomitance is necessary between cause and effect, while the intervention of many dissimilar births between two similar causes an interruption, it may be urged that residua are not the cause of memory, as alleged. The objection is met by saying that the memory remains, and therefore the sequence is, obviously, not broken ”

-> --- <-

<sig>Mitra.</sig>

<summary>This page explains how subconscious impressions (vasanas) and memories persist across many lifetimes and different species, ensuring that karma remains consistent even when interrupted by long periods of time. It argues that a "feline" instinct can resurface whenever the right conditions arise, regardless of how many human or other lives occurred in between.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga-Darsana, karma, reincarnation, subconscious impressions, vasana, apurva, memory, instinct, causal relation, feline birth</keywords>
