<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion of the nature of the mind and the subconscious impressions that drive human existence, following the analysis of how actions produce results across different lifetimes.</meta>

<page-num>141</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER IV—APHORISM 10.</header>

### *Aphorism* (10) — Furthermore, there is no beginning to these impressions, because the desire for life is eternal.

*Commentary:*—There is no beginning to these <term>residua</term> <note>original: "residua" (Sanskrit: vasanas); the subconscious imprints or "smells" left behind by past experiences that shape future character</note>, because the desire for self-preservation is eternal. The selfish <gloss>desire</gloss>—"may I not cease to exist," "may I live"—which is found in every single individual, cannot be called a mere natural product of the current body (and thus temporary). Why? Because, how else could a newly born infant—who has not yet experienced death in this life—feel any fear of death? Such fear can only be caused by a memory of previous aversion and pain. Certainly, a purely physical object does not require such a complex mental cause. The fact, therefore, is that the <term>internal organ</term> <note>the mind-stuff or psyche</note>, chained to these eternal <term>residua</term>, is triggered by certain causes to activate specific impressions, which then present themselves for the soul to experience.

Other theorists have asserted that the internal organ expands and contracts, like a <gloss>lamp placed in a jar or a mansion</gloss> <note>an analogy suggesting the mind changes size to fit the body it inhabits</note>; they argue it takes the same form as the physical body, which would explain its existence in the womb and its rebirth. The Masters <note>referring to the authoritative sages of the Yoga tradition</note> however, lay down that it is only the *function* of the internal organ that expands and contracts, while the organ itself is omnipresent. This internal organ is dependent on causes such as virtue and vice. These causes are of two kinds—external and internal. The former are those achieved through physical means, such as singing hymns, charity, and showing reverence; the latter depend on the mind alone, such as faith and similar qualities. As it is declared: "The practices of the <term>Yogi</term> <note>one who practices the discipline of Yoga</note>, such as friendship and compassion, are naturally independent of external tools, and as such, they bring about the highest virtue." Of these two types of causes, the mental is the stronger. Why? Because nothing can supersede wisdom and dispassion. Who could have cleared the <term>Dandaka forest</term> <note>a vast, legendary forest in Indian epic literature, often associated with exile and spiritual struggle</note> through physical labor alone without mental power? And who else could drink the entire ocean dry, like the sage <term>Agastya</term> <note>a legendary Vedic seer said to have swallowed the ocean to reveal demons hiding at the bottom</note>?

<summary>This page argues that the subconscious impressions (vasanas) driving human behavior are beginningless, evidenced by an infant's instinctive fear of death. It also debates whether the mind physically changes size or if only its focus expands and contracts, concluding that mental power is superior to physical action.</summary>

<keywords>subconscious impressions, eternity of desire, fear of death, internal organ, mental power, Agastya, Dandaka forest, rebirth</keywords>

-># YOGA-DARSANA<-

**Notes:—**(1) “ *How else...aversion and pain* ”: Supply here: ‘if you do not accept the residua <note>residua: the subconscious impressions or mental "seeds" left behind by past experiences</note> of pain caused by death, which occurred in the previous births’—This objection is levelled against the atheists who assert that everything is the product of nature, and deny the existence of anything eternal.

(2) “ *For certainly a natural object &c.* ”—anything that is due to mere nature cannot be dependent on any cause, like the heat of fire.

(3) “ *The internal organ through certain causes &c.* ”—Such a cause is the action whose time of function happens to arrive at the time being.

(4) “ *Gets hold of &c.* ”—i. e., such residua become manifested.

(5) “ *Like the jar—&—mansion lamp* ”—The light of a lamp is contracted or expands, according as it happens to be placed in a jar or in a large house; similarly the internal organ <note>internal organ: the mind or *antahkarana*</note>, through its substrate being either that of a feline or an elephantine body, would contract or expand, and as such would be small or large accordingly; and such the mind can be said to be of the same size as the body it happens to occupy. This theory is opposed to the atomic character of the mind, as laid down by Kanada <note>Kanada: the ancient sage who founded the Vaisheshika school of Indian philosophy, which proposed an atomic theory of the universe</note> and his followers. The view of the author himself is that the mind is neither atomic, nor dependent on the size of its substrate; but it is vast, or omnipresent.

(6) “ *Its presence &c.* ”—During cyclic dissolution <note>cyclic dissolution: *pralaya*, the periodic destruction of the manifest universe</note> the mind is said to be living in the subtle body, and hence of the same form; and it is this subtle body equipped with the aforesaid mind which takes its birth again in the following cycle. Thus according to these theorists, such existence of the internal organ between two births can only be explained by making the mind depend for its form on the body it occupies; according to the author however the mind is omnipresent, and as such there is no difficulty in explaining any facts with regard to it.

(7) “ *The highest virtue* ”—i. e., the white action <note>white action: pure or meritorious deeds that produce positive spiritual results</note> (see above).

---

*Commentary:—*“ *Cause* ”—From virtue results pleasure, and from vice pain; from pleasure proceeds attachment and from pain aversion; thence follows endeavour; and the agent acting through this endeavour, by mind, words and body, tends to favour one individual and hurt the other; thence follow

<meta>This page continues the philosophical analysis of the nature of mental impressions and the cycle of rebirth, specifically addressing how eternal desires can be eliminated by removing their underlying conditions.</meta>

# SECTION IV—APHORISM 11.

Virtue and vice, pleasure and pain, attachment <insert>and</insert> aversion—this operation constitutes the wheel of <term>metempsychosis</term><note>The transmigration of the soul or the cycle of rebirth (Samsara).</note>; of this ever-turning wheel, the guiding force is Ignorance <note>original: "Avidya"</note>, the root of all troubles. Such is the “cause.”

“*The effect*” is that on which is based the existence of virtue and the rest; (for) there is no production of that which never before existed <note>This refers to the doctrine of Satkaryavada, which suggests that an effect pre-exists in its cause.</note>. The mind, equipped with its faculties, is the “substratum” <note>The underlying base or foundation.</note> of the <term>residua</term><note>Mental impressions or subconscious tendencies (Vasanas) left behind by past actions.</note>; <insert>for</insert> as when the faculties of the mind have been destroyed, the residuum cannot continue to exist, for want of a substratum.

That with a view to which an object enlivens a particular residuum, is the “support” of such a residuum.

Thus, all residua, being held together by these—cause, effect, substratum and support—when these latter cease to exist, there follows the absence of the residua based upon them.

Notes:—(1) The aphorism answers the question—how can the eternal be undone? The purport of the reply is that, since desires are the sum total of cause, effect, substratum and support, one has to remove those conditions which produce desires, and that removal *by that very fact* removes desires.

(2) “*There is no production etc.*”—This is added in order to meet the following objection: “How can there be any permanent conglomeration of the cause with the effect? Or again, how can the absence of the effect lead to the absence of the cause? For certainly the cause exists even during the absence of the effect.” The sense of the reply is that, we deny the production of any entity that never existed (see *Verses on the Samkhya Philosophy—9* <note>original: "Sankhyakarika—9"</note>). All that we attribute to the present cause is the faculty of manifesting the effect which has all along lain latent in its material cause. And of course the permanent conglomeration of the cause with the effect becomes quite compatible when considered with reference to the effect in its latent state.

---

(Objection):—‘There is no production for a non-entity; nor is there any destruction of an entity; under the circumstances, how would the residua, being entities, be undone?’—(Reply)—

<summary>This page explains how mental impressions (residua) are sustained by four factors—cause, effect, substratum, and support—and argues that by removing these conditions, one can end the cycle of rebirth. It addresses the philosophical paradox of how "eternal" desires can be eliminated by citing the Samkhya theory of manifestation.</summary>

<keywords>metempsychosis, Ignorance, cause and effect, substratum, mental residua, Vasanas, Samkhya philosophy, Yoga Sutras, liberation</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical analysis of time and existence within the Yoga-Sutras, specifically addressing the ontological status of the past and the future.</meta>

# YOGA-PHILOSOPHY <note>original: "Yoga-Darsana," one of the six orthodox schools of Hindu philosophy</note>

<margin>\</margin>
*Aphorism (12):*—**The past and the future exist in their true nature, due to the difference in the developmental states of their underlying qualities.**

*Commentary:*—That which is *yet to be* is the “*future*,” that which *has already occurred* is the “*past*,” and that which *is currently performing its function* is the present. These three states are perceivable through specialized spiritual insight <note>original: "Yogic cognition"</note>. If these states did not exist in reality, there could be no perception of them, as perception cannot exist without an object. Therefore, the past and the future must exist in a real sense. Furthermore, if the causal force that brings about the results of our actions—leading either to worldly experience or to ultimate Liberation <note>original: "Isolation" or Kaivalya, the state of the soul's independence from matter</note>—were a non-entity, then the efforts of spiritual masters to work through that force would be impossible to explain. A cause is only capable of bringing into the present an effect that already exists in a latent state; it does not create an entirely new entity from nothing. A fully established cause merely assists the effect in manifesting in a specific way, but it does not produce anything fundamentally new.

<margin>का</margin>
An object is always possessed of various qualities <note>original: "properties" or dharmas</note>; and these qualities exist according to different states or conditions. Although the present state exists in a tangible, material form because it is linked to a specific individual moment, the same cannot be said for the *past* and the *future*. How, then, do they exist? The *future* exists in its *potential* form (that which is waiting to be revealed); just as the *past* exists in the form of the individual experience that *has already been*. The full *manifestation* of a form is only possible in the conditions of the *present*; it is not possible for the conditions of the past or the future to be manifest in that same way. Yet, while one condition is active, it is entirely consistent that the other two conditions exist in a close, underlying relationship with the same object. Therefore, the existence of these three conditions is not something that arises out of non-existence.

<summary>This page argues that the past and future are not non-existent, but rather exist in a latent or potential state within the underlying qualities of matter, awaiting their turn to manifest in the present.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga-Sutras, Patanjali, metaphysics of time, causality, manifestation, Kaivalya, potentiality, latent states</keywords>

### Sutra (13):—They are manifested or subtle and consist of the fundamental attributes. <note>The "attributes" refers to the three Gunas: Sattva (goodness/light), Rajas (passion/activity), and Tamas (darkness/inertia).</note>

**Commentary:—** The previously mentioned properties, existing in three conditions of time, consist of the manifested—that is, the present—and the subtle—that is, the past and future; these latter constitute the six non-specific elements <note>original: "avishesha," referring to the subtle states of matter in Sankhya philosophy</note>. All of these, however, are merely specific formations of the Attributes, and therefore, in reality, share the nature of these Attributes. As the authoritative teaching states: “The highest form of the Attributes does not fall within the range of sight; that which does come within the range of vision is entirely insignificant, like an illusion <note>original: "Maya"</note>.”

**Notes:** (1)—“The simple proposition in this aphorism is that all properties are mere modifications of the three primary Attributes (Goodness, Passion, and Darkness). Circumstances may make them subtle or gross; but, whether one or the other, they consist of nothing but the three Attributes... modified in some form or another. They are mere modes of being, but not radically different beings.”—*Mitra*.

(2) “*The six non-specific etc.*”—The *Explanation of Yoga* <note>original: "Yogavartika," a sub-commentary by Vijnanabhikshu</note> does not accept this specific reading of the text.

(3) “Injunction”—This quotation is said to be from a work on the Sankhya philosophy by the Sage <term>Rishi: a realized poet or sage of ancient India</term> Varshaganya, who is mentioned in the *Moonlight of Sankhya Truth* <note>original: "Sankhya Tattwa-Kaumudi"</note>.

Similarly, when these same attributes take on the nature of the object of perception, there is another modification in the form of sound, making sound the object. Of sound and the rest, resembling—

## **Sutra (15):**—**Notwithstanding the sameness of the object, the course of the two are distinct, from diversity of the internal organ.**

### **Commentary:**—A single object, occupying many internal organs <note>The "internal organ" or *antahkarana* refers to the mind, ego, and intellect</note>, is (said to be) “common”; and this (object) is not due to the agency of a single internal organ (or mind), nor to that of many minds; it exists by itself. How? Because of the diversity of minds, notwithstanding the sameness of the object. That is to say, even on the sameness of the object, the mind has the idea of pleasure, through virtue, and also the idea...

<summary>This page argues against the Nihilist view that the physical world is a mere mental projection, asserting instead that objects exist independently of the observer. # SECTION IV—SUTRA 15.

...of pain from the same object, through vice; from the same, the idea of delusion, through ignorance; and lastly from that very object, the idea of indifference through right vision <note>The author argues that because different people have different emotional reactions to the same thing, the thing must exist independently of their individual minds.</note>. Under such circumstances, to whose mind can such an object owe its existence? Certainly it is not likely that the object created by one mind should affect another mind. Consequently the *object* and the *idea* (or cognition)—distinct from each other on account of their respective characters of the cognisable <note>the thing being known</note> and the cogniser <note>the mind that knows</note>—the course is distinct; and there is not the slightest tinge of any confusion among these.

According to the <term>Sankhya</term> <note>One of the six classical schools of Indian philosophy, emphasizing a dualism between consciousness and matter.</note> view again, the object is endowed with the three Attributes <note>The three *Gunas* or qualities of nature: Sattva (goodness), Rajas (passion), and Tamas (darkness).</note>; and the function of the Attributes is mobile; hence the object is related to the minds through the instrumentality of virtue and the rest, and through its different forms, becomes the cause of the different cognitions, appearing in accordance with the aforesaid instruments.

**Notes :** (1) A simple object gives rise to different ideas in different minds, through the difference in the action of the three Attributes (*See <term>Sankhya-tattva-kaumudi</term>* <note>original: "Sankhya-tattva-kaumudi"—a famous 9th-century commentary on Sankhya philosophy.</note>—, 7 and *<term>Bhoja's commentary</term>* <note>A 11th-century commentary on the Yoga Sutras by King Bhoja.</note>.)—“On beholding an attractive woman, pleasure is felt by an amorous person, pain by her rival, and disgust by an ascetic."

(2) “ *and certainly it is not likely etc.*”—For if it were so, the one person’s cognition of redness would produce the same idea in the minds of all persons.

Some people <note>Referring to the Buddhist Yogacara or "Mind-Only" school.</note> have thus declared: “The object is coexistent with cognition, because it is cognisable, like pleasure and the like”—These people by this means set aside the commonality (of object and cognition, mentioned above), and thus deprive the object of its character, at all times, past as well as present.

->( To these subjective Idealists it is replied.)—<-

<page-num>148</page-num>
-># THE PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA<-

## Aphorism (16):—**The object is not dependent upon a single mind; for if it were not perceived by that mind, what would then become of it?**

*Commentary:—*If an object’s existence depended entirely on a single individual's mind, then if that mind became distracted or entered a state of deep meditative suppression, the object would no longer be perceived. Furthermore, because the object would be tied exclusively to that one mind, it could not be perceived by any other person’s mind either. In such a case, the object would become “<term>uncognised</term>” (<term>apramāṇaka</term> <note>original: "apramāṇaka"; literally "unproven" or "without a means of valid knowledge"</note>); that is to say, its nature would be understood by no one. Under those conditions, would the object even exist? 

Furthermore, if the object were merely a product of being connected to a mind, how could it be produced in the first place? Parts of an object not currently before a specific mind would simply cease to be; for example, if one did not perceive the back of an animal, the stomach would likewise vanish from reality. For these reasons, the object must be an independent entity, common to all who perceive it. Minds, too, are independent and function differently for every person. Cognition (or perception) results from the meeting of these two independent realities—the object and the mind—and this interaction constitutes the individual's experience.

**Note: (1)** This aphorism, which is omitted in the commentary by <term>Bhoja</term> <note>Bhoja was an 11th-century king and scholar who wrote a famous commentary on the Yoga Sutras</note>, is intended to answer the following objection: “Even if we grant that the object is distinct from the act of knowing it, the object is still <term>insentient</term> <note>lacking consciousness or the ability to feel</note>. It cannot reveal itself without a mind to illuminate or manifest it. Therefore, the object can only exist at the same time as the cognition and never at any other time.” 

The aphorism solves this difficulty by asking: where does the object come from? If it is created by the perceiver, is there one perceiver or many? The idea of many separate creators for one object contradicts our shared experience. However, if the object were the product of just one person's mind, then the moment that person stopped paying attention, the object would vanish. No one else would be able to see it, which contradicts the common experience of multiple people observing the same thing.

<summary>This page argues against the idea that objects are mere creations of the mind, asserting instead that objects exist independently and are shared across the experiences of different individuals.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga Philosophy, Yoga Sutras, Patanjali, epistemology, external reality, perception, Bhoja, cognition, independence of objects</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical analysis of the nature of the mind and its relationship to the soul or spirit, following the discussion of mental modifications in the previous section.</meta>

<page-num>149</page-num>
->CHAPTER IV—APHORISMS 17-19.<-

### Aphorism (17):—A thing is known or unknown, in consequence of the necessity of the mind being tinged by it.

**Commentary:—The object, being of the nature of a magnet, connects with itself and tinges the mind, which is of the nature of soft iron. The object whereby the mind is tinged, is *known*; and all objects besides this are *unknown*. Thus on account of the object being known and unknown, the mind is modifiable <note>The term "modifiable" refers to the mind's ability to change its shape or state to reflect the objects it perceives.</note>.**

**Notes:—(1) “Being of the nature of a magnet etc.”—Like the magnet, the object is devoid of all action in itself, but draws the iron-like active mind to itself by means of the sense-organs and tinges it—that is, moulds it into its own form.**

**(2) “On account of the object being known etc.”—that is, If the mind were not modifiable, the fact of the object being known or unknown would be inexplicable. This fact also serves to distinguish the mind from the spirit <note>original: "Purusha"</note> to whom all objects are *always* known.**

---

He to whom such a mind itself is an object, to such a one—

### Aphorism 18:—The functions of the mind are always known, for its presiding spirit is unmodifiable.

**Commentary:—If like the mind, the spirit also were liable to mutation then would its objects also, in the shape of the functions of the mind, be known and unknown, like sound and other objects. But the constant consciousness and perceptibility of the mind leads to the inference of the immutable (or constant) character of its presiding spirit.**

---

The following question may arise—The mind itself may be illuminative of itself as well as of the objects, like fire; against this it is declared—

### Aphorism (19):—It is not self-illuminative, since it is perceptible.

<summary>This page explains that the mind is a changing instrument that perceives objects by being "colored" by them, whereas the underlying Spirit is unchanging and always aware of the mind's activities. It argues that the mind cannot be self-aware because it is an object of perception itself.</summary>

<keywords>Yoga Sutras, philosophy of mind, perception, Spirit, Purusha, mental modifications, self-illumination, immutable consciousness</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical commentary on the nature of the mind and consciousness within the Yoga-Darshana tradition, specifically addressing the perception of the mind itself.</meta>

<page-num>150</page-num>
-># PHILOSOPHY OF YOGA<-

*Commentary:*—Just as the other senses, as well as sound and other external objects, are perceptible but do not shine by their own light, so should the mind also be understood. Nor can fire be used as an example here; for fire does not illuminate its own form if it is not already luminous. Furthermore, illumination is observed to result from the connection between the thing that illuminates and the thing being illuminated; however, such a connection is impossible regarding one’s own form <note>The author argues that a single entity cannot be both the subject that sees and the object that is seen at the same moment</note>. And again, the claim that “the mind shines by its own light” literally implies that it cannot be known by any person—for example, Space <note>original: "Âkâsa"</note> existing in its own nature can never exist as another object. Yet, we see that the actions of all living beings follow from the awareness of their own mental activities—for instance, “I am angry,” “I am afraid,” “I have an attachment to this, and an aversion to that.” Such awareness would be impossible to explain if the mind were not itself an object of perception <note>The mind must be "seen" by the higher Self or Spirit to be known</note>.

(1) “Moreover illumination is seen to follow etc.”—All action is based on the relationship between the actor, the act, and the instrument; just as the act of cooking depends on the relationship between the cook, the process of cooking, and the food being cooked. Similarly, because illumination is an action, it must also be based on a similar relationship; but such a relationship is only possible between different objects and not within a single, solitary object.

(2) “But the actions of all living beings etc.”—This addresses the theory that the mind cannot be perceived by the Spirit <note>The "Spirit" or Purusha is the ultimate witness in Yoga philosophy</note>.

## *Aphorism (20):—Two objects cannot be perceived at one and the same time.*

*Commentary:*—At one and the same moment, it is not possible to perceive one’s own form as well as the form of another. The Nihilists <note>Refers here to certain schools of Buddhist philosophy that argue for the momentary and self-knowing nature of consciousness</note> hold that the action, the effect, and the cause—that is, the origin, the act, and the instrument—are all one and the same.

<note>The meaning of the aphorism is that the mind cannot perform the two acts of perceiving an object and knowing itself at the same time; therefore, it is obvious that the mind cannot be the ultimate source of intelligence or consciousness.</note>

<summary>This page argues that the mind is not self-illuminating but is an object perceived by the Spirit, refuting the idea that the mind can be both the observer and the observed simultaneously. It challenges the Nihilist view of identity between actor and action, asserting that consciousness requires a distinction between the knower and the known.</summary>

<keywords>Yoga-Darshana, mind, consciousness, perception, Spirit, Purusha, Akasa, Nihilism, self-illumination, dualism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion regarding the nature of the mind and consciousness, specifically refuting the idea that one mind can be perceived by another in a chain of succession.</meta>

# SECTION IV—SUTRA 21.

It may be held that the mind, suppressed by its own nature <note>meaning its inherent quality of being temporary or destructible</note>, is cognised by another mind following close upon it.

(Reply)

**Sutra (21):—(If) cognition by another mind (be postulated),—there would be an infinity of cognitions and also an *admixture* (or confusion) of memory.**

*Commentary:* If the mind were perceived by another mind, then by what would the cognition of that cognition be perceived? If by another cognition, and this last too by a fourth and so on, then we would be landed in an <term>infinite regress</term> <note>original: "regressus ad infinitum"; a logical fallacy where a sequence of reasoning never reaches a final point</note>,—and there would also arise a confusion of memory; for, as many perceptions of cognitions as there are, there would be just as many corresponding memories. And from this admixture would result the non-ascertainment (that is, uncertainty) of any one memory. Thus we find that the Nihilists <note>referring here to certain Buddhist schools that deny the existence of a permanent self</note> have confounded everything by denying spirit as the one cogniser of all cognitions. These Nihilists, again assuming the role of the cogniser at random, cease to be logical. Some of them assume a mere entity and hold that there is a certain entity which discards the present five *<term>aggregates</term>* <note>original: "skandhas"; the five components—form, sensation, perception, mental formations, and consciousness—that make up a person's being in Buddhist philosophy</note> or ‘bodies’ and takes to other <margin>Transmigration</margin> *<term>aggregates</term>*; and having asserted this much, they fight shy of the same entity: Thus “for the sake of the dispassion of the form of great disgust for the *<term>aggregates</term>*, and for that of the non-production or suppression thereof, I will lead the continent life under a preceptor,”—having said this, they again turn to suppress the very existence of the entity. The Sankhya, Yoga, and the other systems declare that the word “<term>self</term>” <note>original: "swa"</note> denotes the lord of the mind, the spirit, the cogniser.

**Notes:** (1) “Suppressed by its own nature”—that is, on account of its destructibility.

(2) “Confusion or admixture of memory”—because on one idea being called to memory, all its attendant ideas would arise to infinity; because the series of cognitions would be unbroken on account of all...

<summary>This page argues against the theory that the mind is perceived by another mind, claiming this leads to an infinite logical loop and memory confusion. It asserts that a permanent spirit or "self" must exist as the ultimate observer of all thoughts.</summary>
<keywords>infinite regress, memory confusion, Nihilism, aggregates, skandhas, Sankhya, Yoga, self, cogniser, consciousness</keywords>

(3) “Suppress the very existence of the entity”—i.e., by denying the spirit, they strike at the root of the denotation of the word “I.” Here Vijnana Bhikshu <note>A 16th-century philosopher and commentator on Yoga and Samkhya philosophy</note>: “ These Nihilists <note>Referring here to certain schools of Buddhist philosophy that deny a permanent self</note> by so doing become quite illogical, because on the one hand they deny the existence of the cogniser (the spirit), and on the other they accept and strive after spiritual beatitude &c.”

->How ?<-

### Aphorism (22) :—The non-transitional spirit (obtains) the consciousness of its own cognition, when it takes <gloss>changes</gloss> its form.

**Commentary:—**The sentient faculty, unmodifying and unchanging, being reflected in the modifiable (the mind), falls in with its functions; and the function of the cognition (or understanding) is said to be identical with that of the internal organ (mind), on accounts of its ( the reflected sentient faculty ) merely imitating the function of the mind which has attained the form of the superimposition of the sentient faculty. As is declared : 
> “ Neither the nether world, nor mountain caves nor darkness, nor the crevices of the ocean,—(none of these,) is the cave where the eternal Creator <note>original: "Brahma"</note> is concealed; the wise call it the function of the mind, “ pure and simple.”

[**Note :—**The aphorism <note>term: Sutra; a concise rule or statement in Sanskrit literature</note> is capable of a double interpretation, the difference resulting from the construction of “*by assuming that form*” <note>original: "tadâkârâpattyâ"</note>. The clause may be taken to mean : (1) “ When the soul *takes its form* ” (i. e.) when the soul takes the form (of the mental function), and (2) “ *When the mind takes the form of the soul.*” At first sight the order of the aphorism would favour the first interpretation, and Professor Dvivedi accepts this interpretation which is also noticed by Dr. Mitra as being based on the interpretation of some “ later commentators.” But a little consideration will show that the second interpretation is the proper one. Because as the *Commentary* <note>term: Bháshya; a traditional exposition or commentary on a primary text</note> says, when the soul is reflected in the mind, it is the reflecting object that should take the form

<summary>This page discusses the logical inconsistencies of Nihilist philosophy regarding the self and explains how the unchanging spirit perceives the world through its reflection in the mind. It highlights a debate over whether the soul takes the form of the mind or the mind takes the form of the soul during cognition.</summary>

# SECTION IV—APHORISM 23.

So it must be the mind that takes the form of the soul which is “unchanging” <term>apratisankrama: the quality of not moving or transferring from one place to another</term>, and as such cannot take the form of any other object. Vachaspati Misra <note>A 10th-century Indian philosopher who wrote influential commentaries on various schools of thought</note> favours this interpretation. He explains the aphorism thus: “The soul’s consciousness of its own cognition results when the mind takes its form—that is, when the mind becomes the foundation for the reflection of the sentient faculty, and as such takes its form. As for example, even without any action of the moon, the clear rippling water reflecting the disc of the moon manifests the moon also as moving; in the same manner, even without any action of the sentient faculty, the mind reflecting the image of the sentient faculty manifests the sentient faculty as being active through its (the mind’s) action, etc., and attaining to its character of the *experienced*, supplies the sentient faculty with the character of the *experiencer*.”

The force of the argument here depends upon the distinction made between intelligence (the sentient faculty) and the function of the understanding (cognition); for the soul, though intelligence itself, does not exercise any function.

### **Aphorism (23):—The mind tinged by the seer and the seen, is able to perceive all objects.**

**Commentary:—**The mind is tinged by the cognisable object, and being itself an object, is conjoined by the subjective (knowing) soul to its functions; thus does the mind become ‘tinged by the seer and the seen’;—that is, enlivened by the object and the subject, and fallen in with the forms of the sentient and the insentient—and appearing non-objective though really objective, and sentient though really insentient—‘resembling the rock-crystal’ <note>A common metaphor in Indian philosophy where a clear crystal appears colored when placed near a red flower, though the crystal itself never changes</note>—is said to be able to perceive all objects. It is for this reason that certain people, being led away by this similarity of the mind, have declared it as alone sentient. Others again hold that all this is mere mind; any other object, cow or pot etc. together with its cause does not exist. All these theorists deserve to be pitied. Why? Because for their mistake, there is a ground in the shape

Consequently, in meditative consciousness, the object being known is a reflection and is distinct from the mind itself, because the mind serves as its underlying base. If this object were merely the mind itself, then how could the form of consciousness be determined by that same consciousness? For these reasons, it is the Spirit <note>original: "Purusha"; the pure, eternal consciousness that observes the mind</note> by whom the reflected object is determined or identified within consciousness. Thus, those alone are the correct thinkers who distinguish between the knower, the act of knowing, and the object known in their respective characters—basing this distinction on the differences in the mind as it is animated by each of them individually.

Why is this so? (The reply follows:)

**Aphorism (24):—Though colored by countless subconscious impressions, the mind exists for the sake of another, because it acts through association.** <gloss>acting as a collection of combined parts</gloss>

**Commentary:—The mind, though filled with countless impressions <note>original: "vasanas"; the latent tendencies or "smells" left by past actions</note>, nevertheless exists for the purpose of another—that is to say, for the experience and spiritual liberation of others, not for its own sake. This is because it operates through association, much like a house. A house is a collection of materials that serves the inhabitant, not the materials themselves. The mind, acting as it does through the combination of parts, can never exist for itself. Certainly, a mind working toward pleasure does not exist for its own pleasure; nor is wisdom for the sake of the wisdom itself. On the contrary, both of these exist for the purpose of another. This “another” is the soul with a specific purpose—that purpose being worldly experience and ultimate liberation—and not just any random thing other than the mind. If the Nihilist <note>referring to the "Sunyavadin" or "Vijnanavadin" Buddhist schools who argued there is no permanent self</note> were to suggest any random object as being the “other” for whose sake the mind operates,**

<summary>This page argues that the mind is a composite tool that exists to serve the Spirit (Purusha), rather than existing for its own sake. It uses the analogy of a house to show that anything made of combined parts must serve an external inhabitant or observer.</summary>

## Aphorism (25):—For the one who perceives the distinction, there is a cessation of all inquiry into the nature of the self.

**Commentary:—**As in the rainy season, the sprouting of grass allows us to infer the existence of seeds in the ground, so in the same manner, when we find a certain person experiencing **goosebumps** <note>original: "horripilating"</note> and shedding tears of inward joy, we infer the existence of a "seed" in the form of a specific past deed, characterized by spiritual bliss, which is now bearing fruit. For such a person, the natural longing to understand the nature of the self is continually active. However, the absence of such spiritual merit leads to the following condition: having turned away from one's true nature due to some spiritual deficiency, there arises a preference for the nihilistic view and a dislike for the established doctrine. During this state, there is a restless longing to understand the self in these forms: *Who was I? How was I? What is this? How is this? Who shall I be? How shall I be?* This restless inquiry ceases for the one who attains discrimination <term>Viveka: the ability to distinguish between the eternal soul and the temporary mind</term>. Why? Because these questions are merely **peculiar** modifications of the mind; the soul, however, in the absence of Ignorance <term>Avidya: the root cause of suffering and rebirth</term>, is pure and untainted by the qualities of the mind. Therefore, for such an expert, the longing to define the self ceases.

**Notes:—**(1) "The nihilistic view etc."—that is, the position of the Nihilist <note>likely referring to the Charvaka or certain Buddhist schools</note> who claims: "There is no spiritual result of actions because there is no spiritual realm beyond the physical." The established doctrine is the one being explained here in the Yoga system.

<summary>This page explains that once a practitioner achieves "discrimination"—the realization of the difference between the mind and the soul—all speculative and anxious questioning about the identity of the self ceases. The text contrasts the spiritual seeker's joy with the nihilist's skepticism, attributing both to the "seeds" of past actions.</summary>
<keywords>discrimination, soul, mind, self-inquiry, nihilism, karma, spiritual bliss, Yoga Sutras, Sankhya philosophy</keywords>

### Aphorism (26):—Then the mind is turned towards discrimination, and is inclined toward Isolation. <note>original: "Kaivalya." In this context, Isolation refers to the final liberation of the soul from matter.</note>

**Commentary:—**The mind, which was previously inclined toward worldly objects and directed toward ignorance, now undergoes a transformation—that is, it becomes inclined toward isolation and directed toward discriminative knowledge.

**Notes:—“**Now”—this occurs when the mind has become conscious of the distinction between the self and the material world.

---

### Aphorism (27):—In the intervals of that state, other thoughts arise, which are caused by past mental impressions.

**Commentary:—**In the gaps when the mind is directed toward the discrimination of its own nature—flowing in the stream of distinguishing between matter and spirit—other thoughts arise, such as, “I exist,” “This is mine,” “I know,” or “I do not know.” From where do these come? They arise from the fading seeds of previous mental impressions.

**Notes: (1)—**“Directed toward the discrimination of itself”—meaning the distinction of the mind from the faculty of consciousness; <term>Vachaspati Misra</term> <note>A 10th-century philosopher and commentator</note> explains “*Cognition*” <note>original: "Pratyaya"</note> as that which perceives—namely, the mind.

**(2)—**“*Flowing* in the current etc.”—This explains the previous description of being “directed toward the discrimination of itself.”

**(3)—**“Previous mental impressions”—meaning the fading traces left by the former state of worldly consciousness.

During the progress toward isolation, when the mind rests from meditation during these intervals, it is beset by certain worldly ideas. These sprout from the lingering impressions of the former waking state. Such ideas are obstacles and must be avoided.

---

**Commentary:—**Just as the afflictions cease to be capable of sprouting once their germinating power is destroyed, in the same manner, the previous mental impression, having its sprout-

### *Aphorism (29):*—To the one who is disinterested even in *illumination* or *discriminative knowledge* (accrues) **exclusive discriminative wisdom** leading to the meditation called the "Cloud of Virtue." <note>original: "Dharma-megha-samadhi"</note>

**Commentary:**—When such a Priest <note>original: "Brāhmaṇa"; here referring to an enlightened seeker.</note> loses all interest in illumination even—that is, one who desires nothing even from that, and becomes indifferent to it—to him accrues exclusive discriminative wisdom; and thus the seed of the cycle of rebirth <note>original: "metempsychosis"</note> being destroyed, no more cognitions are produced; and thus proceeds the meditation called the "Cloud of Virtue."

**Notes:**—(1) "*Non-aspiring etc.*"—One who has no desire for any of the fruits proceeding from "illumination" by which is meant "the light of knowledge" resulting from discriminative recognition of the twenty-six elements of the Enumeration system <note>original: "Sankhya system," one of the six classical schools of Indian philosophy.</note>; this illumination is described in *Aphorism* 49, section III.

(2)—"Cloud of Virtue"—that is, the condition in which all Virtues are showered down.

(3)—The meaning of the Aphorism is: "When after this illumination, the Practitioner <note>original: "Yogin"</note> works entirely without any attachment or desire, he reaches the state of supreme non-attachment wherein the light of the soul breaks out in full." (Dvivedi) <note>Manilal Dvivedi was a prominent 19th-century commentator on Yoga philosophy.</note> The sense is that though the Practitioner may not wish for any reward, still the reward comes.

### **Sutra (30):—Thence the cessation of distractions and actions.**

**Commentary:—**Upon the attainment of that state, the distractions—namely Ignorance and the other obstacles—are destroyed from their very roots; good and bad actions are also brought to a halt. Upon the cessation of these distractions and actions, the wise practitioner becomes liberated while still inhabiting the physical body <note>Known as Jivanmukti, or "living liberation."</note>. Why is this? Because misconception is the sole cause of being bound to the cycle of worldly existence. For certainly, no one has ever witnessed the rebirth of an individual whose misconceptions have been entirely removed.

**Notes:** (1)—“*Thereof*”—that is, the attainment of the meditation called the “**Cloud of Virtue.**” <note>original: "Dharma-megha-samadhi." This is the highest state of spiritual absorption where the practitioner is showered with the essence of virtue and knowledge.</note>

(2)—“*From their roots*”—The roots being the various types of mental impressions <note>original: "Samskaras," the subconscious inclinations formed by past experiences.</note>.

(3)—“*Is liberated*”—Because the cause of birth, life-span, and worldly experience is the residue of past deeds <term>Karmic residua: the "seeds" of past actions that wait for the right conditions to sprout into new life experiences</term> which are kept alive by actions, distractions, and mental impressions. Under these specific circumstances, when knowledge becomes clear of all impurities and obscurations, the result is its infiniteness. And from the...

# Sutra (32):—Thereupon follows the termination of the succession of the modifications of the Attributes, which have accomplished their end.

**Commentary:**—On the appearance of the Cloud of Virtue, end the successive modifications of the Attributes <term>Gunas: the three fundamental qualities of nature—purity, activity, and inertia—that make up the physical and mental world</term> whose ends have been fulfilled. Because (Attributes), that have fulfilled experience and liberation, and whose succession has ended, dare not tarry a moment longer.

**Note:**—This aphorism <note>original: "Sutra"</note> meets the objection that, though actions and distractions would end, still the attributes, from their very mobile nature, would continue to undergo modifications and thus produce birth, experience etc. The sense of the reply is that the nature of the Attributes is such that when once their purpose (the experience and liberation of the soul) has been fulfilled, they cease to operate, with regard to that particular soul.—compare the concluding Verses of the Sankhya Philosophy <note>original: "Sankhya Karikas," the foundational text of the Sankhya school which Yoga adopts as its theoretical basis</note>.

<summary>This page explains that once a practitioner reaches the highest state of wisdom, the fundamental qualities of nature (Attributes) cease their activity because their purpose—providing experience and liberation—is complete. It includes a debate between commentators on a paradoxical verse regarding the impossibility of effects continuing without a cause.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion on the nature of time and the culmination of the Yoga system, specifically focusing on the concept of 'succession' (krama) and the finality of the material world.</meta>

What is this succession? (Reply:)

*Aphorism* (33):—**Succession is the counterpart of the moment, perceptible at the end of a transformation.** <note>original: "Sutra". An aphorism or concise rule in Sanskrit literature.</note>

*Commentary*:—Succession, which consists of the intervals between moments, is perceived only when a transformation is completed. For example, the "oldness" of a piece of cloth—observed at the end of its use or its destruction—could not occur without a continuous series of moments during its existence. This succession is seen to exist even in relation to eternal objects. 

Eternity is of two kinds: unchanging eternity and transformative eternity. <note>original: "kutastha-nityata" (unchanging) and "parinami-nityata" (transforming).</note> Of these, unchanging eternity belongs to the Soul <note>The Purusha or pure consciousness.</note> and transformative eternity belongs to the primary Attributes of nature. <note>The Gunas: the three fundamental forces of the material world.</note> An object is considered eternal if its various transformations do not destroy its essential nature. Since the essential forms of both the Soul and the Attributes are never destroyed, both are eternal. 

With regard to the products derived from these Attributes—such as the Intellect and the rest—the succession of their changes has a perceptible end. <note>original: "Buddhi". The higher cognitive faculty or 'will'.</note> However, in the case of eternal objects, the end of succession is not perceptible. In the case of the unchanging eternal—the liberated souls existing in their own pure form—the continuity of their existence is perceived as a succession. Here, even though the end of this succession cannot be seen, it is assumed to exist through the very "action" of being; this assumption is based upon the authority of sacred texts.

Question:—“For this Universe, which exists and progresses through the Attributes of nature, is there an end to this succession of change or not?” 

Reply:—This is indeed a question that cannot be answered with a simple 'yes' or 'no'! Why? A question that allows for an absolute reply is something like: “Will all that is born eventually die?” (The reply is): “Yes.” On the other hand, there is another type of question that requires a qualified answer, such as: “Will every object that dies be reborn?” The answer to this must be specific: the wise one whose desires have been extinguished and who has attained spiritual dis-

<summary>This page defines 'succession' as the sequence of moments that constitute time and change, distinguishing between the unchanging eternity of the Soul and the transformative eternity of Nature. It addresses the philosophical problem of whether the cycle of the universe has a definitive end.</summary>
<keywords>succession, moments, eternity, Soul, Attributes, Gunas, Intellect, Buddhi, liberation, transformation, Yoga Sutras</keywords>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page concludes the fourth chapter (Pada) of the Yoga Sutras, focusing on the final definition of Kaivalya (Liberation or Isolation) and the resolution of the three Gunas (Attributes).</meta>

<page-num>161</page-num>
->PADA IV—SUTRA 34.<-

crimination, will not be born; others besides such a one, will be surely born. Similarly on being asked ‘Is the human species the highest or not?’ the reply must be given in a qualified form: The human species is higher than the other animals, whereas it is not so in comparison with the Gods and the <term>Rishis</term> <note>Ancient inspired poets or seers of the Vedic hymns</note>. The question “Has the universe an end or not?” is unanswerable directly; because (the reply must be;) ‘for the wise one (above mentioned), there is an end to the continuance of the universe,—not for any other.’ For this reason, if the reply were based on any *one of the two*, i.e., if the reply were given directly—yes or no—without qualification, there would be a mistake. Hence the aforesaid question must be broken up (before being answered.)

**Notes:** (1)—“The word “succession” is explained in this aphorism to mean the following of one moment after another. The object is to say that the lapse of time is reckoned by the succession of one moment after another, though there is a break between them one totality is divided into many parts only for facility of reckoning.”—*Mitra* <note>Referring to Rajendralal Mitra, a 19th-century scholar and translator of the Yoga Sutras</note>.

---

It has been declared that Isolation follows at the end of the succession of the capacity of the Attributes. This Isolation is now described:

**Sutra (34):—The repression of the Attributes, devoid of the soul’s purpose, is the Isolation; or it is the sentient faculty abiding in itself.**

**Commentary:**—The repression (or inverse resolution) of the Attributes <note>The three fundamental qualities of nature: Sattva (balance), Rajas (activity), and Tamas (inertia)</note> of the nature of cause and effect, that have fulfilled experience and isolation, and are (hence) unable to serve any further purpose of the soul, is Isolation. The sentient faculty of the soul abiding in itself, i.e., disconnected from the attributes of <term>Buddhi</term> <note>The higher intellectual faculty or "intellect-mirror" that reflects consciousness</note>, is pure; and (such a sentient faculty) continuing for ever in that condition, constitutes Isolation.

# THE END

<summary>This concluding page defines Isolation (Kaivalya) as the state where the fundamental attributes of nature cease their activity for the liberated soul, allowing pure consciousness to abide in its own nature. It emphasizes that metaphysical questions often require qualified answers rather than simple "yes" or "no" responses.</summary>
<keywords>Isolation, Kaivalya, Attributes, Gunas, Soul's Purpose, Sentient Faculty, Buddhi, Intellect, Rishi, Succession of Time</keywords>

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the classification of human knowledge, transitioning from the individual nature of man to his role within a political community.</meta>

# THE PHILOSOPHICAL LIFE.

<margin>The double life of man.</margin>
<margin>The double science of man.</margin>
<margin>Science concerning the body and soul.</margin>
Just as we establish that the life of man is twofold—one being natural, the other political—so too is the science of man of a twofold nature. For one part is considered in itself, while the other considers man as a member of a commonwealth <note>original: "reipub." or "respublica," referring to the organized political community or state</note>, about which we shall speak in the next treatise. Regarding the former, it is that which again looks at the two parts of man: the body and the soul. The science concerning the body is Medicine; that concerning the soul is Moral Philosophy.

<margin>Physiognomy.</margin>
<margin>The interpretation of dreams.</margin>
<margin>Socrates.</margin>
Truly, since man consists of body and soul, each of which acts upon the other, it has followed that a certain intermediate science arises for both parts, which serves both and regards both. We shall call this Physiognomy <term>Physiognomy: the practice of assessing a person's character or personality from their outer appearance, especially the face</term> and the Interpretation of Dreams, though others may name them differently. For Physiognomy, from the gestures and features of the body, makes a conjecture about the movement and disposition of the mind; the Interpretation of Dreams, from the movement and disposition of the mind, makes a conjecture about the affections of the body. These two arts, although they are conjectural, are nevertheless not to be entirely rejected. Regarding Physiognomy, Aristotle wrote one entire book. Nor do I see why it should be condemned, since it is wholly occupied with making a judgment about the character of the mind from the features and condition of the body. And this occurs so naturally that certain men have professed to be able to predict from these signs what a person might become. This happened in the case of Socrates, when a certain physiognomist said that he would be lustful; Socrates himself confessed this, saying that he was such by nature, but had been corrected through philosophy. As for dreams, writers say much about these as well, especially regarding their causes and effects, upon which we shall not dwell for long.

<margin>The political science of man.</margin>
Thus far concerning the science of man in himself; now we turn to the science of man as he is a member of the Commonwealth, or concerning the method of living civilly and politically.

<summary>This page categorizes the study of man into individual (medicine and ethics) and political spheres, while defending "intermediate" sciences like physiognomy and dream analysis that bridge the gap between body and soul.</summary>
<keywords>philosophy of man, medicine, moral philosophy, physiognomy, dream interpretation, Aristotle, Socrates, political science, body and soul</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the geometric proofs of Euclid's Elements, specifically focusing on the properties of intersecting lines within a circle. The catchword "A" from the previous page likely refers to the beginning of the point labels in this proposition.</meta>

<page-num>117</page-num>
<sig>P 3</sig>

# PROPOSITION 36.

<margin>Refer to the figure for Proposition 35.</margin>
If two straight lines intersect each other within a circle: the <term>rectangle</term><note>In this context, a "rectangle" refers to the area formed by the product of two line segments.</note> contained under the segments of the one is equal to the rectangle contained under the segments of the other.

<image-desc>A geometrical diagram featuring a circle labeled with points A, B, C, and D on its circumference. Two chords, AB and CD, intersect at point E inside the circle. Point F marks the center of the circle. Perpendicular lines are drawn from the center F to point G on chord AB and to point H on chord CD. Additional line segments connect point F to points A, C, and E.</image-desc>

In the circle *ABCD*, let two straight lines *AB* and *CD* intersect each other at point *E*. I say that the rectangle *AE*, *EB* is equal to the rectangle *CE*, *ED*.

For if the point *E* is the center of the circle, the proposition is manifest <note>If the lines intersect at the center, all segments are radii and therefore equal.</note>. If not, let the center be *F*, from which let the <term>perpendiculars</term><note>Lines meeting at a right angle (90 degrees).</note> *FG* and *FH* be drawn to *AB* and *CD*, and let *FA*, *FC*, and *FE* be joined.

Therefore, since the straight line *FG* from the center is perpendicular to the straight line *AB*, it will bisect it at *G*; and (by the 5th proposition of the second book) the rectangle *AE*, *EB* together with the <term>square</term><note>The symbol □ denotes a square area formed by the line segment.</note> of *EG* will be equal to the square of *AG*. Let the common square of *GF* be added. Then the rectangle *AE*, *EB* with the squares of *EG* and *GF* (that is, by the 47th proposition of the first book <note>The Pythagorean Theorem.</note>, with the square of *EF*) will be equal to the squares of *AG* and *GF* (that is, by the same 47th of the first book, the square of *AF*).

In the same manner, it will be proved that the rectangle *CE*, *ED* together with the square of *EF* is equal to the square of *CF*. But the square of *AF* is equal to the square of *CF*, since the lines *AF* and *CF* are from the center <note>They are both radii of the same circle.</note>. Therefore, the rectangle *AE*, *EB* with the square of *EF* is equal to the rectangle *CE*, *ED* with the square of *EF*. When the common square of *EF* is taken away, the rectangle *AE*, *EB* will remain equal to the rectangle *CE*, *ED*. Which was to be demonstrated.

<summary>This page presents a geometric proof demonstrating that when two chords intersect inside a circle, the product of the segments of one chord equals the product of the segments of the other. This is a fundamental theorem of Euclidean geometry involving the Pythagorean theorem and properties of circles.</summary>
<keywords>Euclid, Geometry, Circle, Proposition, Rectangle, Square, Segment, Center, Perpendicular, Demonstration, Chords</keywords>
